After an exhausting experience in which she had battled with half-a-dozen riotous children, an ailing mother and a father who tried to be confidential about the trials and disappointments of marriage, she had taken a post with an old lady in the hope of comparative leisure and, like an actress who makes a success in a particular kind of part and finds it difficult to get another, she had seemed doomed to old, invalid and lonely ladies for the rest of her life. She had naturally been suspected of inability to deal with the young after an existence of picking up dropped stitches, fetching clean pocket handkerchiefs and reading aloud, though Hannah could have been eloquent about the wearing nature of such work. Often, she had looked at a charwoman with envy, desiring healthy labour with brush and bucket, and for her folly in not hiring a bedroom and letting herself out by the day, she blamed what must have been an odd lingering desire for the gentility she affected to despise. She would have made an admirable charwoman: that vulgar strain in her which Lilla justly deplored, so unsuitable in a companion or a housekeeper, would have been a positive recommendation in a charwoman, and she pictured herself, going from house to house, energetic, good-humoured, free of speech, the perfect charwoman of fiction, with a home which was all her own and none of these tangled personalities to deal with. Well, she would tell herself with a sniff, she might come to it yet, but women in that walk of life were not liable to be made the heirs of rich old gentlemen, and towards that pleasant prospect Hannah still pretended to be gazing. She had seen no one in the chapel who answered to the description, and though she had waved a duster out of every window, she had not met No. 16’s roguish leer. A sparkling October had given place to a damp November and the weather, she supposed, was bad for parrots and for gardening. She sometimes saw No. 16 trundling towards the back gate, she heard him calling in the cats at night, but she had no time to plan encounters, for she worked as hard as any charwoman and had to adapt herself to new and difficult conditions.

In her other situations where there was a man of the house, he had left it at a reasonable hour in the morning and could be trusted not to reappear until the evening: there was no such regularity in the movements of Mr. Corder. The absence or presence of his hat and coat was the only surety that he was out or in, and it became a habit with Hannah to glance at the pegs as she went through the hall and to feel her spirits rising or falling with what she saw there, another unwilling confession that his personality was not negligible. Hannah’s somewhat toneless singing, which contrasted so strangely with the quality of her speaking voice, was generally silenced when he was in the house; he damped down the tempers of his daughters and Hannah wondered if he knew they had them; he stifled conversation, for he was ready with information on all subjects, and opinions which differed from his own either amused or angered him, yet hardly a day passed without a caller, someone needing help or advice, an ardent chapel worker with some difficulty to be solved, a deacon on a mission of importance, and the voice which came from the study was not always Robert Corder’s, and though he might lead the laughter, there was response to it, and people went away looking happier than they had come. Nevertheless, Hannah would make a grimace at the study door as she passed. She was sure Mrs. Corder, from her place on the great man’s desk, was listening gravely to what he said and making her acute, silent comments, balancing the counsels he gave against what she knew of him and yet, more tolerant than Hannah, refusing to judge him harshly.

She had made of Mrs. Corder a person like herself, with more wisdom, more kindness and more patience, qualities she must have needed in excess, Hannah thought grimly, for she who prided herself on her willingness to accept the good and bad in men and women as easily as she accepted their physical and mental parts, was deliberately antagonistic to Robert Corder. The swing of his coat tails vexed her as probably the swing of her skirts vexed him; she would not believe in the boasted broad-mindedness of a man who sneered at opposing views or waved them aside, and whose small, tight mouth she could discern under the moustache which masked it. Like most childless women, she exaggerated the joys and privileges of possessing offspring and Robert Corder seemed unaware of them. He was not an unkind father; he was amiable enough and ready to expand under the affection he had made it impossible for them to show him, but he seemed to Hannah to treat his daughters as an audience for his sentiments and the record of his doings and to forget that these girls had characters, unless they happened to annoy him. While Hannah chafed under his bland assumptions, she enjoyed watching for corroborative evidence of the estimate she had made of him and he rarely disappointed her, for, when things went well with him, he had to talk, and it was then that Wilfrid’s eyes sought hers and with the tiniest droop of an eyelid, lift of an eyebrow or face of unnatural solemnity, sent his message to her across the table.

===Chinese===

汉娜（Hannah）已经很久没跟一家人同住了。先前有过一段耗人心力的经历：她要对付五六个闹翻天的孩子、一位缠绵病榻的母亲，还有一位总想拉她推心置腹、倾诉婚姻之烦恼与幻灭的父亲；那之后她谋了个伺候老太太的差事，指望图个比较的清闲，谁知就像女演员把某一路角色演出了名，别的角色便再难到手，她的后半生似乎注定要交付给老太太、病太太、孤太太们了。自然而然，人家便疑心她带不了年轻人——毕竟她这些年的生涯，无非捡拾脱针的线脚、递送干净手帕、朗声念书，虽然这类活计多么磨人，汉娜满可以讲得头头是道。她常常眼羡打杂女工，巴望拎着水桶、拿把刷子干点爽利的力气活；至于自己不曾租下一间卧室、按日零雇出卖劳力，这份傻气，她归咎于心里必定还残着一种古怪的、断不了念头的体面欲——那体面恰是她口头上摆出鄙夷样子的。她原可以成为一个出色的打杂女工：她身上那股粗俗气——莉拉（Lilla）曾公道地为之扼腕，说它在女伴或女管家身上实在要不得——搁到打杂女工身上，反倒成了一桩响当当的长处；她想象自己挨家挨户地走，精神抖擞、和和气气、嘴里没遮拦，正是小说里写的那种十全十美的打杂女工，有一个全然属于自己的家，再不必同这些乱麻似的性格周旋。唉，她鼻子里哼一声，对自己说，没准儿早晚有这一天；可干那一行的女人，不大容易当上阔绰老绅士的遗产继承人，而对那幅美妙的指望，汉娜至今还假装睁着眼睛凝望呢。礼拜堂里她没见过一个对得上那副写照的人；尽管她从每扇窗口都挥过掸子，却始终没遇上十六号（No. 16）那道促狭的斜睨。十月原是晴光泼辣，如今换作了潮乎乎的十一月；这天气，她寻思，对鹦鹉不利，对园艺也不利。她有时看见十六号慢腾腾地往后门挪，夜里听见他唤猫进屋，可她抽不出工夫筹划相遇的场面，因为她干得不比哪个打杂女工轻省，还得让自己去适应种种新的、棘手的环境。

在她从前那些有男主人的差事里，男主人总在上午一个像样的钟点出门，天黑之前尽可以放心他不会露面；科德先生（Mr. Corder）的行踪却没有这份规律。他的帽子与外套在与不在，是他在家在外的唯一凭据；汉娜由此养成了习惯：穿过门厅时总要瞟一眼那排挂衣钩，心情随看见的东西起起落落——这又是一桩身不由己的招供：他这个人不容小觑。汉娜唱歌略微不着调，同她说话嗓音的质量反差得出奇；他在家时，歌声通常便止住了；他压得住两个女儿的火气，汉娜纳闷他是否知道她们有火气；他扼杀谈话，因为无论什么题目他都备好了现成的学问，与他相左的意见不是逗乐他便惹恼他；然而几乎没有一天没有客人上门：求帮助或讨主意的，热心礼拜堂事务、带着难处来求解的会众，负着要务而来的执事；书房里传出的声音并不总是罗伯特·科德（Robert Corder）一个人的，笑声纵然由他带头，却总有人应和，人们走时脸上的神色比来时亮堂。尽管如此，汉娜路过书房门口，总要朝那扇门做个鬼脸。她确信，科德太太（Mrs. Corder）在那位大人物的写字台上守着自己的位置，正一脸郑重地听他说话，做着犀利的无声点评，把他给出的忠告同她对他的了解放在天平上称量，却又比汉娜宽容，不肯把他往苛刻里断定。

她把科德太太想成了一个同自己相像的人，只是更有智慧、更和善、更有耐心——汉娜冷冷地想道，这些品质她在世时想必得多备着几份才够用——因为她这个向来以胸襟自许、自认接纳男女身上的善恶就像接纳他们的皮肉与心智一样容易的人，偏偏存心同罗伯特·科德作对。他外套下摆的摆动惹她心烦，正如她裙裾的摆动多半也惹他心烦；一个讥诮相反意见、或挥手把它扫到一边的人，一个胡子底下藏着她分明看得出的一张绷紧的小嘴的人——这般人物吹嘘的胸襟开阔，她才不信呢。同大多数没有儿女的女人一样，她把生儿育女的欢乐与特权夸大了几分，而罗伯特·科德对自己拥有的这一切似乎浑然不觉。他并不是个狠心的父亲；他够和蔼了，本也乐意在儿女的亲爱里舒展开来——尽管正是他叫儿女没法向他表露亲爱——可在汉娜看来，他把两个女儿当作他一腔感怀的听众、他一桩桩事迹的记事本，忘掉了这两个姑娘各有各的性子，除非她们碰巧惹恼了他。汉娜一边在他那些不动声色的想当然底下暗暗气闷，一边又乐于留心搜罗佐证，印证自己对他下的判语，而他很少让她失望：他诸事顺遂时就憋不住要高谈阔论，每当那时，威尔弗里德（Wilfrid）的眼睛便会找她的眼睛，眼皮极轻地一垂、眉毛微微一挑，或是摆出一副不合常情的正经脸，隔着餐桌把他的口信递过去。

===Original===

Hannah took a penitential pleasure in controlling herself. If she asserted her personality before she had established herself firmly, even Lilla’s patronage would not save her. She had to persuade Robert Corder that she was useful before she let him suspect her of a mind quicker than his own, and she behaved discreetly, for she had her compact with Mrs. Corder to keep, she had her own powers to prove and, though she would have laughed at the idea, she had the zeal of a reformer under her thin crust of cynicism. She wanted to fatten Ruth and see an occasional look of happiness on her face, to ease Ethel’s restlessness and get some sort of beauty into the house. She could not change the ugly furniture﻿—and there Mrs. Corder had badly failed﻿—but friendliness and humour and gaiety cost no money; they were, in fact, in the penniless Hannah’s pocket, waiting for these difficult people to take them, and Hannah bided their time and her own.

She found that Ethel’s labours at the Mission were not so arduous as the state of the house implied. She had bursts of feverish activity, she was constant in her attendance at the Girls’ Club and she sometimes helped her father with his correspondence, and then, for a whole day, it would seem she had nothing to do, and she would shadow Hannah about the house, as though she dreaded loneliness, watch her as she worked, without offering to help, and spend the evening turning the pages of a book, making fitful conversation, repairing, or making changes in her rather tawdry clothes. She had a misguided passion for colour and for ornaments, and the jingling of her beads was the constant accompaniment to her restless movements. Ruth, frowning over her lessons, would beg her to be quiet and one night she asked why the drawing-room fire should not be lit, so that Ethel and Miss Mole could sit there and leave her in peace.

===Chinese===

汉娜在克制自己当中得到一种忏悔式的快乐。要是脚跟还没站稳就伸张自己的个性，连莉拉的庇护也救不了她。她得先让罗伯特·科德相信她有用处，然后才容他疑心她的脑子比他的转得快；她行事谨慎，因为她有一份同科德太太订下的默契要守，有一身自己的本事要证明；而且——尽管这念头准会逗她发笑——她那层薄薄的愤世硬壳底下，还捂着一副改革家的热肠。她想喂胖露丝（Ruth），想在她脸上偶尔看见一点快活的神色；想抚平埃塞尔（Ethel）的躁郁，给这个家添上一点说得过去的美。丑家具她改不了——在那上头，科德太太是败得很惨的——可是友善、幽默与欢快不花一个钱；它们其实就揣在身无分文的汉娜的口袋里，等着这些难缠的人伸手来拿，汉娜便守候着，守它们的时机，也守她自己的时机。

她发现，埃塞尔在布道会（the Mission）的劳作，并不像这所房子的乱糟糟所暗示的那么繁重。她有阵阵发热般忙乱的劲头，少女俱乐部（the Girls' Club）倒是风雨无阻回回必到，偶尔也帮父亲料理信件；然后，又会有一整天显得无事可做，她便像影子似的满屋子跟着汉娜转，仿佛害怕孤独，看她干活，又不肯搭把手；到了晚上，就翻翻书页，有一搭没一搭地搭几句，缝缝补补，或是在她那几件未免俗艳的衣裳上改来改去。她对颜色和饰物有一种走错了门的痴情，珠串的叮当声给她坐立不定的动作做着永不缺席的伴奏。露丝皱着眉头做功课，会求她安静些；一天晚上她问，为什么不能把客厅的炉火生起来，好让埃塞尔和莫尔小姐（Miss Mole）坐到那边去，还她一个清静。

===Original===

“We can’t afford a fire in every room in the house,” Ethel explained.

“Doris has one to herself, Father has another, why should the rest of us have to share one? And anyhow, it’s Miss Mole who manages the money now, so you needn’t interfere!”

Miss Mole said nothing. This remark was probably intended as a jeer at Ethel, but, at least, it recognised her own existence, and a small smile must have come on her lips, for Wilfrid, entering at that moment, gave a clap of his hands and cried, “I’ve been wondering who you were ever since the happy hour when I first saw you and I’ve found out at last! Good evening, Mona Lisa. And don’t pretend you don’t know it’s you I’m talking to!”

Hannah looked up, then down. “It’s the long nose,” she said.

===Chinese===

“咱们家可养不起每间屋子都生火，”埃塞尔解释道。

“多丽丝（Doris）一个人占一个，父亲又一个，凭什么剩下的人就得合用一个？再说，如今管账的是莫尔小姐，你犯不着插手！”

莫尔小姐没有作声。这话八成是存心挖苦埃塞尔的，可它至少承认了她的存在，她唇边想必已经挂上了一丝笑影，因为威尔弗里德恰在此时走进来，一拍手嚷道：“打初次见您那个愉快的时辰起，我就一直在纳闷您是谁，如今到底让我猜着了！晚上好，蒙娜丽莎（Mona Lisa）。别装不知道我是在跟您说话！”

汉娜抬起眼，又垂下去。“是这长鼻子，”她说。

===Original===

“Not a bit of it! It’s the secret smile. It’s all the wisdom of the world.”

Ethel was at a loss and seemed distressed. Ruth glanced up curiously for an instant and then bowed her head over her books and shielded her face with her hands.

“I can’t remember, for the moment,” Ethel said, “who Mona Lisa is.”

“A plain woman,” Hannah said.

“Then it’s very rude of Wilfrid,” there was relief in Ethel’s voice, “to say you’re like her.”

===Chinese===

“才不是呢！是那抹神秘的笑。那是把全世界的智慧都收在里面了。”

埃塞尔摸不着头脑，一副难受的样子。露丝好奇地抬眼瞥了一瞬，随即把头埋回书本，用双手挡住自己的脸。

“我一时想不起来，”埃塞尔说，“蒙娜丽莎是谁。”

“一个相貌平常的女人，”汉娜说。

“那威尔弗里德可太无礼了，”埃塞尔的声音里透出宽慰，“居然说你像她。”

===Original===

“On the contrary,” he said, “she may be plain, but she’s the most fascinating woman in the world.”

“Oh!” said Ethel blankly, and, after a moment’s fidgeting, she went out of the room.

Wilfrid nodded towards the door. “She’s gone to look her up in the dictionary!”

“No, she hasn’t,” Ruth spoke drily. “She’s gone up to her room and she’ll be opening and shutting drawers and banging things about for hours and I shan’t be able to go to sleep.” Her voice rose painfully. “Why haven’t you more sense?” she cried. “If you want to say things like that, why can’t you say them when she isn’t here?”

===Chinese===

“恰恰相反，”他说，“她也许是相貌平常，可她是全世界最迷人的女人。”

“哦！”埃塞尔怔怔地说，局促了一阵，便走出了房间。

威尔弗里德朝门那边点点头。“她查字典去了！”

“才不会呢，”露丝冷冷地说。“她是上楼回自己屋去了；往后几个钟头，她会开开关关抽屉、乒乒乓乓摔打东西，我就别想睡着了。”她的声音痛苦地扬了起来。“你怎么没点分寸？”她嚷道。“想说这种话，为什么不能挑她不在的时候说？”

===Original===

For the first time in weeks, Hannah forgot to be on her guard. A feeling of great mental weariness, of physical sickness, overcame her. The work slipped from her hands and she leaned back in her chair, shutting her eyes for a minute. It seemed to her horrible that Ruth should have so clear an insight into Ethel’s nature, and such bitter experience of it, that Ethel’s nature should be what it was. At Ruth’s age, Hannah had just gone to school in Upper Radstowe, with an intimate, frank knowledge of sexual processes, acquired by living on a farm, and was discovering that matters which her father had not scrupled to discuss in her presence were the subjects of sly whisperings in the school. The shock she suffered was different from the one to which Lilla piously laid claim, for Lilla was disgusted by physical details and Hannah was disgusted that anyone should consider them unclean, and she had been spared Ruth’s irritating contact with a mind subject, no doubt unconsciously, to the dictates of the body.

The crudity of this thought was distasteful to Miss Mole; the truth of it was worse. It was all very well to talk about civilization’s benefits to women and the preservation of their chastity, but what was happening to the minds of countless virgins who would never be anything else if they wished to be thought respectable? And while Ruth, like Ethel, was probably in ignorance of causes, she, too, was the unfortunate victim of effects.

Hannah sighed, and raised her eyes to find Ruth looking at her with a startled interest, and to wonder, under that look, whether her policy of self-effacement was the right one.

===Chinese===

几个星期以来头一遭，汉娜忘了设防。一阵深深的脑筋疲惫、一阵肉体上的恶心袭来，把她压倒了。手里的活计滑落下去，她仰靠进椅背，闭了一小会儿眼睛。露丝对埃塞尔的天性竟有这般透彻的洞见、这般切肤的苦经验，而埃塞尔的天性竟是这样一副样子——在她看来，这一切可怕极了。露丝这个年纪的时候，汉娜刚到上拉德斯托（Upper Radstowe）去上学，带着农场生活教给她的、对两性之事的实情亲密而坦率的了解，正发现父亲当着她的面从不忌讳谈论的种种事情，到了学校里竟成了鬼鬼祟祟的耳语题目。她当年受的那场震动，同莉拉一本正经自陈受过的那一场并不相同：莉拉厌恶的是肉体的细枝末节，汉娜厌恶的却是竟有人把肉体的细枝末节看作秽物；至于露丝那份叫人恼火的煎熬——同一颗无疑并非有意、却听凭肉体支配的心灵朝夕相触——她总算幸免了。

这念头太粗鄙，莫尔小姐自己都嫌恶；可它的真确更糟。侈谈文明施与女人的种种益处、谈它如何保全她们的贞洁，自然动听；可无数处女——她们若想落个正经女人的名声，便永远只能是处女——的心智正在经受什么？露丝同埃塞尔一样，对“因”恐怕一无所知，却也是“果”的不幸牺牲品。

汉娜叹了口气，抬起眼睛，正撞见露丝带着一种受了惊动的好奇望着她；在这道目光底下，她不由得犯疑：自己这套敛迹藏锋的方针究竟对不对。

===Original===

There was a fire in the drawing-room the next evening and there was a feeling of holiday in the house. Robert Corder was speaking at some meeting outside Radstowe and would not be back that night, and Hannah prepared a supper of surprises, such as they could not often have, for he was a hearty eater and needed solid fare. The family had the grace to recognise her efforts: Ethel pathetically did her best to pretend she had no grudge against Wilfrid or Miss Mole, Ruth openly enjoyed the food, Wilfrid forebore to flatter or to tease, and Hannah told herself that this was a very good imitation of a temporarily happy family.

When the meal was over Ruth was left in the dining-room to do her work, as she had desired, in peace, but Hannah lingered to repair the fire and gather up the mending which was her nightly occupation.

“Now you’ll be all right, won’t you?” she said cheerfully.

Ruth’s small, worried, face became more strained. “I didn’t say I wanted to be alone,” she said, and Hannah realized that her apparent sullenness was embarrassment. “I only wanted to be quiet. You sit so still. You’re not like Ethel. And she’ll be happier, alone, in there with Wilfrid.”

===Chinese===

第二天晚上，客厅里生起了火，满屋子有过节的气象。罗伯特·科德在拉德斯托（Radstowe）城外的什么会上演讲，当夜不回来；汉娜备下一顿满是惊喜的夜宵——这种吃食他们平日难得有——因为他在家时胃口健旺，须得扎实的饭食。这一家人倒有几分良心，认了她的情：埃塞尔可怜巴巴地尽力装出对威尔弗里德和莫尔小姐毫无芥蒂的样子；露丝明明白白吃得欢畅；威尔弗里德忍住了性子，既不奉承，也不打趣；汉娜对自己说，这是一个暂时幸福的家庭的一幅绝佳摹本。

饭吃完，露丝如愿留在饭厅里，清清静静地做功课；汉娜却磨蹭着不走，拨拢炉火，收拾起每晚必做的针线。

“这下你自己没问题了吧，啊？”她兴致勃勃地说。

露丝那张小小的、发愁的脸绷得更紧了。“我没说要一个人待着，”她说，汉娜这才明白，她那副闷闷不乐的样子其实是难为情。“我只想清静。你坐得那么安稳，你又不像埃塞尔。再说她跟威尔弗里德单独在那边，还会快活些。”

===Original===

“And I’d rather stay here,” said Hannah, and neither of them spoke again until Ruth pushed her books aside and said she was going to bed.

“Good night,” Hannah said, with a cool nod and smile.

Ruth stooped to the fire and warmed her hands and then, with a little catch of her breath, she went away.

“I shall get her yet!” Hannah said to herself.

===Chinese===

“我倒是更乐意待在这儿，”汉娜说。此后两人都没再开口，直到露丝把书推到一边，说她要睡觉去了。

“晚安，”汉娜说，淡淡地点头一笑。

露丝俯身向火，烘了烘手，随后轻轻倒抽了一口气，走了。

“我迟早要收服她的！”汉娜对自己说。

===Original===

At some time during that night, she woke with a start. She had been dreaming a variation of a dream she often had. The scene was always the same. Somewhere in the neighbourhood of her cottage, in one of the low-ceilinged rooms or in the orchard, she was supremely happy, bewildered, or in great distress, and tonight trouble had been predominant. She thought the pain of it must have waked her, or her own cry, but as she lay, trying to compose herself, she heard a sound outside her door and the turning of the handle.

“Who is it?” she said and, under the influence of her dream, her voice was not quite steady.

“It’s only me, Miss Mole. I thought I heard funny noises.”

Hannah fumbled for the matches and lit the candle by her bed. Ruth stood in the doorway, clad only in her nightgown, with her feet bare, and in the uncertain light she looked like a little wraith with frightened eyes.

Hannah swung herself out of bed. “Get in, quick!” she cried. She threw the bedclothes over Ruth and put on her own dressing-gown. “What is it?” she asked briskly. “Burglars?”

===Chinese===

那夜的某个时辰，她猛地惊醒。她刚做了一个梦，是常做的那个梦的又一种变体。场景永远不变：在她那间村舍左近，或在某间顶棚低矮的屋子里，或者在果园里，她时而幸福到了极点，时而惶惑无措，时而深陷苦楚；今夜占上风的是苦恼。她想，一定是那份痛把她惊醒的，要么是她自己喊出了声；她躺着，想让自己定下神来，却听见门外有响动，还有拧门把的声音。

“是谁？”她说；梦的余威底下，她的声音不太稳。

“是我，莫尔小姐。我好像听见了怪声。”

汉娜摸到火柴，点亮了床头的蜡烛。露丝站在门口，只穿着睡袍，光着两只脚；烛光摇曳不定，她看上去就像一个小幽灵，眼睛里满是惊惶。

汉娜一骨碌翻身下床。“快进来！”她叫道。她把被子甩过去盖住露丝，披上自己的晨衣。“怎么了？”她利利索索地问。“有贼？”

===Original===

“I don’t know.” Ruth’s teeth were chattering. “I’d been dreaming.”

“Ah﻿—so had I,” Hannah said.

“I’d been dreaming﻿—and I expect I’m being silly, but I wish I needn’t sleep in that dressing-room. It’s bad enough when Father’s there, but tonight his room was so empty or so﻿—or so full. And I couldn’t find the matches to light the gas, and I thought I heard someone moving, so I ran up here. I’m sorry, Miss Mole.”

“Don’t mention it!” Hannah said, making a funny face and sitting on the bed. “If it’s burglars, I propose to stay here. No good interfering with them. Might cause bad feeling. We’ll give them a few minutes to help themselves, and when I think they’ve gone I’ll go and look for them.”

Ruth laughed, and it was the first time Hannah had heard her do it naturally. “I don’t suppose it was burglars at all. They wouldn’t come to a house like this, would they? But I don’t want to go back to that room, Miss Mole.”

===Chinese===

“不知道。”露丝的牙齿咯咯打战。“我刚才在做梦。”

“啊——我也是，”汉娜说。

“我在做梦——我知道我这准是犯傻，可我真巴不得用不着睡那间更衣室。父亲在家的时候已经够呛了，可今晚他那屋子那么空，要么那么——要么那么满。火柴也找不着，煤气灯点不上，我好像听见有人在走动，就跑到这儿来了。对不起，莫尔小姐。”

“提这个干吗！”汉娜说着做了个滑稽的鬼脸，在床沿坐下。“要真是贼，我打算就待在这儿。犯不上跟贼捣乱，要伤和气的。咱们给他们几分钟自便，等我想着他们走净了，再去寻他们。”

露丝笑了，汉娜头一回听见她笑得这样自然。“我想多半压根儿不是贼。贼也不会上这样一户人家来，对吧？可我不想回那间屋去，莫尔小姐。”

===Original===

“You shan’t. I’ll go. We don’t mind each other’s sheets, do we? And you’ll feel happy up here, won’t you, with my little ship on the mantelpiece, and you’ll go to sleep?”

Ruth nodded. “Where did you get your little ship?”

“Off the mantelpiece in my old home in the country. I’ll tell you about it some day.”

“Whereabouts in the country?”

===Chinese===

“那就不回。我去。彼此的床单，谁也不会嫌弃谁的，对吧？你睡这儿会舒坦的，壁炉台上还有我那艘小船陪着，是不是？这就睡，啊？”

露丝点点头。“你那艘小船是哪儿来的？”

“从乡下老家的壁炉台上取下来的。哪天讲给你听。”

“乡下什么地方？”

===Original===

“Over the hills﻿—but not very far away.” She was silent for a minute or two, looking down. “Well,” she said, “I should think they’ve gone by this time. Good night. Promise you’ll go to sleep.”

“Won’t you be frightened yourself?”

“Not a bit. I met a burglar once and liked him. I’ll tell you about that, too, in the daytime. I shall have to put out the candle, you know.”

“I know. I don’t mind. Miss Mole﻿—” darkness made this confession easier﻿—“I don’t believe I really believed there were burglars at all.”

“No. It was a bad dream. I was having one myself. I’m glad you woke me. I’ll buy some night-lights tomorrow. The matches are never where you want them.”

“And they go out when you’re in a hurry. And Miss Mole﻿—” this was still more difficult﻿—“you won’t tell anybody, will you?”

===Chinese===

“翻过山冈就是——并没有多远。”她垂着眼，静了一两分钟。“好了，”她说，“我想这会儿他们也走净了。晚安。答应我，这就睡觉。”

“你自己不怕吗？”

“一点儿也不怕。我从前跟一个贼打过一回照面，还挺喜欢他。这件事也等白天讲给你听。你知道，蜡烛我可得吹了。”

“我知道。我不在乎。莫尔小姐——”黑暗叫这句招认容易了些——“我压根儿就不信真有什么贼。”

“可不是。是一场噩梦。我自己也正做着一个。你把我惊醒了，我倒高兴。明天我去买几盏小夜灯。火柴从来不肯待在你要找它们的地方。”

“还有，人一着急它们偏偏就灭。还有，莫尔小姐——”这一句更加难以启齿——“你不会告诉任何人，对吧？”

===Original===

“But, of course I shall!” Hannah said with a mocking seriousness. “The first thing I shall do in the morning is to tell Doris, then your sister, then your cousin, and when your father comes home he shall hear all about it.”

Ruth laughed again, a little ghostly sound, and Hannah, as she went down the dark stairs, said to herself triumphantly, “I’ve got her now!” but with her triumph a little dismay was mixed. She knew the hampering nature of possessions.

===Chinese===

“咦，我当然要讲喽！”汉娜一本正经地打趣道。“明儿一早，我头一件事就是告诉多丽丝，再告诉你姐姐，再告诉你表哥；等你父亲回来，还要一五一十讲给他听呢。”

露丝又笑了，幽幽的一点声息。汉娜摸黑下楼时，得意地对自己说：“这下我可把她抓住了！”只是得意之中还掺着一丝惶惑。她深知拥有之物是何等缠累人的东西。

## V / 第五章

===Original===

The wind had risen strongly as night came on and Hannah crossed the Downs under swaying branches and swirling leaves. The football-players, the riders, the children had all gone home; lamps edged the roads but, where Hannah walked under the elms, there was a stormy darkness. The branches creaked lugubriously or with shrill protest, and those which still kept their leaves were like great flails, threshing the winds, maddened by their sterile efforts, for it was the wind, threshing harder, that produced the harvest, whipping it from the trees and driving it before him. Hannah was driven, too; a wisp of a woman, exhilarated by the noise and the buffeting. Lilla’s comfortable, bright room seemed unreal to her, Mr. Corder was the invention of an idle moment and Hannah Mole had no past, no future, only this breathless present when the wind would have had her go westwards and she was making for the south. For ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, until she reached lower ground and the shelter of the streets, where the wind did its best with the trees in the gardens but found their weaker resistance a dull affair, she had that freedom from care which is the reward of exciting physical effort; but in the comparative quiet of Chatterton Road she became conscious of the self which needed money for food and clothing and, absurdly, she saw it handed to her by Mr. Corder on one of his own offertory plates. She shook her head and made a grimace of refusal. She had a prejudice against Nonconformist ministers, she pictured Mr. Corder according to the pattern in her mind, ignorantly unctuous, pretending to a humility which was patently absent, and she had a moment of rebellion. She could see herself clearly enough with other people’s eyes: she was drab, she was nearing, if she had not reached, middle-age, she bore the stamp of a woman who had always worked against the grain, she was, in fact, the ideal housekeeper for Mr. Corder. She admitted that no one sitting in his dining-room and mending his woven underwear at a table with a rusty little fern in the middle of a green serge cloth, could look more suitable than Hannah Mole. Who would suspect her of a sense of fun and irony, of a passionate love for beauty and the power to drag it from its hidden places? Who could imagine that Miss Mole had pictured herself, at different times, as an explorer in strange lands, as a lady wrapped in luxury and delicate garments, as the mother of adorably naughty children and the inspiringly elusive mistress of a poet? She could turn up her own long nose at these fanciful excursions, without convincing herself of their improbability. The desires, the energy, the gaiety were there, but they were ruled by an ironic conception of herself which did not seem inconsistent and which was also the armour she assumed against the world when it was not willing to be friendly. And, after all, as she told herself, quelling her useless rebellion, the things she wanted, if she had them, would soon turn into those she wanted no longer and﻿—here was God’s happy idea of compensation again﻿—she found a wealth of amusement in going about disguised, while because her clear-sightedness was not kept entirely for the weaknesses of others, she had to own that she who had been a failure in the lot forced on her, was not likely to be a success in any one she chose. She was a vagrant and with the vagrant’s advantages, the readiness to move on, the carelessness of possessions, she had to support the inconvenience of being moved on before she was ready to go and of finding herself poorer than was comfortable. Now both these conditions had befallen her. Few things, she thought at that moment, could be more distasteful than leaving the house where she was treated as a friend, where she had a sly, lazy pleasure in listening to Mrs. Gibson’s platitudes and a keener one in discomfiting Mr. Blenkinsop by waylaying him on the stairs and forcing him into conversation; a house which she could leave at her caprice for a saunter round Upper Radstowe or a long walk on the other side of the river, to which she could return with the certainty of a welcome, and she must resign all this for the sake of mending Mr. Corder’s daughter’s stockings and keeping herself fed and clad.

===Chinese===

夜幕四合，风势转猛，汉娜穿过丘陵草原（the Downs），头顶枝条摇撼，落叶打着旋飞舞。踢球的、骑马的、孩子们都已回家去了；路灯沿路镶着边，可汉娜走在榆树底下，唯有一片风雨将至的昏暗。枝条咯吱作响，凄惶的，或是尖声抗议的；那些还挂得住叶子的，活像一具具大连枷，拍打着风，为劳而无功气得发狂——因为打出这场收成的是风，是风拍打得更狠，把收成从树上鞭落，又赶在自己前头驱驰。汉娜也一样被赶着走；一缕游丝似的瘦小女人，被这喧嚣与扑打撩得心神畅旺。莉拉（Lilla）那间舒适明亮的屋子在她心里虚幻起来，科德先生（Mr. Corder）不过是闲散一刻的凭空杜撰，而汉娜·莫尔没有过去，没有将来，只有这个叫人屏息的当下——风要她往西去，她偏朝南走。有那么十分钟，或是一刻钟——直到她走到低处、躲进街道的庇护；在那里，风对园中的树木依旧竭尽全力，却嫌它们抵抗得软弱，兴味索然——她一直享有一份摆脱忧烦的自在，那是剧烈的体力活动给人的犒赏；可一走进查特顿路（Chatterton Road）相对的安静，她又觉察到那个吃饭穿衣都得要钱的自家了，而且荒唐地看见，科德先生用他自己那只奉献盘，把这样一个自家端送到她面前。她摇摇头，做了个拒绝的鬼脸。她对非国教派牧师素怀成见，便照心里的老套模子描画科德先生：无知而油滑，装出一副一眼便知的假谦卑；她心头一时起了叛逆。借旁人的眼睛，她把自己看得够清楚：她灰暗土气，年近中年——纵使还未到——浑身带着一种女人一辈子干着违拗本性的营生的印记；说实在的，她正是科德先生理想的女管家。她承认，坐在他的餐室里，在一张铺着绿哔叽台布、正中摆着一盆锈暗小蕨草的桌上，缝补他的针织内衣——再没有人能比汉娜·莫尔显得更般配了。谁会疑心她也有诙谐与讥讽的趣味，也炽热地爱着美，还有把美从藏匿的角落里硬拽出来的本事？谁又能想见，莫尔小姐曾在不同的年月里把自己想象成陌生疆土的探险家，想象成让奢华与精细衣裳裹着的贵妇人，想象成一群娇憨顽童的母亲，想象成叫诗人捉摸不透、却因此灵感泉涌的情人？对这些想入非非的漫游，她尽可以竖起自己的长鼻子嗤笑，却终究说服不了自己：它们绝非可能。欲望、精力、欢快，样样都在，只是全归她那套讥诮的自我观照辖制——这观照似乎并无不谐，况且每逢世界不肯示好，它还是她披挂起来抵御世界的铠甲。何况说到底，正如她对自己讲的，把那点无用的叛逆按下去便是：她想要的东西，纵然到手，转眼也会变成她不再想要的；而——上帝那套乐呵呵的补偿法子在此又见应验——她发现乔装行走自有无穷乐趣；只是她的眼力并没有全数留给旁人的短处，她不得不承认：在被强派的命运里做了失败者的她，换了自选的路，也未必就能成功。她是个流浪人，享着流浪人的好处——说挪就挪的爽利，对身外之物的淡漠——也就得受流浪人的不便利：还没打算挪窝就被人撵，等挪了，又发觉自己穷得叫人不舒服。如今这两样她都赶上了。此刻她想着：世间少有别的事比离开那座宅子更叫她不情愿——在那里她被当作朋友，在那里听吉布森太太（Mrs. Gibson）说些老生常谈，她有一份狡黠而慵懒的快活，而在楼梯上截住布伦金索普先生（Mr. Blenkinsop）、逼他攀谈、叫他败下阵来，更有一份锋利的快活；那座宅子，她尽可兴之所至说走就走，去上拉德斯托闲逛一圈，或是到河对岸远足一场，回来必定受到欢迎——而这一切，她竟得统统舍下，为的是替科德先生的女儿补袜子，换自己一饱一暖。

===Original===

Yet it was better to be Hannah Mole than to be Lilla who could see herself as one person only, and that was Mrs. Spenser-Smith, who had never broken a basement window to save a man from gas poisoning, dragged him from the neighbourhood of the oven, and then consoled the baby who was crying, neglected, in his perambulator: better than to be poor little Mrs. Ridding with that strange look on her face. It was the look, Hannah thought, of someone who had braced herself for approaching an inevitable catastrophe and seen its postponement with despair. The look had no more than flashed across her face but Hannah had seen it and she could recall it plainly now, in the darkness. “Oh, for money!” Hannah moaned, not thinking of herself. Money could cause neurotics to be cured or, if it failed in that, it could enable a young widow to bring up her boy, and Hannah began to desire it passionately. She had heard Lilla speak with grand disparagement of it, but that was just what Lilla, who had always had it, would take care to do. Money was one of the best things in the world, used properly, used by Miss Hannah Mole, and all the way down Prince’s Road she was buying annuities for people like herself, settling some thousands of pounds on Mrs. Ridding, and sending people Christmas cards and valentines in the shape of five pound notes.

When she reached Mrs. Gibson’s house she saw a light in the basement kitchen and, through the mended window, which was open, she could hear Mrs. Ridding singing. Hannah’s big mouth drooped. She heard that singing every morning before Mr. Ridding went to work and every evening when he came home, but never at any other time of day, and it hurt her that anyone so young should be so unhappy and so brave. She was ashamed of her own discontent and her concentration on herself. What happened to her, who had lived more than half her life and had some fun in it and, yes, one mad, romantic interlude, was of very little importance now, but Mrs. Ridding was a girl and Hannah’s large, erratic heart was aching for her. And there was nothing she could do. Her funds of advice﻿—which she did not take herself﻿—of drollery, of encouragement, were of no use, for Mrs. Ridding was very bright and cold with the witnesses of that sordid scene in the basement, even with the woman who had comforted and bathed the baby. Hannah wished she could bath the baby again. Mrs. Gibson had been much impressed by her handling of him; Mrs. Gibson, in fact, admired everything Hannah did, and perhaps it would be salutary for her to live with a man who was much more likely to admire what he did himself.

===Chinese===

可话说回来，做汉娜·莫尔总强似做莉拉——莉拉只能把自己看成一个人，那便是斯宾塞-史密斯太太（Mrs. Spenser-Smith），从不曾砸开地下室的窗子，把一个男人从煤气毒气里救出来，把他从烤炉近旁拖开，再去哄那个被撇下、在婴儿车里啼哭的娃娃；也强似做可怜的小里丁太太（Mrs. Ridding），脸上带着那么一种怪神情。汉娜想，那神情属于这样一种人：攒足了劲去迎一场躲不开的大祸，却眼看祸事延期，反而陷入绝望。那神情不过在她脸上一闪而过，可汉娜看见了，此刻在黑暗里，还能把它清清楚楚回想起来。“唉，要是有钱！”汉娜呻吟道——想的并不是自己。钱能让神经症患者痊愈；就算治不好，也能让一个年轻的寡妇把儿子拉扯成人。汉娜开始热烈地渴望起钱来。她听莉拉谈起钱来，总是一副高姿态的不屑；可一贯不缺钱的莉拉，正少不得要摆这副腔调。只要使得得当，又是汉娜·莫尔小姐在使，钱便是世上顶好的东西之一；沿王子路一路走下去，她忙着给同自己一样的人置办年金，划几千镑给里丁太太作终身受用，还把一张张五镑钞票做成圣诞卡和情人节卡片，分赠众人。

到了吉布森太太的宅子，她看见地下室厨房里亮着灯，透过那扇修补过的窗子——窗开着——听得见里丁太太在唱歌。汉娜的大嘴角耷拉了下来。每天早晨，里丁先生（Mr. Ridding）出门上工之前；每天傍晚，他回家进门之后——她都听见这歌声；可一天里其余的时辰，从来不曾有过。这样年轻的人竟这样不幸，又这样勇敢，叫她心里生疼。她为自己那份不满足、为自己一味只顾自己，暗暗羞愧。她自己的遭遇——一个活过半辈子的人，有过些乐趣，也有过，是的，一段疯狂而浪漫的插曲——如今实在无足轻重；可里丁太太还是个姑娘家，汉娜那颗又大又不服管束的心，正为她作痛。而她什么忙也帮不上。她那满库的忠告——连她自己都不服用的那种——满库的诙谐、满库的鼓励，一概无用；因为对地下室那桩龌龊场面的目击者们，里丁太太一概凛然冷淡，连那个哄过娃娃、给他洗过澡的女人也不例外。汉娜巴不得能再给那娃娃洗一回澡。吉布森太太对她摆弄娃娃的手法大为叹服；说实在的，汉娜做的桩桩件件，吉布森太太无不佩服；而要她去同一个更可能佩服自家所作所为的男人住在一处，对她倒许是一服良药。

===Original===

Hannah turned the lock with the latchkey Mrs. Gibson lent her and found Mr. Blenkinsop hanging up his hat in the hall.

“Oh, good evening, Mr. Blenkinsop!” she exclaimed girlishly. “You’re rather late, aren’t you?”

Mr. Blenkinsop looked at her severely through his spectacles. “Purposely,” he remarked significantly, and stood aside to let her pass up the stairs before him.

===Chinese===

汉娜用吉布森太太借给她的钥匙拧开门锁，正撞见布伦金索普先生在门厅里挂帽子。

“哦，晚上好，布伦金索普先生！”她少女般欢快地叫道。“您回来得挺晚哪，是不是？”

布伦金索普先生隔着眼镜严厉地打量她。“故意的，”他意味深长地说，随即闪身站开，让她先行上楼。

===Original===

Hannah went ahead meekly. She had not yet found the manner to which Mr. Blenkinsop would respond. She had tried to deepen the impression which her prowess in the basement kitchen must have made on him; she had hinted that she, too, had an interest in literature and Charles Lamb; she had asked foolish feminine questions about banking, which was Mr. Blenkinsop’s profession, but nothing stirred him. He remained grave, solid, and as monosyllabic as language and bare courtesy would allow.

“Sickening!” she said to herself, straightening her back, for she knew that the view, from below, of a woman ascending the stairs is often unfortunate, but when she had turned on the light in her room and looked at her reflection, she forgave him, though she had not done with him yet. Mr. Blenkinsop was clearly not a reader of character or a connoisseur of human rarities, and there was no reason why he should encourage the attentions of this woman with the satirical nose, a rather sallow skin and eyes of no particular colour, yet she felt as uneasy as a soldier in a hostile country who has left an unconquered fortified place behind him. She wished she had introduced the subject of Mr. Corder; that might have roused him and instructed her at the same time. Forewarned was forearmed and Mrs. Gibson’s views would be of no value. To her, all reverend gentlemen were good and most of them were awful; they were like the stars; they shed their light, but they were unapproachable. However, though there was no doubt about what she would say, her way of saying it might be amusing, and when Hannah had changed her outdoor dress for an old silk one which looked well enough by artificial light, she tapped on Mrs. Gibson’s sitting-room door and popped in without waiting for a summons.

“Oh, there you are, dear,” Mrs. Gibson sighed. “Always so cheerful!”

===Chinese===

汉娜温顺地走在前头。布伦金索普先生肯回应的是哪一路做派，她还没摸着门道。她曾设法加深自己在地下室厨房里的那番身手想必给他留下的印象；她曾暗示自己对文学、对查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）也素有兴味；她曾就银行业问过些傻里傻气的小女人问题——那可是布伦金索普先生的行当——却什么也没能撼动他。他依旧庄重、敦实，话短到只剩单音节，只要语言和起码的礼数还容许。

“可恶！”她挺直背脊，暗自骂道——她知道，打楼下仰望一个上楼的女人，那景象往往不雅；可待她开亮房里的灯，照了照镜中的自己，便原谅了他，虽然她跟他的账还没有算完。布伦金索普先生显然不懂得品读性格，也算不得人间奇珍的鉴赏家，这位女人——生着讥诮的鼻子、肤色颇为蜡黄、眼睛说不上是什么颜色——他原犯不着招惹她的殷勤垂青；可她心里总不踏实，像身陷敌国的士兵，背后留着一座未曾攻克的要塞。她悔不曾把科德先生这个话头端出来；那或许既能叫他开口，又能叫自己长见识。预先得知，便是预先设防；何况吉布森太太的高见，准保一文不值。在她看来，牧师先生们个个良善，而其中大多数又令人敬畏；他们像天上的星辰，洒下光华，却高不可攀。不过，说什么她心里有数，怎么说出口的那副腔调，或许倒有趣；汉娜脱下出门的衣裳，换了件旧绸袍——在灯光底下看着也还过得去——便去敲吉布森太太起居室的门，不等召唤，一头钻了进去。

“哦，你回来啦，亲爱的，”吉布森太太叹道。“总是这么欢欢喜喜的！”

===Original===

“What’s the matter?” Hannah asked, for Mrs. Gibson’s voice was melancholy and she was sunk in her chair as though she had been pushed there.

“He’s been at me,” Mrs. Gibson said, “about the Riddings. He’s just this minute left me. It’s either them or him, he says. What d’you think of that? I’m sorry to say it, but I call it unkind, unkind to me and to those poor things down there. Now, what would you do yourself, Miss Mole, dear? Would you turn them out? No, I know you wouldn’t. Standoffish as she is, considering everything, if you know what I mean, I can’t help feeling I’ve got a duty by her. I can keep my eye on her. And there’s that baby. I never had one of my own and, if you ask me, the motherly ones are those that never had any.”

“Ah,” said Hannah weightily. Her thoughts, straying from Mrs. Gibson’s problem, were pursuing this idea. She had believed it was her own and she was surprised to find that it was also Mrs. Gibson’s. “But you had a husband,” she said.

“Well, of course, dear. And I was a good wife to him. Those are his own words.”

===Chinese===

“出什么事啦？”汉娜问。吉布森太太的声音满含愁郁，人陷在椅子里，倒像叫人一把推上去的。

“他缠着我说了个没完，”吉布森太太说，“为着里丁家两口子的事。这会儿刚从我这儿走。他说，要么他们走，要么他走。你说说看，这像什么话？说出来我难过，可我得说这叫薄情，对我薄情，对楼下那两个可怜人也薄情。那么，换了你自己会怎么办，亲爱的莫尔小姐？你会把他们赶出去吗？不会，我知道你不会。她虽待人不亲热——把种种情形都算上，你懂我的意思——我总觉得自己对她有一份责任。我可以替她看着点。还有那个娃娃。我自己一个也没养过；要我说呀，顶有母性的，偏偏是那些从没养过孩子的。”

“啊，”汉娜沉甸甸地应了一声。她的心思早从吉布森太太的难题上溜了号，正追着这个念头跑。她原以为那是自己独有的见解，不想发现吉布森太太也持此论，不免一惊。“可您有过丈夫呀，”她说。

“嗳，那自然，亲爱的。我对他也算得贤妻。这是他自己的话。”

===Original===

“I was wondering,” Hannah said, “if the best wives are the ones who are not married.”

“Oh, my dear, I don’t hold with that kind of thing!”

But Hannah was trying to find proofs for her theory that non-realization was the highest good.

“I don’t mean what you mean,” she said.

“I’m glad of that,” said Mrs. Gibson. “There’s too much of that kind of thing nowadays﻿—so I’m told.”

===Chinese===

“我正纳闷呢，”汉娜说，“顶好的妻子，是不是恰恰是没结婚的那些。”

“哦哟我的天，那种话我可不敢苟同！”

可汉娜正在替自己的理论搜求论据：不得实现，方为至善。

“我说的不是您想的那个意思，”她说。

“那就好，”吉布森太太说。“如今这号事太多啦——我是听人这么讲的。”

===Original===

“Dreadful, isn’t it?” Hannah murmured back.

“And anyhow, there’s no question of that here, I’m thankful to say, but he tells me there’ll be trouble again; he says he doesn’t feel the same about the place. He says he needs quiet after his day’s work.”

Hannah made a loud, derisive noise. “Work! Chasing money with a little shovel! It’s like playing tiddleywinks! And quiet!” She held up a hand. “Listen, Mrs. Gibson. There’s not a sound.”

Mrs. Gibson nodded complaisantly. “A well-built house. I don’t know where he’d find a better. And then, you see, I knew his ma. At the sewing-meeting. I don’t go now, dear. I’ve enough to do with the mending at home and Mr. Blenkinsop’s very hard on his socks, but in the old days, with Mrs. Blenkinsop and Mrs. Corder, I went. And now she’s passed away and little did I think then I’d ever have her son for a lodger. She was a gloomy woman, I must say, but all the same, there it is.”

===Chinese===

“吓人，是不是？”汉娜低声应和。

“再说，这儿压根儿谈不到那上头去，谢天谢地；可他跟我说，麻烦还要再来；他说他对这宅子不像从前那样有感情了。他说他做完一天的活，需要清静。”

汉娜嗤地出了声，又响又刻薄。“工作！拿把小铲子满地追钱！跟玩弹圆片有什么两样！还要清静！”她举起一只手。“您听，吉布森太太。一点儿声音都没有。”

吉布森太太欣然点头。“这房子造得结实。我不知道他上哪儿还能寻着更好的。再说，您瞧，我认得他妈妈。在缝纫会（sewing-meeting）上。如今我不去了，亲爱的。家里补的衣裳就够我忙的，布伦金索普先生的袜子又费得厉害；可在早年间，我同布伦金索普太太、科德太太（Mrs. Corder）一道去。如今她老人家过世了，当年哪想得到，日后她儿子会做了我的房客。说句实话，她是个阴沉沉的女人，可话又说回来，事情就是这么档子事。”

===Original===

“And Mrs. Corder﻿—what’s she like?”

“Dead, too, dear. Yes. Pneumonia. It’s a terrible thing. Here today and gone tomorrow. Only ill for a week. Poor man! I’ll never forget the funeral.”

Now Hannah made a vague sound of sympathy. “A loss to the chapel,” she suggested.

“Well”﻿—Mrs. Gibson, who had been growing drowsy over the fire and her reminiscences, tried to sit more upright and her voice was almost a whisper﻿—“well, I don’t know about that. People used to say things. She was never at the Sunday evening service, and that didn’t look well, did it?”

===Chinese===

“那位科德太太——她是个什么样的人？”

“也不在了，亲爱的。是啊。肺炎。真是桩可怕的事。今天还在，明天就走。病了统共一个礼拜。可怜的男人！那场葬礼我这辈子忘不了。”

这回汉娜含含糊糊地应出一声同情。“礼拜堂的一大损失，”她凑上一句。

“这个嘛”——吉布森太太对着炉火、也对着自己的旧忆，本是越坐越沉了，这时竭力坐直了些，声音几乎成了耳语——“这个嘛，我可说不上。人们从前是说三道四的。礼拜天晚间的崇拜她从来不到，那可不太好看，是不是？”

===Original===

“Tired of hearing him talk, perhaps.”

“That might have been it,” Mrs. Gibson said with unexpected tolerance. “A wife feels different to anybody else. But at the sewing-meeting, now and then, she’d be funny rather. Absentminded,” she added, triumphant at finding the right word.

“Thinking of him,” Hannah suggested again.

“Ah, now, you can’t have it both ways!” Mrs. Gibson cried cunningly.

===Chinese===

“也许是听他讲话听腻了。”

“兴许正是这个缘故，”吉布森太太说着，露出叫人意外的宽容。“做妻子的，感受总跟旁人两样。不过在缝纫会上，她倒时常有点儿怪怪的。心不在焉，”她补上一句，为寻着了恰切的字眼而得意。

“想他想的呢，”汉娜又凑上一句。

“嗳呀，这可两头的好处都叫您占了！”吉布森太太狡黠地嚷道。

===Original===

“No, but you can think in heaps of them,” Hannah said, and she gave her nose the twist which could mean disgust or a bitter kind of satisfaction.

Mrs. Gibson wisely ignored these possibilities. “And then, he’d come in and give us a look round, as cheery as you could want.”

“I know,” said Hannah.

“And laugh! He was full of his jokes.”

===Chinese===

“是不行，可想头儿有的是，尽够成堆地想，”汉娜说，一面把鼻子那么一拧——这一拧，可以是厌恶，也可以是一种苦涩的快意。

吉布森太太明智地不去理会这些可能。“还有呢，他会走进来，朝我们四下里望一望，要多欢脱有多欢脱。”

“我知道，”汉娜说。

“还大笑！他满肚子都是笑话。”

===Original===

“I know,” Hannah repeated grimly. How was she going to meet those jokes, or were they less frequent in the family circle? She was convinced that his wife had hated him, and while Mrs. Gibson rambled on, Hannah was either gazing into a future full of dislike for that hearty man or reconstructing the married misery of Mrs. Corder.

“How I’ve been talking!” Mrs. Gibson said at last. “And you haven’t told me what I’m to do about Mr. Blenkinsop.”

“Tell him he ought to be ashamed of himself,” Hannah said, rising from the hearthrug, and she went away, leaving Mrs. Gibson disappointed, for the first time, in the resourcefulness of Miss Mole.

===Chinese===

“我知道，”汉娜沉下脸来重复道。到了她，该怎么招架那些笑话？还是说，在自家人圈子里，笑话会稀落一些？她确信他那亡妻恨透了他；吉布森太太絮絮叨叨往下讲，汉娜的心思忽而盯着一个前景出神——那里满是对那个粗门大嗓快活汉子的厌恶——忽而又在重构科德太太婚后那本苦账。

“瞧我，一说就说了半天！”吉布森太太末了说。“可你还没告诉我，布伦金索普先生的事，我该怎么办呢。”

“告诉他，他该为自己害臊，”汉娜说罢，从炉前地毯上起身走了，留下吉布森太太平生头一回对莫尔小姐的足智多谋感到失望。

## VI / 第六章

===Original===

Beresford Road and Prince’s Road meet at a point just below Albert Square, and as they are both on the western side of Nunnery Road, where the tramcars go up to the Downs and down to the city, they can claim to be in Upper Radstowe, but, except for that terraced row of houses on one side of what the errand boys call Prince’s, they might belong to any other mid-Victorian suburb. The houses in Beresford Road are just emerging from the basement era. Their kitchens are still a few steps below the level of the sitting-rooms, but they have been raised from the cellarage whence a sturdier, or dumber, race of servants was content to ascend a long flight of stairs an innumerable number of times a day. Some of the houses are surrounded by their own gardens; others look like one house and are really two, with their entrances deceptively placed at the side; they give, and want to give, the impression that nothing unusual or indecorous can happen within their walls, and the red bulk of Beresford Road Congregational Chapel proclaims that Nonconformity has been received into the bosom of respectability. Perhaps the great days of the chapel are over. It was built for the benefit of those rising families whose incomes permitted a removal to this part of Radstowe and whose religious convictions had not changed with their fortunes; at a time when there was still something faintly defiant in marching a large, decently clad family to chapel in the face of churchgoers who had never dared anything for freedom, but now that the church had amiably acknowledged dissenters as men and brothers, the only flavour left in Nonconformity was the unpleasant knowledge that its supporters were still considered socially doubtful. Big families were now out of fashion: many of those girls and boys who had regarded Sundays as half festival, half penance, who had been glad to wear their best clothes over the prickly discomfort of their clean under garments, and to watch their acquaintances through the boredom of the services, had moved on to what was called a better part of Upper Radstowe, reared two or three children and quietly seceded from the faith of their fathers. The bolder spirits, who still went anywhere, went to the Unitarian Church, but Upper Radstowe was not the right soil for Nonconformity, which flourished more abundantly on the other side of Nunnery Road, and the appointment of Robert Corder to Beresford Road Chapel, fifteen years ago, had been an attempt to stir the sluggish ground with the chemical of a vigorous personality. His predecessor had been a gentle old man who preached patiently to the shining empty pews and could not be expected to lure young people to hear him, while Robert Corder was a bold, upstanding figure and the sight and sound of him advertised the vitality of his faith. He could be seen, striding about the streets, leaping on to moving tramcars and off them, rushing to and from committee meetings, always in a hurry, yet always willing to check his speed for a few words with his acquaintances, and these words were cheery, optimistic and delivered in a loud voice, unless a quieter sympathy was called for. A member of his chapel brought him to a halt; a deacon could divert him from his course, and then he would stride on, still faster, making up for time which, as he often and merrily told these waylayers, was not lost, but had gone, before he knew it.

These were habits and occupations with which Miss Mole was not yet acquainted. From the window of her bed-sitting room she could see the roof of the chapel and she had noticed it as a warm patch of red among the other roofs and the trees. She was a specialist in roofs and from many high windows in Upper Radstowe they could be seen in every shape and colour. They slid down the steep slope to Lower Radstowe, red, grey, blue and green, and spread like a flower garden over the city. There were old red tiles in close neighbourhood to shining slates, there were mossy green roofs squeezed between the walls of higher houses and, with all these variations in height, there were trees overtopping chimneys and chimneys sending their smoke into other people’s windows, but this could not be seen from Hannah’s room. Her view of the newer part of Upper Radstowe was ordinary enough, and she had made the most of the chapel roof until it had suddenly changed into a portent. She liked it no longer, yet she looked at it more often: she imagined herself sitting under it, a small drab speck against the varnish, and, during the days which passed before she heard from Lilla, she spent some time in Beresford Road, feeling like a conspirator or a private detective. She tried to get into the chapel and found, as she expected, that the doors were locked. “This,” she said, addressing the ivy which grew round the porch, “is enough to drive anybody to Rome, and I’ve a good mind to go there. I suppose the place is so ugly that they daren’t let anyone see it unless there’s one of their entertainments going on, and then they’ve got each other’s hats to look at. Not,” she added, “that there can be anything much in the way of hats.”

She would have been very gloomy if she had not been enjoying her prejudices and her prophetic powers. She was sure the red roof was painted blue on the inside, like the firmament, and sprinkled with opaque golden stars: she had seen Mr. Corder’s house, No. 14﻿—a long stone’s throw from the chapel and on the other side of the road﻿—and her forebodings had been justified. It was one of the coupled houses, with an asphalt path running up to the inconspicuous door. There were a bow and a flat window on each of two floors and a small one, like an eye, in the gable. Hannah had strolled past the house adventurously: at any moment Mr. Corder might appear and it would be hard to look like a woman with a right to be in the road. She was not curious about him: it was the house she was concerned with, and if she could put her head inside its door she would know, at once, whether she could be happy in it. That, however, was further than she dared to go and she had to content herself with an external view, finding nothing hopeful in the lace curtains and the plot of grass edged with laurels and confined by iron railings. The architect of that house had been no artist. It was an ugly house, yet its twin, next door, looked infinitely more habitable, though the Venetian blinds were askew. Dusty-looking red curtains, clasped by brass chains, draped the lower bow window and a birdcage containing a canary reminded Hannah painfully of Mrs. Widdows. No. 16 was quite as unattractive as No. 14 to the eye, but Hannah would have felt happier if the red curtains had belonged to Mr. Corder.

===Chinese===

贝雷斯福德路（Beresford Road）与王子路在阿尔伯特广场（Albert Square）略下方一点交汇；两条路都在修女院路（Nunnery Road）以西——电车打这条路上行去丘陵草原，下行进城——因此好歹有资格自居为上拉德斯托（Upper Radstowe）的地界；可除了“王子”一侧那排梯式老屋——“王子”是跑腿的小伙计们对王子路的叫法——这两条路放进随便哪个维多利亚时代中叶的市郊都毫无破绽。贝雷斯福德路上的房子，正从地下室时代里探出头来。厨房仍比客厅低上几级台阶，但好歹已从地窖里升了上来——想当年，有一族更皮实、或者更迟钝的仆人，甘愿一天里无数遍攀爬那道长长的楼梯。有的房子四周围着自己的花园；有的看着像一栋，其实是两栋，入口诡诈地设在侧面；这些房子呈现着、也一心要呈现这样一种气象：四堵墙之内绝不会出什么出格的、失体面的事；而贝雷斯福德路公理会教堂（Beresford Road Congregational Chapel）那红色的庞然躯体，则昭告世人：非国教（Nonconformity）已被拥入体面的怀抱。教堂的黄金岁月或许已成过去。当年建它，是为着那些正在发迹的家庭：他们的进项允许他们搬进拉德斯托（Radstowe）的这一带来住，宗教信念却没有随家运一同改换；在那个年月，领着一大群衣冠齐整的儿女，当着那些从未敢为自由冒半点风险的国教徒的面，昂然走向礼拜堂，那行进里多少还带着一丝隐约的挑衅；可如今教会已和颜悦色地承认非国教徒也是人、也是弟兄，非国教信仰里剩下的唯一滋味，便是一桩叫人不痛快的事实：信众依旧被看作社会上靠不住的一类。如今大家庭不时兴了：当年那些把礼拜天过成一半节庆、一半苦修的姑娘小伙——身穿最好的衣裳，里面是浆洗干净却扎人的内衣，倒也自得其乐；礼拜冗长乏味，全靠打量熟识的人来消磨——其中许多早已迁往所谓上拉德斯托更好的地段，养大两三个儿女，悄悄脱离了祖辈的信仰。胆子大些、还肯哪儿都去的人，就进了那位论派教堂（Unitarian Church）；然而上拉德斯托的土壤不宜非国教生长，它在修女院路另一侧反倒繁盛得多；十五年前把罗伯特·科德（Robert Corder）派到贝雷斯福德路礼拜堂来，便是想拿一剂名叫“强人个性”的化学药水，搅动这片沉滞的土壤。他的前任是位温厚的老先生，对着亮闪闪的空长椅耐着性子讲道，自然指望不上把年轻人诱来听他；罗伯特·科德却是个气宇轩昂、腰杆笔挺的人物，他的音容举止本身，就替他信仰的活力做了广告。人人都能看见他大步流星地穿街过巷，一跃跳上正在行驶的电车，又一跃跳下，奔走于一场场委员会会议之间，永远匆忙，却又永远肯放慢脚步同熟人寒暄几句；这几句话欢快、乐观、嗓门洪亮——除非场合需要一种压低嗓音的体恤。会里随便哪位平信徒都能叫他驻足；一位执事就能引他岔开原路；随后他会迈开更快的步子往前赶，把时间补回来——他还常乐呵呵地对这些半路拦截的人说，时间并没有丢，只是在他不知不觉之间已经溜走了。

这些习惯与营生，莫尔小姐（Miss Mole）眼下还无从知晓。从她那间卧室兼起居室的窗口，她望得见礼拜堂的屋顶，起先只把它当作众屋顶与树木之间的一抹暖红。她是看屋顶的行家；凭着上拉德斯托的许多高窗，各种形状、各种颜色的屋顶尽收眼底。它们顺着陡坡一路溜向下拉德斯托（Lower Radstowe），红的、灰的、蓝的、绿的，像一座花园铺满全城。旧红瓦紧挨着亮闪闪的石板瓦，生了青苔的绿屋顶挤在高房子的墙垣之间；高低错落之间，有树梢高过烟囱，也有烟囱把烟送进别人家的窗户——不过这些从汉娜（Hannah）的房间里是看不见的。她望出去的上拉德斯托新城那一带，本也是平平常常的景致，她便一直就着礼拜堂那片屋顶尽量赏玩，直到它忽然变作一个凶兆。她不再喜欢它了，看它的次数却更勤：她想象自己坐在那屋顶底下，成了漆光之间一个小小的灰暗斑点；在等到莉拉（Lilla）来信之前的那几天里，她不时跑到贝雷斯福德路上消磨时光，自觉像个密谋者，又像个私家侦探。她试过进礼拜堂，不出所料，门都锁着。“这种做派，”她对着门廊四周攀爬的常春藤说，“足够把谁都逼得改宗罗马，我都有几分当真想走这一步了。我猜这地方丑得见不得人，除非正赶上他们办游艺会，那也无非是彼此的帽子可瞧。倒不是说，”她补了一句，“他们的帽子能有多少看头。”

若不是成见与预言的本领还能让她自得其乐，她本该十分阴郁。她笃定那红屋顶内里刷的是天青色，同苍穹一样，还洒着一层不透光的金星：她看过科德先生的宅子——十四号，离礼拜堂一箭之遥，就在马路对面——种种不祥的预感于是尽数得到了印证。那是联排双宅之一，一条沥青小径直通那扇毫不起眼的门。上下两层各有一扇凸肚窗、一扇平窗，山墙上另有一扇小窗，像一只眼睛。汉娜曾斗胆打宅前踱过：科德先生随时可能露面，到那时可就难装成一个有权走在这条路上的女人了。她对他本人并无好奇：她惦记的是那房子；只要能把脑袋探进它的门里，她立刻就能知道自己住在里面快活不快活。然而她没有胆量走到那一步，只得满足于观其外观：蕾丝窗帘，月桂镶边的草皮，铁栏围拢——寻不出一丝叫人有盼头的东西。那宅子的建筑师绝非艺术家。它长得丑，可隔壁那栋孪生宅子却显得宜居得多，尽管百叶窗歪歪斜斜。楼下的凸窗上垂着看上去蒙了尘的红窗帘，用黄铜链束着；一只鸟笼里养着金丝雀，疼得汉娜想起了威多斯太太（Mrs. Widdows）。就眼睛而论，十六号同十四号一样不讨人喜欢，可倘若那红窗帘是挂在科德先生家里的，汉娜心里反倒会畅快些。

===Original===

She was startled, the next morning, when she passed again, to hear a harsh voice bidding her good day and, standing on tiptoe to peer over the privet hedge which grew above No. 16’s railings, she saw a parrot in a cage in the middle of the grass plot. The bird leered at her for an instant and then with an insulting expression, pretended it had never seen her, though she offered it the usual complimentary remarks.

“Fond of birds?” asked another voice, and a face popped over the privet hedge, close to her own. “Picking up the dead leaves,” said its owner, showing her a handful, “and giving Poll an airing at the same time. Can’t let him out alone, ’cos of the cats. Even my own cats. Jealousy, I suppose. Now you’ll tell me that a bird is always a bird to a cat﻿—and they’d eat Minnie, that’s the canary, there, if they could get her, I haven’t a doubt, but﻿—and I’ve made a study of this﻿—it’s not food they’re after with Poll. It’s the human voice that upsets them and he’s remarkably chatty at times. The human voice, coming from the wrong place. It’s natural, when you come to think of it.”

Miss Mole had discovered why No. 16 was a fitter habitation for her than No. 14. She recognised something native to herself in this elderly man who could fall into conversation with a stranger, something congenial in his battered old face and his roguish, disrespectful eye. As much as she could see of him was arrayed in a sleeved woollen waistcoat, a high, stiff collar, and a red tie pierced by a pin with a diamond and opal horseshoe head, and he had the look of a man who would wear his cap in the house.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨她再打那里经过，冷不防听见一个刺耳的嗓音向她道日安，吓了一跳；她踮起脚尖，从长过十六号铁栏的女贞树篱上方张望，看见草坪正中一只笼子里立着一只鹦鹉。那鸟斜眼睨了她一瞬，随即摆出一副侮辱人的神气，假装从未见过她——尽管她照例向它奉上了一套恭维话。

“喜欢鸟吗？”另一个声音问道，一张脸从女贞树篱上方探出来，正贴着她的脸。“捡枯叶子呢，”脸的主人给她看满满一把，“顺带放波尔（Poll）出来透透气。不能让它自个儿出来，还不是因为猫。连我自己养的猫也得防。想必是嫉妒。这会儿您准要说了，猫眼里鸟总是鸟——它们要是抓得着米妮（Minnie），准把它一口吞了，这我毫不怀疑，米妮就是那只金丝雀——可是——这个我做过专门研究——它们盯着波尔，图的可不是吃。坏就坏在人的嗓音搅得它们心烦，而它有时候又健谈得惊人。人的声音，偏偏从不对的地方传出来。您细想想，这本是天性使然。”

莫尔小姐这就发现了，为什么十六号比十四号更适合她安身。这位老先生能同陌生人攀谈起来，她在他身上认出了某种同自己一脉相承的东西；他那张饱经风霜的老脸，那双促狭而不恭的眼睛里，也有几分气味相投。她能看见的那一部分他，裹在有袖的毛线背心里，领口是又高又硬的领子，红领带上别着一枚别针，针头是钻石配蛋白石的马蹄铁；瞧那副做派，他就是待在自己屋里也舍不得摘掉帽子。

===Original===

“That’s very interesting,” she said, dropping back on to her heels, while he pressed closer to the hedge to get a better sight of her, and his humorous, rather watery eyes seemed to be comparing her unfavourably with all the fine-looking women they had rested on.

“Saw you yesterday, didn’t I?” he asked. “Shaving at the time, up there,” he jerked a thumb backwards, “and saw a woman at the chapel door. ‘That’s a new thing,’ I said to myself. Couldn’t make it out at all, so I kept my eye on you. Seemed funny to me.”

“It would have been funnier if I’d got in,” Hannah said with a sniff.

===Chinese===

“真有意思，”她说着落回脚跟；他把脸凑得离树篱更近，好看她看得更真，那双诙谐而水汪汪的眼睛，似乎正拿她同它们曾经驻留过的所有漂亮女人一一相较，比来比去，她总是相形见绌。

“昨天不是见过您吗？”他问。“那会儿我正在上头刮脸，”他大拇指朝身后一翘，“瞧见礼拜堂门口站着个女人。我心里念叨：‘这可是桩新鲜事。’全然闹不明白，就盯上了您。我瞧着就觉得稀奇。”

“我要是进去了，才更稀奇呢，”汉娜哼了一下鼻子说。

===Original===

“Ah, not my idea of fun, but if you want to get in,” he jerked his thumb sideways and his tone made large allowances for human vagaries, “you’ll get the key, I dessay, next door. Parson lives there. Went out half an hour ago with his coat tails flying. Pretended he didn’t see me,” he gave Hannah a slow wink, “but I could tell him a thing or two if I liked. Only, as it happens,” he sank out of sight and his voice came muffled through the hedge, “I don’t like.” She could hear him gathering up more leaves.

A farewell seemed unnecessary, to go without one seemed rude, and she murmured something to which he made no response, but he had brightened her outlook; to live next door to a man who could tell Robert Corder a thing or two, to discover what those things were, would be an alleviation of the dreariness she anticipated, and when she opened Lilla’s letter with the news that Mrs. Spenser-Smith had had her way and that Miss Mole would be expected in Beresford Road on the Tuesday of next week, Hannah could think more lightly of her bondage and face the fact that there were not many pounds left in her purse, but she made a wry face at Lilla’s much-underlined conclusion in which she pointed out that with fifty pounds a year, her board and lodging and the rent from her house in the country, Hannah would surely be able to lay something by for a rainy day.

“About enough to buy a cheap umbrella,” Hannah said, flipping the letter across the table before she tore it into little pieces in carefulness that the secret of her relationship to Lilla should be kept.

===Chinese===

“嗐，那可算不得我说的乐子。不过您真想进去嘛，”他大拇指往旁边一翘，那口气对人类的种种古怪一概宽容，“钥匙嘛，我敢说，上隔壁就能讨来。牧师住那儿。半个钟头前刚出去，外套下摆飞得老高。他装着没瞧见我，”他朝汉娜慢悠悠地眨了下眼，“其实我高兴的话，能数落他几桩事。只不过，碰巧，”他缩回树篱后面，声音隔着枝叶闷闷地传出来，“我不高兴。”她听得见他在继续拾枯叶。

道别似乎并无必要，可不道别又显得失礼，她咕哝了一句什么，对方也没应声；可他到底把她的前景照亮了几分：同一位能给罗伯特·科德数落几桩事的男人做邻居，再把那几桩事打听个水落石出，想必能冲淡她预感中的凄清。等到拆开莉拉的来信——信里说斯宾塞-史密斯太太（Mrs. Spenser-Smith）如愿以偿，莫尔小姐下星期二就该到贝雷斯福德路报到了——汉娜对自己这副枷锁倒能看得轻些，也敢于面对钱袋里没剩下几镑的现实了；不过对莉拉在结尾处画满着重线的那番高论，她只报以一脸苦相：莉拉指出，汉娜一年有五十镑进项，管吃管住，乡下那处房子还有租金，想必总能攒下几个钱，以备雨天之需。

“也就够买把便宜伞，”汉娜说着，把信往桌子对面一撩，随即小心翼翼地把它撕成碎片，唯恐她同莉拉关系的秘密有半点闪失。

===Original===

For Mrs. Gibson, the next few days had a noble sadness in them. She was to lose Miss Mole but could not grudge her to the exalted state of being housekeeper to Mr. Corder, and Miss Mole knew she would be welcome at any time if she liked to drop in for a cup of tea. Mrs. Gibson gazed admiringly at this woman who had appeared out of nowhere to save Mr. Ridding’s life, to keep out the policeman and avert an inquest and who betrayed no nervousness at the prospect of living with a minister, a man whose jokes, as Mrs. Gibson recognised, were comparable to passing froth on a pool of unknown depth.

Hannah’s own sadness was shot with a sense of adventure. She was prepared for every kind of dullness and annoyance, she was prepared to be sent adrift again on a world that did not want her, but her belief in approaching good was irrepressible: there was the man next door with his cats and his parrot and his canary and, for all she knew, he might be the one who was to leave her a fortune; there would be the fun of watching Lilla’s secret anxiety and careful condescension, and she was in the place she loved with the chance, if she behaved herself, of finding primroses on the other side of the river, in the spring.

Yet she wished she could have had a little longer with Mrs. Gibson, for Mrs. Ridding was still unfriendly and Mr. Blenkinsop was still the fortified place she had left behind her. She understood the nature of Mrs. Ridding’s defences and respected them, but she itched to tease Mr. Blenkinsop with feints of attack. In daily expectation of another ultimatum, Mrs. Gibson was treating him as though he were seriously ill: she whispered if she encountered Hannah on the landing outside his rooms, took special pains with the cooking of his food and carried it to him herself lest the blundering of the little servant should distress him, and this both angered Hannah and gave her the opportunity she wanted.

===Chinese===

在吉布森太太（Mrs. Gibson）那里，随后几天浸在一种高贵的哀伤之中。她要失去莫尔小姐了，却又不能不舍——人家是去高就，给科德先生当女管家；莫尔小姐也知道，往后只要愿意进来喝杯茶，她随时都受欢迎。吉布森太太满眼钦慕地端详着这个不知从哪儿冒出来的女人：她救了里丁先生（Mr. Ridding）一命，把警察挡在了门外，还消弭了一场死因研讯；如今要同一位牧师同住，她竟不见半点忐忑——而据吉布森太太的识人之明，牧师的玩笑，恰似一泓深浅难测的水面上漂过的一层浮沫。

汉娜自己的哀伤里则交织着冒险的兴味。各式各样的沉闷与烦扰，她都备下了；再被抛上那个不要她的世界、随波漂流，她也备下了；可她对将至之善的信念按捺不住：隔壁有那位先生，带着他的猫、他的鹦鹉、他的金丝雀，说不定，日后给她留下一笔遗产的正是此人呢；看莉拉暗暗提心吊胆、又小心翼翼摆出俯就的姿态，也是一桩乐趣；何况她如今身在心爱之地，只要自己行为检点，开春兴许还能在河那边寻着几朵报春花呢。

可她还是巴望能同吉布森太太多盘桓些时日，因为里丁太太（Mrs. Ridding）依旧不友善，布伦金索普先生（Mr. Blenkinsop）也仍旧是她抛在身后的一座设防要塞。里丁太太的防线是什么性质，她心里明白，也予以尊重；布伦金索普先生那里，她却手痒，想用几路佯攻去撩拨撩拨他。吉布森太太天天提防着又一道最后通牒，便把他当重病人一般伺候：在房门外楼梯平台上碰见汉娜就压低嗓音，给他做饭格外用心，还亲自把饭菜端上楼去，生怕那个笨手笨脚的小女佣搅扰了他——这既叫汉娜来气，也正给了她求之不得的机会。

===Original===

On her last evening, she went into the kitchen and picked up the tray.

“He won’t like it!” Mrs. Gibson gasped.

“He’ll have to lump it,” Hannah said vulgarly. “What about your poor legs, as you call them?”

===Chinese===

最后一个晚上，她走进厨房，提起了托盘。

“他不会乐意的！”吉布森太太倒抽一口冷气。

“他不乐意也得受着，”汉娜的口气有失斯文，“您那两条可怜的腿呢——不是您自己说的吗？”

===Original===

There were no limits to Miss Mole’s audacity: Mrs. Gibson could not cope with it and she looked at Hannah with the mournful, helpless interest she had once experienced when she saw a man go into a cage of lions.

Mr. Blenkinsop was sitting by the fire in a large sitting-room heavily furnished with his mother’s mahogany. In front of him was a chessboard on a stool and his hand was poised above one of the pieces. He did not look up and Hannah felt as if she had carelessly entered a church while a service was in progress. The proper thing was to slip away and trust the appetising smell of cooked meats to creep through Mr. Blenkinsop’s absorption, but, instead of doing that, she said crisply, “Dinner is served, sir!” and taking a step forward, she added, “So that’s what you do in the evenings! It must be a great resource.”

Mr. Blenkinsop looked astonished and then frowned. “It needs concentration,” he said pointedly.

===Chinese===

莫尔小姐的胆大妄为没有边际：吉布森太太全然招架不住，只带着一种哀婉而无可奈何的兴趣望着汉娜，就像当年看见一条大汉径直走进狮子笼时一样。

布伦金索普先生坐在炉火旁，待在一间宽大的起居室里，满屋沉甸甸地摆着他母亲的桃花心木家具。他面前的凳子上摆着一副棋盘，一只手悬停在一枚棋子上方。他头也不抬，汉娜只觉得自己像是冒冒失失闯进了一座正在行礼拜的教堂。识相的做法是悄悄退下，听凭烧好的肉食香气丝丝渗入布伦金索普先生的出神之中；她偏不，反而清脆利落地说：“晚饭伺候好了，先生！”又上前一步，补上一句：“敢情您晚上的营生就是这个！这准是桩顶管用的排遣。”

布伦金索普先生先是愕然，继而皱起眉头。“下这棋，得专心。”他话里带刺地说。

===Original===

“That’s what I mean,” Hannah replied obtusely. “I’ve brought up your dinner because Mrs. Gibson’s legs ache.”

“There’s no reason why Mrs. Gibson should do it.”

“Fear,” said Hannah, “is one of the strongest human emotions.”

===Chinese===

“我说的就是专心，”汉娜存心装糊涂，“吉布森太太腿疼，晚饭是我给您端上来的。”

“没有道理非得吉布森太太来端。”

“恐惧嘛，”汉娜说，“是人类最强烈的情感之一。”

===Original===

“I’m afraid I don’t follow you,” Mr. Blenkinsop said with marked politeness.

“The poor dear is afraid of losing you.”

“She knows how to keep me.” Mr. Blenkinsop took his seat at the table and unfolded his napkin. “And really,” he went on, indignation mastering courtesy, “I don’t quite understand why you should interest yourself in the question.”

===Chinese===

“恐怕我没听懂您的意思，”布伦金索普先生答道，客气得扎眼。

“那位可怜的宝贝儿，是怕失了您哪。”

“她知道怎么拴得住我。”布伦金索普先生在桌边落座，展开餐巾。“再说实话，”他接着说，愤慨压过了客气，“我实在不明白，这个问题干您什么事，值得您操心。”

===Original===

“No, you don’t understand,” Hannah said gently. “And I make no apology. I’m speaking, as it were, from my deathbed. *Moriturus te saluto!* Tomorrow, you’ll be glad to know, I’m moving on. I’m going to live with Mr. Corder﻿—as his housekeeper﻿—oh Lord!” A faint gleam of interest passed across Mr. Blenkinsop’s face and she took advantage of it. “Yes, think of that!” she cried, “I’d rather live with the Riddings. Why don’t you teach Mr. Ridding to play chess? That would keep him out of the oven! And what an inconvenience for you to find new lodgings! And Mrs. Gibson’s heart will break! Stay where you are, Mr. Blenkinsop, and think of me tomorrow at this time, when you’re here in your comfortable room and I’m in a strange land. But perhaps I shall see you sometimes at the chapel. That will cheer me up.”

“Not very likely,” Mr. Blenkinsop said, firmly nipping this bud of hope, and he applied himself to his dinner with an unmistakable air of dismissal.

===Chinese===

“对，您不明白，”汉娜温声说，“我也无意道歉。我这是——可以这么说——躺在临终的病榻上同您讲话。*Moriturus te saluto!*——将死之人，向您致敬！明天，您听了准高兴，我就要挪窝了。我要搬去同科德先生住——做他的女管家——天哪！”一缕浅淡的兴味掠过布伦金索普先生的脸，她当即乘虚而入。“是啊，您想想看！”她嚷道，“我宁可去里丁家住。您干吗不教教里丁先生下棋呢？那就叫他腾不出工夫往烤箱跟前凑了！您要另觅寓所，麻烦还小得了吗！吉布森太太的心都要碎了！您就稳坐原地别动，布伦金索普先生，明天这个时辰想一想我：您坐在这间舒服的屋子里，我却流落他乡。不过，礼拜堂里我说不定还能时常碰见您。那也能叫我打起精神来。”

“那可未必，”布伦金索普先生说着，把这点希望的蓓蕾掐了个干净，随即埋头吃起饭来，那股下逐客令的意思再明白不过。

## VII / 第七章

===Original===
Mrs. Gibson’s greengrocer undertook to deliver Miss Mole’s box at the house in Beresford Road and thither, in the dusk, Miss Mole walked, following the cart and feeling that this was her own funeral and she the solitary mourner. Slowly the cart creaked down the road and slowly Miss Mole walked after it, and it seemed to her that her old trunk was her coffin and that the solitary mourner was her own ghost. A little wind was driving the fallen leaves along the pavement, there was a rustling of the bushes in the gardens, the tired pony’s steps and the turning of the wheels were dismal sounds, and she wished she had gone to the expense of hiring a cab and arriving with some appearance of eagerness. This was a very melancholy procession, a detachment of an army of women like herself who went from house to house behind their boxes, a sad multitude of women with carefully pleasant faces, hiding their ailments, lowering their ages and thankfully accepting less than they earned. What became of them all? What was to become of herself? Age was creeping on her all the time and she had saved nothing, she would soon be told she was too old for this post or that, and, for a second, fear took hold of her with a cold hand and the whispering of the dead leaves warned her that, like them, she would be swept into the gutter and no one would ask where she had gone, and her fear changed into a craving that there should be at least one person to whom her disappearance would be a calamity. “No one!” the leaves whispered maliciously, while a little gust of laughter came from the bushes, and at that, Hannah paused and looked disdainfully in their direction. She was not to be laughed at! She was not to be laughed at and she refused to be frightened! Her head went up and she walked more quickly, to get level with the cart, and the ruby glow in No. 16’s bow window applauded her spirit. It was pleasant to think he was in there by the fire with the parrot and the canary and the cats. He would be surprised when he saw her flicking a duster from a window of No. 14, and Hannah liked surprising people. This was something to look forward to and she was not depressed when she saw no welcoming lights shining from Mr. Corder’s house.

The greengrocer shouldered the box, Hannah followed him up the asphalt path and rang the bell. There was a glimmer of light in the hall, but no one could be heard stirring in the house.

“Seems as if they’re all out,” the greengrocer said, and whistled under his breath.

===Chinese===
吉布森太太（Mrs. Gibson）的蔬果店老板应承下这桩差事：把莫尔小姐（Miss Mole）的衣箱送到贝雷斯福德路（Beresford Road）那所宅子去。暮色四合，莫尔小姐跟在货车后头朝那儿走，心里觉得这分明是自己的送葬行列，而她就是那唯一的送葬人。货车吱吱呀呀，沿马路慢慢走，莫尔小姐也慢慢地跟在后头；她恍恍惚惚觉得，那只旧衣箱便是她的棺材，那唯一的送葬人则是她自己的魂灵。一阵小风卷着落叶沿人行道翻滚，园中灌木窸窣作响，疲马的四蹄声和车轮的转动声听来凄凄惶惶；她懊悔不曾破费雇一辆马车，好歹也能带着几分急切的样子到场。这支队伍实在愁惨——这是一支妇女大军分遣出来的小队，尽是些同她一样的女人，拖着自己的箱子一家一家地投奔；一大群可悲的女人，脸上小心经营着和悦，掩着病痛，把年纪往小里报，感激不尽地领着低于辛苦应得的工钱。她们这些人后来都怎么样了？她自己，又会有怎样的下场？年岁一刻不停地悄悄爬上她身来，她却分文未存；用不了多久，就会有人对她说，这个差事她年纪太大，那个差事也年纪太大——刹那间，恐惧伸出一只冰冷的手攫住了她，枯叶窸窣的低语警诫她：她也将同这些叶子一样，被扫进阴沟，没有人会问一声她去了哪里。恐惧随即化作一种渴念：但愿世上至少有一个人，她的消失于他会是一场灾祸。“没有谁！”叶子们恶毒地低语，灌木丛里同时滚出一小阵风似的嗤笑；听见这个，汉娜停住脚，朝那一边轻蔑地望了过去。她不是让人笑话的！她不是让人笑话的，她也决计不肯给吓住！她昂起头来，脚步加快，赶到与货车并齐的地方；十六号凸肚窗里那团红宝石色的火光，正为她的精神喝彩。想到他就待在那里面，守着炉火，身边有鹦鹉、金丝雀和猫儿们，实在是桩乐事。等他看见她从十四号的窗口里抖开一块掸布，他准会吃一惊，而汉娜素来喜欢叫人吃惊。这总算是一桩可盼的事；所以望见科德先生（Mr. Corder）的宅子没有亮出半点迎客的灯火，她也不曾气馁。

蔬果店老板扛起衣箱，汉娜跟着他走上沥青小径，按响了门铃。门厅里透出一线微光，宅子里却听不见有人走动的动静。

“看样子他们全出门了，”蔬果店老板说，低低地打了个呼哨。

===Original===
Hannah rang the bell more loudly, the greengrocer cocked his head with polite attention, and now they heard the sound of someone running down the stairs.

Half an hour later, when Hannah was kneeling in front of her box, she fancied she could still hear that sound. It had struck her ear, at her first hearing of it, with a strange significance, as though Fate itself were coming to let her in, yet when the door was opened it revealed no more than the figure of a small, thin girl who did not know how to greet a stranger or to apologize for the necessary absence of her elders and the neglect of the servant who should have answered the door.

Hannah made a mental note about that servant and another that this girl, who must be the one who had holes in her stockings, was not at all pleased to see her, and she answered reserve with reserve, but when the greengrocer had struggled upstairs with the trunk and Ruth had found a box of matches and lighted the gas in Miss Mole’s attic bedroom and, doing her best, had gone to the window to pull down the blind, Hannah forgot to be dignified and called out, “Oh, don’t do that! I want to look. It faces nearly south, doesn’t it?”

===Chinese===
汉娜把门铃按得更响；蔬果店老板客客气气地偏过头去细听；这一回，他们听见有人奔下楼梯的声响了。

半个钟头以后，汉娜跪在衣箱跟前，恍惚还听得见那个声音。头一耳听见它时，它带着一种奇异的意味撞进她的耳朵，仿佛命运本人正赶来为她开门；可门一开，现出的不过是一个瘦小姑娘的身影——她既不知道该怎样招呼一个陌生人，也不懂得为长辈们难免的外出、为那个本该应门的女仆的失职道一声歉。

汉娜在心里给那个女仆记下了一笔，又记下另一笔：这个姑娘准是长袜破洞的那一个，她见着新来的人丝毫没有欢喜的意思；汉娜便拿自己的矜持去还敬那份矜持。可等蔬果店老板吭哧吭哧把衣箱扛上楼、露丝（Ruth）摸出一盒火柴点着了莫尔小姐那间阁楼卧室的煤气灯、又好心好意走到窗前要放下帘子的时候，汉娜把体面忘得干干净净，嚷出声来：“哦，先别放！我要看看呢。这窗差不多朝正南，是不是？”

===Original===
It was a dormer window, the one she had seen from the road, and she felt like a pigeon peering from a hole in a dovecot. The house was set higher than she had thought and, over the opposite roofs, she could see thousands of twinkling lights and the dim outline of more roofs and chimneys. And this was a view which would be no less lovely in the morning, when the spires and towers of Radstowe’s innumerable churches and the factory chimneys with their pennons of smoke would clear themselves from the widespread huddle of buildings. She turned her head a little to the right and the wind came straight across the hills from the place where her pink cottage stood in its little orchard, and it was characteristic of Hannah to accept the pleasure and ignore the pain of the wind’s reminders, to overpay the greengrocer and to smile as she told Ruth she was going to unpack.

Cheerfully she looked about her when she was left alone and she decided that she liked this narrow room with its sloping walls, and then, with the wariness of an old campaigner, she examined the blankets, which were clean, and the sheets, which were rather coarse, and thumped the mattress critically.

“Lumpy,” she said, frowning a little. But never mind! She had the view from the window, she thought she would be able to hear the ships hooting up and down the river, and not far away there was the real Upper Radstowe with its old streets and crescents, its odd passages and flights of steps, and she unstrapped her box, forgetting she had seen it as a coffin.

===Chinese===
那是一扇老虎窗，就是她打大路上望见的那一扇；她觉得自己活像鸽棚洞口探头张望的一只鸽子。宅子的地势比她料想的更高，越过对面层层叠叠的屋顶，她望得见千万点闪烁的灯火，望得见更远处屋顶与烟囱的朦胧轮廓。这景致到了早晨也断不会减色：拉德斯托（Radstowe）数不清的教堂的尖塔与钟楼，还有顶着燕尾旗般烟缕的工厂烟囱，都将从大片挤挤挨挨的屋海中一一显豁出来。她把头稍稍偏向右边，风便打山丘上笔直地吹过来——她那栋粉色小屋守着它的一小片果园，正立在那山丘上。汉娜的一贯做派正是如此：领受风的提醒里的欢愉，无视它的苦涩；给蔬果店老板多付工钱；又笑吟吟地对露丝说，她要开箱整理了。

屋里只剩她一个人的时候，她兴致勃勃地打量四下，认定自己喜欢这间墙壁倾斜的窄屋；跟着，又拿出身经百战的老手的戒心，查验了毯子——还算干净；验了床单——粗了些；末了，挑剔地在床垫上捶了几下。

“净是疙瘩，”她说着，微微皱了皱眉。不过不要紧！她有这扇窗的景致；她想，往后她还听得见轮船在河上呜呜地往返；不远处便是真正的上拉德斯托（Upper Radstowe），有它的老街与弧形排屋，古里古怪的过道和一级级的台阶；她解开箱子上的皮带，忘了自己方才还把它看作棺材。

===Original===
The size of the box erroneously suggested that Miss Mole’s wardrobe was extensive. There was still plenty of room in drawers and cupboards when she had laid out and hung up her clothes, and there was still a good deal left in the box, for Miss Mole’s treasures travelled with her and the chief of these was the model of a sailing ship miraculously enclosed in a pale green bottle. She took it from its wrapping of cotton wool and gazed at it tenderly when she had put it on the narrow mantelpiece. She liked to see it sailing all alone, never getting any further and never losing its gallantness, and it brought back memories of her very early childhood when it stood on the parlour mantelpiece, far out of her reach, a mystery in itself and a hint of greater mysteries. It was connected with the few sights, sounds and smells that remained to her of those days; heavy bees buzzing among the pinks on a hot afternoon, a turn in the garden path where danger lurked behind the hedge of box, the crackling of her starched pinafore, the rattling of milk pails and the creaking of her father’s corded breeches.

She had a lot to be thankful for, she thought. It was good to have such clean country memories and it was astonishing how solid a background they made to life. Consciously or unconsciously, they were there, and, however murky and sordid some of her experiences had been, her roots were in wholesome earth and she had sprouted among sweet-smelling things. No one loved streets better than Hannah Mole, but she had a secret satisfaction in her knowledge of matters on which these townspeople depended and which they took for granted, and it gave her a feeling of permanency, of something more real than anything else in her restless flittings about the world and her changing views of what she was or might have been.

She had finished dressing herself for the latest part she had undertaken when a knock came at the door and the thin little girl appeared again, evidently unwilling to be the messenger who summoned Miss Mole to supper. She was a little breathless, but whether with nervousness, indignation or the ascent of the stairs, Hannah did not know.

===Chinese===
箱子的个头叫人错以为莫尔小姐的行头极其可观。衣裳取出安放、悬挂停当，抽屉和柜橱竟还余出许多空处；箱子里剩下的东西却也还着实不少，因为莫尔小姐的宝贝一向随身云游，而宝贝中的头一名，是一艘奇迹般封在淡绿瓶子里的帆船模型。她把它从裹着的棉絮里取出来，安在窄窄的壁炉台上，含着温存端详。她爱看它独自远航，永远到不了更远的地方，却永远不失那股昂扬的英气；它勾回她最最早的童年记忆：那时它立在堂屋的壁炉台上，高得她再踮脚也够不着，本身就是一桩神秘，又是更大的神秘的一点暗示。那些年月留给她的零零星星几样光景、声响与气味，都同它连在一处：炎热的午后，笨重的蜜蜂在石竹花丛里嗡嗡；园径的一个转弯，黄杨篱背后潜伏着危险；浆过的围裙窸窣发脆；奶桶哐啷作响，父亲那条棱纹马裤咯吱咯吱。

她想，她该感谢的实在不少。有这样干净的乡土记忆是桩好事；它们给人生铺出的底子何等厚实，简直叫人吃惊。无论自觉与否，它们始终在那里；纵使她的某些经历何等晦暗腌臜，她的根总扎在肥沃干净的泥土里，她的芽总发在馨香的草木之间。没有人比汉娜·莫尔更爱街道，可这些城里人赖以度日、又视作理所当然的种种营生，她无不通晓，这给她一种隐秘的快意，也给她一种恒久之感：在她辗转不宁地迁徙人间、在她对自己是什么或本可能是什么的层层变卦的看法之外，总还有一样比什么都真切的东西在。

她为最新接下的这个角色穿戴完毕，门上便响起了叩门声，那个瘦小的姑娘又出现了，显然极不情愿充当那个请莫尔小姐下楼用晚饭的报信人。她微微气喘；可这气喘是紧张、是委屈、还是爬楼梯爬出来的，汉娜就不得而知了。

===Original===
“She wants feeding up,” she thought, as she smiled with determined brightness, and she promised herself that the day would come when this child would be glad of an excuse to knock at Miss Mole’s door. Even now her eyes had lighted on the bottled ship with a quickly hidden interest, and of that, too, Hannah made a mental note.

Her bedroom had been a pleasant surprise; the rest of the house was what she had known it must be. The hall smelt faintly of the morning’s cooking, the gas was protected by a lantern of red and blue glass, and though, when she entered the dining-room, she missed the green serge cover on a table which was laid for supper, the rusty fern was there, under a three-armed chandelier. One of these arms was fitted for incandescent gas which bubbled inside a frosted globe; the others were neglected and stood out gauntly like withered branches on a tree, but the room was further illuminated﻿—though it still seemed rather dark﻿—by an ordinary gas jet on each side of the fireplace, and these flames gently hissed in their globes of pink and white.

She noticed these things with a glance of her practised eye, and she had no time to verify her suspicion that there was cold mutton on the table before her hand was seized by a young woman who pranced to meet her.

===Chinese===
“这孩子得好好补养。”她一面想，一面堆起强打精神的明快笑容，又向自己许下心愿：总有一天，这孩子会巴不得找个借口来敲莫尔小姐的门。就连方才，她的眼睛也在那艘瓶中船上落过一瞬，那点兴趣刚露头就急忙藏起；这一笔，汉娜也照样记下了。

她的卧室是一桩可喜的意外；宅子其余的部分，则同她早料定的模样分毫不差。门厅里隐隐有一股上午做饭的气味，煤气灯外面护着红蓝玻璃的灯笼罩；走进餐室，摆好晚饭的桌上不见了那块绿哔叽台布，她心下怅然，那盆锈暗的小蕨草倒还在，立在三臂吊灯底下。一条灯臂装着白热煤气灯嘴，在磨砂玻璃罩里咕咕作响；其余两条无人理会，枯瘦地支棱出来，像树上的焦枝；屋里另有添亮之处——虽说瞧着还是相当暗——壁炉两侧各有一盏寻常煤气灯，两簇火苗在粉的、白的玻璃罩里轻轻地嘶嘶。

这些情形，她那训练有素的眼睛一瞥便尽收眼底；她疑心桌上是冷羊肉，还没来得及证实，一只手已被一个连蹦带跳迎上来的年轻女人一把攥住了。

===Original===
In her nervous or happy moments, prancing was Ethel Corder’s gait and, indeed, she reminded Hannah of an awkward-tempered colt. There was a display of teeth and eyeballs, a look of half-playful viciousness for which her physical peculiarities were chiefly responsible. Her pale, scanty hair started growing too far back and there was a deficiency of eyebrow, yet her plainness had a certain feverish quality which attracted and held the attention.

“Someone’s been knocking her about in the stable or stealing her oats,” Hannah thought, while Ethel volubly explained that an emergency committee meeting had engaged her father and herself. “I shall have to be careful. And the little one looks like a starved donkey. It’s lucky I was brought up on a farm.” She wanted to stroke and reassure them both, to tell them she could make them plump and happy if they would trust her. She wanted to stop the gas from bubbling like a turkey and hissing like a pair of geese. She saw there was plenty here for a farmer’s daughter to do, and though this might not be the place Hannah Mole would have chosen for herself, it was the one that needed Hannah, and she was turning the cold mutton into a savoury hash for tomorrow’s dinner and getting rid of the rusty fern, when the voice which had cheered many a sewing-meeting bade her good evening.

“So this is Miss Mole,” he said, nicely fitting his tone to the one suitable for an emissary of Mrs. Spenser-Smith who was far from being Mrs. Spenser-Smith herself, and Hannah, prejudiced already, thought she saw him quickly appraising her as a useful nobody.

===Chinese===
紧张或是高兴的时刻，埃塞尔·科德（Ethel Corder）的步态便是这样一路腾跃；说真的，她让汉娜想起一匹脾气别扭的小马驹。她一露面便牙齿与眼白齐现，带着半是戏耍的凶相——这多半是她那副异相招来的。浅色的头发又稀又少，发际生得太靠后，眉毛也欠奉；可她的不好看里自有一种燥热的质地，摄住人的注意，不放。

“有人在马厩里揍过它，要不就是偷了它的燕麦。”埃塞尔滔滔不绝地解释说，一桩紧急的委员会会务把她父亲和她自己都缠住了；汉娜心里却这样想。“我得小心着点。那只小的瞧着像头饿瘪了的小毛驴。幸亏我是在农场上长大的。”她只想抚摸这两个、安抚这两个，想告诉她们：只要肯信她，她准能把她们喂得丰润、哄得快活。她还想叫煤气灯住口，别再像一只火鸡似的咕咕、像一对鹅似的嘶嘶。她看明白了，这里有的是让一个农场女儿施展的活计；这地方纵然不是汉娜·莫尔自己会挑的，却偏偏是用得着汉娜的地方。她心里正把那冷羊肉翻制成明天正餐吃的一锅香喷喷的肉丁烩，正要把那盆锈暗的蕨草请出去，忽然，那个曾在多少次缝纫会（sewing-meeting）上鼓舞过众人的嗓音，向她道了一声晚上好。

“哦，这就是莫尔小姐了，”他说。语调拿捏得恰到好处，正是款待斯宾塞-史密斯太太（Mrs. Spenser-Smith）差来的信使合该有的那一种——而这位信使，离斯宾塞-史密斯太太本人可差着十万八千里；汉娜成见在先，自觉看得分明：他正飞快地给她估价，估她是个有用的无名之辈。

===Original===
With the best will in the world it was impossible to think the same of him. His height, his handsome head of dark chestnut hair just flecked with grey, the pointed beard of a lighter colour, his suggestion of great physical energy, dominated the room, and with a stiffened back, but a crestfallen spirit, Hannah had to admit that Mr. Corder, too, was something of a surprise.

Meekly, she took her seat opposite to Ruth, the servant brought in a dish of damp potatoes, and Mr. Corder took up the carving knife and fork. Ethel had fallen silent and Ruth seemed determined not to speak. She looked crossly at the mutton on her plate and at Miss Mole on the other side of the table, but she bent industriously over her food when her father began to talk.

“This is a fine old city, Miss Mole,” he said, “full of historic associations, and we have one of the finest parish churches in the country﻿—if you are interested in architecture,” he added, with a subtle suggestion that this was not likely.

===Chinese===
纵有天大的善意，也没法把他作同样的想头。他的身量，他那颗漂亮的脑袋——深栗色的头发刚缀上几星灰白——那绺颜色淡些的尖须，他那副浑身力气的气象，一进屋便把四下压住了。汉娜背脊越挺越直，心里却败下阵来，只得承认：科德先生也叫她吃了一惊。

她低眉顺眼地在露丝对面坐下来；女仆端进一盘潮乎乎的土豆；科德先生拿起切肉刀叉。埃塞尔没了声息，露丝像打定了主意一个字也不说。她气鼓鼓地瞪着盘子里的羊肉，又瞪着桌子对面的莫尔小姐；可她父亲一开腔，她便埋头苦吃起来。

“这是座很好的古城，莫尔小姐，”他说，“尽是历史的掌故，我们还拥有全国顶好的教区教堂之一——如果您对建筑感兴趣的话，”他又补上一句，话音里含着一丝微妙的意思：多半是不感兴趣的。

===Original===
Hannah longed to ask what effect her indifference would have on the building, but Mr. Corder did not wait for reassurance about its safety.

“Ruth must take you to see it, some day. On a Saturday afternoon, perhaps Ruthie?”

“I play hockey on Saturday afternoons,” Ruth muttered.

===Chinese===
汉娜真想问上一句：她的不感兴趣，会对那座建筑起什么效用；可科德先生并不等她担保教堂的平安，径自说了下去。

“改天总得叫露丝带您去看看。挑个星期六下午吧，好不好，小露丝？”

“我星期六下午打曲棍球，”露丝咕哝说。

===Original===
“Ah, yes, of course, these games!” Mr. Corder said good-humouredly. “Well, Miss Mole may find her way there herself. The Cathedral is not so good. I don’t care about the Cathedral, but we are rather proud of our Chapter House. It may surprise you﻿—.” He interrupted himself. “By the way, what has happened to Wilfrid? Wilfrid is my nephew and he is supposed to be studying medicine at the University,” he told Hannah. “Do you know where he is, Ethel?”

Ethel’s eyes goggled nervously. “He had an engagement,” she said in haste, and Hannah suspected that Ruth’s smile had been calculated to make her sister exclaim angrily, “It’s perfectly true, Ruth! He told me about it yesterday.”

“And I knew he’d have it a week ago,” Ruth retorted coolly, and Hannah realised that this rude little girl was hinting that her cousin had taken care to avoid Miss Mole’s first evening.

===Chinese===
“啊，是啊，当然了，这些游戏嘛！”科德先生好脾气地说。“那么，莫尔小姐自己寻得着去的路。大教堂么，就不大行了。大教堂我不大喜欢，可我们颇为自己的牧师会礼堂（Chapter House）得意。这一层也许会让您吃惊——”他忽然收住口。“对了，威尔弗里德（Wilfrid）这是怎么了？威尔弗里德是我外甥，本该在大学念医学的，”他对汉娜说。“你知道他在哪儿么，埃塞尔？”

埃塞尔的眼睛慌得滴溜溜转。“他有约会，”她急急地说；汉娜疑心，露丝那一下笑是精心算计过的，为的就是撩得妹妹气冲冲嚷出声来：“这事千真万确，露丝！他昨天亲口告诉我的。”

“我一礼拜前就料到他有约会，”露丝冷冷回敬。汉娜于是明白：这个无礼的小丫头是在暗示，她表哥特意避开了莫尔小姐进门的头一个晚上。

===Original===
Robert Corder lifted his eyebrows and managed to look bland as he said, “I think Ruth shares my opinion about the genuineness of Wilfrid’s engagements. However, we need not waste our time over Wilfrid. I was saying, Miss Mole, that you may be surprised at my interest in ecclesiastical architecture, but whatever our religious differences may be, these buildings are a common heritage, and when I first came to Radstowe, fifteen years ago, I made a point of seeing everything of interest or importance, and very valuable the knowledge has been to me. I have been able to wake a civic pride in a great many people, I believe, but not, I’m afraid,” he said playfully, “in my own children. You know the saying about the prophet! I’m pretty sure Ruth has never been inside St. Mary’s﻿—eh, Ruthie?”

Ruth flushed an angry red and said she hated churches.

“Ruth is a stout Nonconformist, Miss Mole, but we mustn’t be narrow. And there are other beauties in Radstowe. ‘A thing of beauty is a joy forever.’ You recognise that, Ruth, Ethel? And there are other interests. Radstowe was once the most important port in England, but with the increase in tonnage she has lost her place. The big ships can’t get up the river. It is a tidal river﻿—most picturesque in its gorge﻿—you must be sure to see that, too﻿—but the channel is very narrow, with large deposits of mud.” And at some length and with what accuracy Hannah could not judge, he explained how these deposits were made. “Dredging goes on, but does very little good. It is a great misfortune for our trade.”

===Chinese===
罗伯特·科德（Robert Corder）扬起双眉，竭力作出一团和气：“我想，关于威尔弗里德那些约会的真假，露丝跟我的看法一致。不过，威尔弗里德不值得咱们浪费工夫。我方才说，莫尔小姐，您也许会奇怪我竟对教会建筑这样有兴趣；可无论我们宗教上的分歧多大，这些建筑总归是共同的遗产。十五年前我初到拉德斯托，凡有意思、有分量的东西，我都特意看过一遍；这份见识后来对我大有益处。我相信，我唤起过许许多多人的市民自豪感；只怕，”他打趣地说，“自家的儿女不在其列。先知在自己家乡不吃香的那句老话，您总知道的！我敢断定，露丝压根儿没进过圣玛丽教堂（St. Mary’s）——是不是，小露丝？”

露丝气得满脸通红，说她恨透了教堂。

“露丝是个坚定的非国教徒，莫尔小姐，可咱们不能心胸狭窄。拉德斯托另有别样的美。‘美的事物是永恒的喜悦。’认得这一句吧，露丝，埃塞尔？另外还有些别的兴致。拉德斯托从前是英格兰数一数二的要港，可随着船只吨位增大，它便把地位丢了。大船开不上河来。那是一条潮汐河——峡谷一段最富画意——那个您也千万得去看看——只是河道很窄，泥淤得很厚。”他絮絮地讲了足足好一阵，讲这些淤泥是怎样淤成的；讲得对不对，汉娜无从判断。“疏浚一直在做，可是收效甚微。这对我们的商业是一大不幸。”

===Original===
“Yes,” said Hannah, deciding to be dumb no longer as she could not be deaf, “but it’s well worth it. At low tide the mud is beautiful. All the colours of the rainbow, and it makes such a nice promenade for the gulls.”
