They had nudged and giggled and she had not said a word. She ought to have called out to Miss Mole and stopped her, but she had been afraid of ridicule. She had not only committed a disloyal act, but one which might be discovered, and in that bitter moment she learnt that secret sin could be forgotten, while sin revealed to the world could be remembered forever. All she could do now was to hurry and wipe away some of the stain.

Going home was not quite as bad as it had been for the last two years, she was not so anxious to linger in the streets, her habit of running past the next-door house and entering her own with a rush, was an old one, and Miss Mole, lighting the hall gas, showed no surprise at Ruth’s breathless entrance, though her sharply benevolent eyes may have seen more than the dampness of Ruth’s clothes as she said briskly, “Don’t stand about in your coat. And you’ll change your stockings, won’t you?”

“What about you, Miss Mole?” Ruth said faintly. “You’re wet, too.”

===Chinese===

那身影过去了；露丝觉得左右两肋各挨了一记轻捅；有人吃吃地笑，露丝照旧喋喋不休；可等同女伴们分了手，她拔腿就跑，从可怜巴巴张开的嘴里倒抽着粗气。她像极了彼得（Peter）：她不认自己的朋友。那些女生倘若哪天再见到那件大衣、认出穿它的正是莫尔小姐，会把露丝看作什么人？她们又是捅她又是哂笑，她却一言未发。她本该唤住莫尔小姐、拦下她，可她怕人笑话。她不但干了不忠的事，还干了件可能败露的事；在那个苦涩的时刻她悟出来：隐秘的罪可以被人忘却，昭示于世的罪却要被记到永远。如今她唯一能做的，就是赶紧跑，把那污点揩去几分。

回家这件事，已不像过去两年那样难捱了；她不再那样一心想在街上磨蹭。跑过隔壁那家门前、一阵风似的冲进自家门，原是她的老习惯；莫尔小姐点着门厅的煤气灯，对露丝气咻咻的进门并不显出惊讶，只是利利落落地说：“别穿着外衣杵在这儿。袜子是要换的，对不对？”——虽然她那双锐利而慈和的眼睛，看见的恐怕不止露丝衣裳上的潮气。

“那您呢，莫尔小姐？”露丝有气无力地说，“您也湿了。”

===Original===

Miss Mole patted the abominable ulster. “It can’t get through this. Do go and change or you’ll have a cold and it seems a pity to waste one when there isn’t a party to dodge.”

Ruth’s smile was wan. Miss Mole, revealing herself as a person of humour and understanding, was simply making things worse for her. She went towards the stairs where there was more shadow. “Did you come across Regent Square?”

“Yes. I’d been for a walk round the hill to look at the river. Lovely it was, too, in the rain. The mist was thick on the water and there was a tree on the other side like a torch blazing through a fog. But the leaves will be dropping under this rain.”

===Chinese===

莫尔小姐拍了拍那件可憎的大衣。“水透不进它去。快去换吧，不然要着凉的；再说，又没有宴会可躲，白白糟蹋一场感冒，多可惜。”

露丝的微笑惨白。莫尔小姐这样显露她的幽默与体贴，反倒叫她心里更难受。她朝楼梯那边走去，那边的阴影更浓。“您经过摄政广场（Regent Square）了吗？”

“经过了。我绕着山冈散步去了，看看河。雨里头也真好看。水上雾气很浓，对岸有一棵树，像一支穿透浓雾燃烧的火把。不过这场雨一下，叶子该落了。”

===Original===

For a moment, Ruth was held from her purpose. She wished Miss Mole would go on talking like that. She said things differently, in Ruth’s experience, from other people and in a different voice. She had spoken of a moony night, and beauty and peace had stolen over her listener, and now Ruth felt the chill danger of thick mist and the joy of seeing light ahead. The necessity for confession seemed less pressing, the world of vision became of more importance than the one of facts, and while she was persuading herself that it would be easy and kinder to be silent, she began to speak.

“Miss Mole,” she said, “I think I saw you. I mean﻿—I saw you. But you went past so quickly and I was too late. You know how things happen. I ought to have shouted to you at once, but it would have looked so funny to the other girls if I’d recognised you when you were nearly out of sight, so I didn’t﻿—but I feel so mean.”

“Mean?” said Hannah. “I’m grateful. When I’m in this ulster, I’m supposed to be invisible. I know what it’s like. I ought not to wear it at all, but it’s thick and it’s an old friend. If you’d stopped me I should have died of shame. Thank goodness you didn’t. I won’t run the risk again, except in the dark. Now will you kindly go and change those clothes? You ought to have worn your mackintosh. It’s high-tea night and when I was up on the hill I suddenly thought of mushrooms and I bought some on the way home. We’ll have them with scrambled eggs and you can come and help me if you like.”

===Chinese===

有那么一刻，露丝被岔开了心事。她盼着莫尔小姐就这样一直说下去。照露丝的经验，她说起事情来同旁人不一样，连嗓音也不一样。她说起过一个有月亮的夜晚，听的人不由得漫上一层美与安宁；此刻露丝感到浓雾里彻骨的凶险，又感到望见前方有光的欢喜。招认的必要似乎不那么急迫了，幻象的世界变得比事实的世界更要紧；她正劝着自己——不开口既容易也更仁慈——话却已经出了口。

“莫尔小姐，”她说，“我好像看见您了。我是说——我看见您了。可您走过去那么快，我来不及了。您知道事情是怎么发生的。我本该立刻喊您才对，可您眼看就要望不见了，我才认出来，在别的女生看来要多滑稽有多滑稽，所以我没有——可我觉得自己太卑劣了。”

“卑劣？”汉娜说，“我还要谢你呢。穿上这件大衣，我按理就该是隐形的。那滋味我懂。我压根儿不该穿它出来，可它厚实，又是位老朋友。你要是喊住了我，我羞也羞死了。谢天谢地你没有。往后我不再冒这个险了，天黑的时候不算。行了，劳驾去把那些衣裳换了好不好？你本该穿雨衣的。今儿晚上吃傍晚茶，我在山冈上忽然惦记起蘑菇来，回家路上就买了些。咱们拿它配炒蛋，你愿意就来帮帮忙。”

===Original===

Ruth was childish in some ways, but she was not stupid. She could see that Miss Mole’s words might be tactful as well as true. She had not dared to ask whether Miss Mole had seen and avoided her and, if so, whether it was for her own sake or for Ruth’s. The confession had not been a full one, but, after Miss Mole’s expressed horror of the ulster, how could she mention it? That would have been easing her soul at the cost of Miss Mole’s feelings and though it was convenient to say no more, it was not necessarily wrong. Less and less, as she grew older, did she believe that the unpleasant things were the good ones and, as she slowly changed her stockings, she gave way willingly to the belief, which had been reluctant until today, that Miss Mole could be trusted with her omissions as safely as with her fears. She felt, rather than thought, that Miss Mole’s mind could overleap gaps and understand how they came there, and when she went down to the kitchen, she felt stiff and awkward in the consciousness of a surrender she had been determined not to make.

Miss Mole was skinning the mushrooms, and when she had shown Ruth how to do it they sat at the kitchen table together and worked busily.

“I’ve been thinking about clothes,” Hannah said, and Ruth, though she turned red, decided that it was a good thing to talk about them at once, before the thought of the ulster became a solid barrier against the subject. “I’ve always liked them, and never had what I wanted. When I went to school, we weren’t all dressed alike, and I was a scarecrow﻿—but much more noticeable. The only thing to do was to pretend I had an original taste in dress and the other poor things hadn’t, and I’ve done that ever since, except about my shoes and stockings. I’m extravagant about them, so I have to go short elsewhere.” She glanced down at the shoes she had been too hurried to change. “There isn’t a better pair in Radstowe, but I broke a window with this one once,” she said carelessly.

===Chinese===

露丝有些地方孩子气，却不傻。她看得出，莫尔小姐的话既可能是实情，也可能是体贴。她没敢问莫尔小姐是不是看见了她、避开了她；若是，那是为了莫尔小姐自己，还是为了露丝。这场招认本就不完全，可莫尔小姐既然明明白白表示过对那件大衣的深恶痛绝，她怎么还提得出口？那等于拿莫尔小姐的感情作代价，来宽慰自己的良心；虽说不再提很省事，却也未必就是错。年纪越长，她越不信讨人厌的事就准是好事；她慢慢换着袜子，心甘情愿地归顺了那条直到今天她还不大肯信的道理：把省下没提的事托付给莫尔小姐，同把恐惧托付给她一样稳妥。她感觉到——与其说是想到——莫尔小姐的心思能一步跨过缺口，还明白缺口是怎么来的；等她下楼去厨房时，她浑身发僵、手脚都不知往哪儿放，因为她意识到自己终究做了投降，而她本是打定主意不投降的。

莫尔小姐正在削蘑菇；等她教会露丝怎么削，两人便一起坐在厨房桌边，忙忙碌碌地干起活来。

“我一直在想衣裳的事，”汉娜说。露丝虽然红了脸，却断定趁早谈这个倒好，省得那件大衣的念头结成一堵厚墙，把这个话头挡死。“我一向喜欢衣裳，却从没得到过想要的。我上学那会儿，大家穿得还不都一样，我就是个稻草人——不过扎眼得多。唯一的法子，就是装出我在穿戴上有独到的品味，那些可怜东西可没有；打那以后我一直这么装，只有鞋和袜子除外。鞋袜上我大手大脚，别处就只好省着过。”她低头瞥了一眼自己方才来不及换的那双鞋。“全拉德斯托找不出更好的一双，不过这只鞋砸破过一扇窗户，”她满不在乎地说。

===Original===

Ruth looked up. “What did you do that for?”

“It’s one of the stories I can’t tell you, but it was very sad﻿—and exciting.”

“Can’t you tell me, really? You’re always talking about stories and not telling them. There’s the one about the burglar﻿—”

===Chinese===

露丝抬起头。“您干吗砸人家窗户？”

“那是我不能讲给你听的故事当中的一个，不过它非常悲伤——也非常惊险。”

“真的不能讲给我听吗？您老是说有这么些故事，又老是不讲。还有那个窃贼的故事——”

===Original===

“Ah yes, but there’s so little time. You have to work in the evenings and then you have to go to bed, or there’s someone else in the room.”

“Tonight would be a good night,” Ruth suggested, “and I haven’t so very much to do. Unless Wilfrid’s going to be in?”

“No, he’ll be out.”

===Chinese===

“啊，是啊，可是时间太少。你晚上要做功课，做完又得上床，要不屋里就还有别人。”

“今儿晚上就挺合适，”露丝提议，“我的功课也不算多。除非威尔弗里德晚上要在家？”

“不在，他要出门。”

===Original===

“Oh well, then﻿—!” Ruth exclaimed.

“We’ll see. You have to be in the right mood for telling stories. But do remind me to ask Ethel if she knows any old woman who would condescend to wear that ulster.”

“Oh, Miss Mole,” Ruth almost pleaded, “I shouldn’t give it away, if you like it.”

===Chinese===

“哦，那就——！”露丝欢呼道。

“再说吧。讲故事得有讲故事的心境。不过你可千万提醒我，问问埃塞尔，看她认不认得哪位老太太肯屈尊穿上那件大衣。”

“哦，莫尔小姐，”露丝几乎是恳求了，“您要是喜欢它，我不会把它送掉的呢。”

===Original===

“I don’t. I merely master it.”

“But you said it was an old friend, and there must be yards and yards of stuff in it. Perhaps you could have it altered.”

“No, I’m not brave enough to show it to a tailor. But I think I’ll keep it. It’s good enough for wearing to the pillar-box on a dark night, but I won’t wear it anywhere else,” she said, and now Ruth knew that Miss Mole could forgive everything she understood, and that she probably understood everything.

===Chinese===

“我不喜欢。我只是降得住它。”

“可您说它是位老朋友，再说那上头的料子拆下来总有好几码。也许可以送去改一改。”

“不成，我可没那份胆量把它拿给裁缝看。不过我想我会留下它。摸黑上邮筒那儿去，穿它还够格，别的地方我是不穿的了，”她说。此刻露丝明白了：莫尔小姐能宽恕她所懂得的一切，而她多半懂得一切。

## XIV / 第十四章

===Original===

For once, Hannah’s hands were idle. She lay back in the armchair by the dining-room fire with a book on her knee, and Ruth, glancing up from her work, now and then, saw that her eyes were often shut. She looked different like that; younger and, though Ruth did not find words for her thought, more vulnerable. Her dress of dark red silk was not fashionable and it was old, but the skirt of it and her silk stockings shimmered in the firelight, the buckles on her slippers sparkled, and her idleness and her elegant feet gave Ruth a feeling of satisfaction and of approach to the ideal life in the Vicarage, where everybody changed for dinner, and no one was in a hurry. Her mother, whose resistance to evening meetings had not been complete and who might be called out at any hour on missions of mercy, had seldom worn a dress unsuitable for sudden excursions, and this had offended Ruth’s sense of fitness and added to the restlessness of a home in which everybody was expected to be doing good outside it.

All stuffy things had been implied, for Ruth, in the name of housekeeper; stuffy frocks, thick stockings, a prim face and an oppressive sense of duty, yet here was Miss Mole looking, for all her lack of fashion, like a lady who belonged to a world unconnected with chapels, where beauty and leisure were expected and attained. It was a peep through a door Ruth had always wanted to open, and she said quietly, “I like it when you’re not darning.”

Hannah opened her eyes for a moment. “Half asleep,” she said drowsily and shut them again. This was not the truth, for her mind was busy, but Hannah was not scrupulous about truth. She was not convinced of its positive value as human beings knew it, she considered it a limiting and an embarrassing convention. The bare truth was often dull and more often awkward, while lies were a form of imagination and a protection for the privacy of her thoughts and, in a life lived in houses which were not her own and where she was never safe from intrusion, it was necessary to have this retreat.

Now, behind the veil of her sleepiness, she was wondering if she was altogether glad of Ruth’s change of front. It was what she had been working for and she found, once more, that gain was often loss. She had won Ruth and that exciting campaign was over. It had been conducted with a skill of which, unfortunately, only she was aware, and of which she could not boast, and her reward was a possession needing care and involving obligations. She was growing fond of Ruth and, ten years ago, she had promised herself future freedom from soft emotions. They were more trouble than they were worth, but vanity was her weakness. She strove for admiration and found she could get nothing without giving something else. She saw that she would have to resign herself to being fond of Ruth, she had gone too far to withdraw, but there she would have to stop: she must not saddle herself with the whole family and, indeed, it was not likely that she would have the chance. Robert Corder and she were naturally antipathetic, though he had been amiable this evening, praising the mushrooms, with perhaps a covert suggestion that the kitchen was her sphere and she would do well to stay in it, while Ethel was so much divided against herself, dragged in one direction by religion and in another by mundane desires, that no one else could put the parts together and handle them.

===Chinese===

难得这一回，汉娜的双手闲着。她仰靠在餐厅炉火边的扶手椅里，膝上摊着一本书；露丝不时从手里的活计上抬起眼来，看见她的眼睛常常阖着。她那副模样看着不一样；年轻些，而且——露丝虽找不出字眼来说清这层感觉——更见得单薄易伤。她那条深红绸连衣裙既不时新，又见旧了，可裙幅和她的丝袜在火光里微微闪烁，拖鞋上的鞋扣亮晶晶的；她这一份闲逸和一双秀致的脚，叫露丝生出一种心满意足之感，仿佛挨近了牧师住宅里的理想生活——在那儿，人人赴晚宴必换礼服，谁也不慌不忙。她母亲对晚间聚会的抵挡向来不曾彻底，随时可能应召出门去做慈悲差事，身上难得有不合适突然出门的衣裳；这一层开罪了露丝的合宜之感，也给那个家更添了几分躁动——在那个家里，人人都被指望到外头去做好事。

在露丝心里，“女管家”这个名头一向意味着种种古板气闷的东西：古板的连衫裙、厚墩墩的长袜、一本正经的面孔、压得人透不过气的责任感；可眼前的莫尔小姐，纵然毫不入时，看上去却像一位属于另一个世界的太太——那世界同礼拜堂毫不搭界，在那儿，美与闲逸是理所应得、也果然到手的东西。这等于从一扇露丝一直想推开的门里，窥见了里头的光景。她轻轻地说：“我喜欢你不做织补活儿的时候。”

汉娜睁开眼睛，睁了一会儿。“半睡半醒呢，”她睡意沉沉地说，随即又阖上了。这并非实话，她心里正忙着呢；不过汉娜对实话从不拘泥。世人所理解的那种真究竟有什么实在价值，她并不信服；她认为那是一件限制人又叫人难堪的俗套。赤裸裸的真往往乏味，更多的时候是尴尬；谎呢，倒是想象力的一种形式，是护住她内心私密的屏障。她一辈子住在别人的家中，随时防不住人家闯进来，这样一处退身之地是非有不可的。

此刻，在那层睡意的帷幕后头，她正暗自盘算：露丝阵前倒戈，她是否全然觉得可喜。这本是她苦心争取的目标，可她又一次发现：所得往往即是所失。她赢下了露丝，那场激动人心的战役就此终结。战役打得极有章法，可惜唯有她自己心知，又无从夸耀；而她得到的犒赏，是一件须加照料、还附带种种义务的战利品。她渐渐喜欢起露丝来，而十年前她就对自己许下过诺言：往后的岁月，再不受柔情软意的牵累。那类东西麻烦多于价值，可虚荣是她的弱点。她奋力博取爱慕，却发现不付出点别的便一无所获。她明白，自己只得认了这份对露丝的喜欢——她走得已远，无法回头——但也只能到此为止：她决不能把一家子都驮上自己的背；况且说实话，多半也不会有这个机会。罗伯特·科德与她天性相克，虽然今晚他很和气，还夸了蘑菇，话里或许暗藏机锋，暗示厨房才是她的本分，她识相就该守在本分里；而埃塞尔呢，自己跟自己就闹着分裂，一头让宗教拽着，一头让尘俗的欲望拖着，旁人谁也没法把那几部分拼拢起来，更遑论驾驭了。

===Original===

“I’ve nearly finished,” she heard Ruth saying, “I’m nearly ready for the burglar story.”

“It’s not a good one for this time of night.”

“Oh, Miss Mole, you said you liked him!”

“So I did, but then I’ve got queer tastes. And I’m afraid you’ve scamped your work.”

“But it’s such a chance﻿—when everybody’s out.”

“Oh well﻿—” Hannah said, hastily gathering her thoughts. “It was when I was living with an old lady who wore a wig. People without experience of wigs believe they simplify life. They think you just take them off and put them on. Nothing of the sort. A wig needs as much care as a pedigree Pekinese and I know, because I’ve looked after both. I lost a situation once because a woman heard me telling her nasty little dog what I really thought of it, but it did me good, and the dog too, I daresay. Well, anyhow, this old lady had a wig, in fact she had two, because they had to be sent to the hairdresser, now and then, to be tidied up, and they were bright gold. She was rich, and I suppose she liked the colour. But she wasn’t a bad old woman. I was fond of her. Well, one night﻿—it was a lonely house, simply asking for burglars to walk into it﻿—and I ought to have told you that one of the wigs had come back from the hairdresser that very evening, in a registered parcel, and I’d left it, unopened, on the landing table where the bedroom candles were put. It was that kind of house.”

===Chinese===

“我快做完了，”她听见露丝说，“窃贼的故事，我差不多预备好了。”

“这个故事在这个钟点讲，可不合适。”

“哎呀，莫尔小姐，你说过你喜欢他的！”

“我是说过，可我的趣味古怪。再说，我怕你这活儿做得有些偷工。”

“可这机会多难得呀——全家都出门了。”

“哦，好吧——”汉娜说，赶忙收拢心思。“那是我陪一位戴假发的老太太过活的时候。没伺候过假发的人，都当假发叫日子省心。他们以为，不过是脱下来、再戴上去罢了。全然不是那么回事。一顶假发费的心思，赛过一只纯种哈巴狗，这我有数，因为两样我都伺候过。有一回我丢了差事，就因为一位太太听见我对她那只讨厌的小狗吐了实话；不过这对我有好处，我敢说，对狗也一样。闲话少说，这位老太太有一顶假发，其实有两顶，因为假发得时不时送去理发师那儿拾掇拾掇；假发是灿亮的金黄色。她有钱，我猜她就爱这个颜色。可她不是坏老太太，我喜欢她。好，一天夜里——那是所孤零零的宅子，简直专候着窃贼上门——这一点本该先同你交代：恰在那天傍晚，其中一顶假发刚从理发师那儿寄回来，装在挂号包裹里，我把它原封不动搁在楼梯口的桌上，卧室蜡烛就放在那儿。那户人家，就是这么个格局。”

===Original===

“Ah,” said Ruth, “the burglar thought it was jewels!”

“Wait a minute. I saw my old lady into bed and into her nightcap and I longed to tell her how nice she looked when she wasn’t tricked out in the yellow hair﻿—”

“But it must have been awful when the wig was off and the cap wasn’t on! I don’t think I could have stayed there.”

“I didn’t stay long. The old lady died. She was going to leave me some money, she said, and I think she meant it, but she died first. It’s a way old ladies have.”

“Did she die of fright about the burglar?”

“She didn’t know there’d been one, because he took nothing. Nothing!” Hannah repeated impressively. “And all through me! If she’d known that, she might have given me the money there and then.”

===Chinese===

“啊，”露丝说，“窃贼当那是珠宝！”

“等一等。我伺候老太太上了床，戴好了睡帽，心里真想对她说：不打扮上那头黄毛的时候，她模样多好看——”

“可假发摘了、睡帽还没戴上的当儿，那一定吓人得很！我恐怕在那儿待不下去。”

“我没待多久。老太太去世了。她说要留给我一笔钱，我想她是当真的，可她先死了。老太太们都有这个毛病。”

“她是让窃贼给吓死的吗？”

“她压根儿不知道进过贼，因为他什么也没拿。什么也没拿！”汉娜把语气放重，郑重地又说了一遍。“而且这全亏了我！她要是知道这一节，说不定当场就把钱给了我。”

===Original===

“And you didn’t tell her?”

“I haven’t told a soul until tonight. And I’d better warn you that the story’s got a moral.”

“Of course it has. And the moral’s that you mustn’t be too modest.”

“That’s always been one of my failings,” Hannah said with a wink. “But this story’s hanging fire a bit, isn’t it? I’ll cut it short. I woke up in the night and heard a rustling sort of noise. ‘Mice!’ I said to myself, but it wasn’t exactly a mousy noise. So I listened and my heart began to thump and, very quietly, I got out of bed. I turned the door handle without making a sound, and then﻿—” she sat up to illustrate her action, “I threw the door open﻿—like that﻿—and what do you think I saw?”

Ruth shook her head. She knew she was not expected to reply.

“I saw the burglar, looking at himself in the mirror, with my old lady’s wig on the top of his head!”

===Chinese===

“那你没告诉她？”

“到今晚为止，我对谁也没讲过。还得先提醒你：这故事带一句寓意。”

“那自然。寓意就是：人切不可过分谦虚。”

“过分谦虚向来是我的毛病之一，”汉娜眨眨眼说。“不过这故事有点磨磨蹭蹭，是不是？我拣要紧的说。夜里我醒了，听见一阵窸窸窣窣的响动。‘老鼠！’我心里说，可那声音又不太像老鼠弄出来的。于是我侧耳细听，心口怦怦地跳起来，我悄悄地下了床。我拧动门把，没弄出一点声响，跟着——”她坐直身子，把动作比划出来，“我猛地把门推开——就像这样——你猜我瞧见了什么？”

露丝摇了摇头。她知道自己无须答话。

“我看见那个窃贼，正对着镜子照自己，头顶上扣着我老太太的假发！”

===Original===

“Then,” said Ruth slowly, “there must have been a light on the landing.”

“Yes, there was,” Hannah said quickly. “He’d turned it on. And that’s where you’re supposed to laugh and you haven’t even smiled.”

“I’m thinking about the light. He can’t have been a good burglar.”

“He wasn’t. He was a funny one. I laughed and he laughed and, after that, we felt we were friends, and he went away like a gentleman, saying he was glad he’d met me. And the moral is that we must be ready to laugh on the most terrifying occasions. Now, I call that rather a good story and a good moral, and you don’t seem to like it a bit.”

“I’m rather worried about it,” said Ruth, and she looked frowningly past Hannah’s head, “because, if it was a house that had bedroom candles, would the landing have had electric light? I really do like the story, Miss Mole, but I can’t bear not to get things clear. Now, if he’d had an electric torch﻿—”

“Yes,” said Hannah gloomily, “I ought to have thought of that, but it wouldn’t have done. You see, the drama of the thing is opening your bedroom door in the dark, simply shaking like a leaf, as they say, and finding a blaze of light and a burglar standing in front of a looking-glass with a wig on his head. The bedroom candles were a mistake.”

===Chinese===

“那么，”露丝慢吞吞地说，“楼梯口必定是点着灯的。”

“不错，点着了，”汉娜急忙说。“是他开的灯。讲到这儿你该笑了，可你连一丝笑影都没有。”

“我在想那盏灯。他不会是个高明的窃贼。”

“他是算不上。他是个滑稽的窃贼。我笑了，他也笑了，打那以后，我们只觉得彼此是朋友了；他走的时候一副绅士气派，还说同我相识很高兴。寓意便是：顶骇人的场合，我们也得备好一笑。依我看，这故事不坏，寓意也不坏，可你听着竟一点也不领情。”

“这个故事我倒有点犯愁，”露丝说，皱着眉头，目光越过汉娜的头顶，“因为，既然那人家点的是卧室蜡烛，楼梯口会有电灯么？故事我是真心喜欢，莫尔小姐，可事情交代不清，我受不了。要不然，他若带着一只手电筒——”

“唉，”汉娜悻悻地说，“这个我本该想到，可是那样就不成戏了。你明白么，这桩事的戏眼，全在黑地里推开卧室的房门——像人家说的，抖得跟片树叶子似的——迎面却撞见灯火通明，一个窃贼立在穿衣镜跟前，头上顶着假发。卧室蜡烛是个失算。”

===Original===

“Miss Mole,” Ruth said solemnly, “did you make it up?”

Three times Hannah slowly nodded her head catching her lip like a naughty child. “If I’d had more time,” she began apologetically﻿—

“If you’d had more time, I shouldn’t have found out,” Ruth said, and the anxious look, which troubled Hannah, returned to her face. “And I suppose it isn’t true about your cottage and the owls.”

“Every word of it,” Hannah said. “And the old lady and the wigs are true, but what was the good of them without the burglar? You wanted a burglar and I had to give you a nice one.”

“But have you ever known a nasty one?”

“No, but I can make one up.”

===Chinese===

“莫尔小姐，”露丝一本正经地问，“这是你编出来的吧？”

汉娜慢慢地点了三下头，像个淘气孩子似的衔住嘴唇。“要是我有更多的工夫，”她带着歉意开了口——

“要是有更多的工夫，我就发现不了啦，”露丝说，脸上又浮起那副叫汉娜不安的愁容。“我想，你那所村舍和猫头鹰的事，也当不得真了。”

“一个字都不假，”汉娜说。“老太太和假发是真的，可没有窃贼，光有这两样顶什么用？你要一个窃贼，我就得给你备一个可爱的。”

“可你真见识过一个讨厌的窃贼么？”

“没见识过，不过我可以编一个。”

===Original===

Ruth smiled feebly. “Ethel would think it was dreadful.”

“I wouldn’t have told it to Ethel,” Hannah said quickly, and Ruth’s smile broadened.

“She’d think it was lying.”

“Not lying. Fiction,” Hannah said.

“Yes, fiction,” Ruth agreed willingly. “But how,” she cried, “am I going to know when it really happened and when it didn’t?”

“Ah, that’s the fun of it. You’ve got to find out and, next time, I shall be more careful.”

===Chinese===

露丝淡淡一笑。“埃塞尔会觉得这太要不得。”

“这故事我决不讲给埃塞尔听，”汉娜赶忙说，露丝的笑意便舒展开了。

“她会说这是撒谎。”

“不是撒谎，是虚构，”汉娜说。

“对，虚构，”露丝欣然同意。“可是往后，”她忽然嚷起来，“我怎么知道哪些是真事、哪些不是呢？”

“嗐，妙处就在这里。得你自己去发现；下回，我可要小心些了。”

===Original===

“And did you break the window with your foot?”

“Yes, that was true.”

“And you can’t tell me about that?”

“You wouldn’t like it, though it had its funny side, I must admit. I’m afraid you wouldn’t see it. You’re not very good at laughing.”

“No. But I’m glad it’s true about the cottage,” she said contentedly.

“Are you?” Hannah asked rather wearily, and she sank back in her chair and shut her eyes again.

===Chinese===

“那你还是用脚把窗户踢破的？”

“是啊，那个是真的。”

“这个你就不能讲给我听？”

“你不会爱听的，虽然我得承认，那桩事也有它可笑的一面。我怕你就瞧不出来。你不大擅长笑。”

“是不会。可村舍的事是真的，我就高兴，”她心满意足地说。

“是么？”汉娜问得颇有几分倦意，随即靠回椅背，重新阖上了眼睛。

===Original===

Ruth felt a little uneasy. The quality of Miss Mole’s relaxation had changed. She was no longer a lady of leisure, but one who was tired, and, perhaps, unhappy, and Ruth had a peep through another door which led into the places where Miss Mole’s spirit had wandered.

She cleared her throat and said in a small voice, “Miss Mole, are you all right?”

“I’m doing my best,” Hannah said, smiling, but keeping her eyes shut.

“I mean, do you feel ill, or something?”

“I don’t feel ill, but I do feel something.”

“A pain?”

===Chinese===

露丝觉得有些不安了。莫尔小姐这份闲适的质地变了。她不再是有闲的太太，倒像个累了的人，或许还是个不快活的人；露丝又从另一扇门里窥见了一角，那扇门通向莫尔小姐的心灵游荡过的地方。

她清了清嗓子，细声细气地问：“莫尔小姐，你没事吧？”

“我正在尽力而为，”汉娜说，嘴角含笑，眼睛却没有睁开。

“我是说，你是不是觉得不舒服，还是怎么的？”

“不舒服倒不觉得，不过确实觉着点什么。”

“疼么？”

===Original===

“A kind of pain.”

“Well, would you like anything?”

“Heaps of things,” Hannah said, and now she disclosed her eyes which were bright and merry. “I want a small fortune to begin with. Fetch me that, if you can. If you can’t, you’d better go to bed.”

“Not yet. Let’s talk about it. If you had it, what would you do?”

“Pack my box. No offence meant, but wouldn’t you do it yourself?”

“I suppose so,” Ruth said, trying to be reasonable and not hurt.

===Chinese===

“算是一种疼吧。”

“那么，你想要点什么吗？”

“要的东西可多着呢，”汉娜说，这会儿她亮出了那双眼睛，又亮又欢。“先要一小笔财产。办得到就给我取来。办不到，你顶好上床睡觉去。”

“还早呢。咱们说说这个吧。你要是有了它，打算怎么办？”

“收拾我的箱子。并无得罪之意，换了你，难道不这么办？”

“我想也会的，”露丝说，竭力做得通情达理，不露出受伤的样子。

===Original===

“I’d pack my box, but I’d leave you my little ship, for remembrance, and a good home. I should want a good home for it. It would be an awkward thing to take into the Arabian desert, for instance. You want ships of the desert there, not bottled ones. However, I’m not sure that I should go to Arabia. I’ve never been able to eat dates. London first, and new clothes of the very best cut and quality, and while they’re being made, for I’m not going to have anything off a peg, I’ll go into travel agencies and ask questions of young men who don’t know the answers.”

“How do you know they don’t?” Ruth asked sharply.

“Because I’ve tried them. Many an afternoon I’ve spent, leaning over one of those counters. You get all the uncertainty of foreign travel without the expense. But I won’t make things too difficult for them at first. I’m going to Spain. I’ve never been there though it’s full of my own castles.”

“And of mine,” said Ruth.

“Yes, I wonder there’s any room left in the place. Let’s go and see. Will you come with me?”

Ruth nodded. “I’d love to.”

===Chinese===

“我要收拾箱子，不过我会把我的小船留给你，做个纪念，还给它寻个好归宿。总得给它寻个好人家。比方说，带进阿拉伯沙漠里去就麻烦了。那儿要的是沙漠之舟，不是瓶里泊着的舟。不过，去不去阿拉伯我还没准呢。椰枣我一向吃不惯。先到伦敦，做几身剪裁和质地都顶好的新衣；衣裳做着的当儿——现成衣架上挂的货我可不买——我就跑旅行社，拣那些小伙子答不上来的问题去问。”

“你怎么知道他们答不上来？”露丝尖利地问。

“因为我试过他们。多少个下午，我都趴在那类柜台上消磨掉了。旅费分文不花，国外旅行的种种没把握倒全尝遍了。不过起头我不打算叫他们太下不来台。我先去西班牙。那儿我从来没去过，虽然它满处都是我自家的空中楼阁。”

“也是我的，”露丝说。

“是啊，那地方竟还有空地剩下，倒也奇了。咱们瞧瞧去。你肯同我一起去么？”

露丝点点头。“我乐意。”

===Original===

“Good,” said Miss Mole. “I can easily afford it. And after that, where shall we go? Not Italy. Too much culture and too many spinsters like myself. We might pick up a little boat at Marseilles and go jogging down the Mediterranean. And we wouldn’t come back until we wanted to, and we’d begun to wish we hadn’t so much time on our hands. But we’d see South America first.”

The loud ringing of the front door bell shattered the visions they were sharing of Creole beauties, vast mountain ranges, immense rivers and impassable jungles.

“There!” Ruth exclaimed. “Somebody’s come to spoil it!”

“Only the postman,” Hannah said airily, “with a registered letter from my lawyers, about the fortune.”

There was the chance that something exciting was going to happen with every knock or ring, and though few people would have applied the adjective to Mr. Blenkinsop, who was standing on the doorstep, Hannah felt laughter rising in her at the sight of him.

“I call this very kind,” she said brightly. “Do come in.”

===Chinese===

“好，”莫尔小姐说。“这笔钱我全然出得起。那以后，咱们上哪儿？不去意大利。文化太多，同我一样的老小姐也太多。咱们可以在马赛（Marseilles）搭一条小船，一路颠颠簸簸地下地中海（the Mediterranean）去。咱们不想回来就不回来，直待到开始巴不得手里没那么多闲工夫为止。不过得先去南美洲逛逛。”

前门的门铃大声响起来，把她们正共享的幻景震得粉碎——克里奥尔的美人、莽莽的山脉、浩浩的江流、人迹难通的丛林。

“瞧！”露丝嚷道，“有人来搅了！”

“不过是邮差罢了，”汉娜轻飘飘地说，“送来我律师的挂号信，讲那笔财产的事。”

每一声叩门或铃响，都可能带来一桩叫人兴奋的事；虽然难得有人肯把“叫人兴奋”这个形容词安在布伦金索普先生头上——他这会儿正站在门阶上——汉娜一看见他，还是觉得笑意直往上冒。

“我说，这可真够客气的，”她满面春风地说。“快请进来。”

===Original===

Raising his hat, Mr. Blenkinsop asked if Mr. Corder was at home.

“Mr. Corder?” Hannah said, pretending to be disappointed. “No, he’s out.”

“I’m sorry,” said Mr. Blenkinsop, turning to go.

“Wait a bit!” Hannah cried. “I haven’t seen you since I carried up your dinner. But I’ve heard about you. Come in, and I’ll tell you what I’ve heard.”

“Thank you, but I wanted to see Mr. Corder. I’ll come another day.”

“You won’t find him in on a Wednesday. It’s the weeknight service.”

===Chinese===

布伦金索普先生举了举帽子，问科德先生在家不在。

“科德先生？”汉娜说，装出失望的样子。“不在，出门了。”

“遗憾，”布伦金索普先生说着，转身要走。

“等一等！”汉娜叫道。“打那回给您把饭端上楼去，还没见过您呢。不过您的情形我倒听说了些。进来吧，我讲给您听听。”

“谢谢，不过我要见的是科德先生。改天再来吧。”

“礼拜三晚上他准不在家。是周间晚礼拜。”

===Original===

“Stupid of me,” Mr. Blenkinsop muttered.

“Mr. Corder will consider it a sad lapse of memory. I don’t think I’ll tell him you came.”

“It’s a matter of indifference to me,” he said.

“Yes, that’s what he’ll realize, I’m afraid. I’ve looked for you every Sunday, Mr. Blenkinsop.”

“I don’t quite see why you should trouble.”

“I have a tenderness for Mr. Corder’s feelings.”

===Chinese===

“瞧我这记性，”布伦金索普先生嘟哝道。

“科德先生会说这是记性的一次可悲失检。我想，您来过这件事我就不告诉他了。”

“这种事我不在乎，”他说。

“是啊，恐怕他领会的正是这一层。每个礼拜天我都在寻您呢，布伦金索普先生。”

“我不大明白，您何必费这个心。”

“我不过心疼科德先生的感受罢了。”

===Original===

“Oh,” said Mr. Blenkinsop, “so you’ve settled down.”

“You can put it that way if you like. I wish you’d come in, but it’s a nice night, after the rain,” she said, looking skyward. “What bright stars!”

“Yes, very bright.”

“But it’s cold,” said Miss Mole.

“Don’t let me keep you,” said he, but he did not move, and Hannah went on conversationally. “Yes, it’s cold, but I suppose we ought to call it seasonable. It’s funny about that word. It’s only cold, never hot, weather that’s seasonable. Now why? I find words very fascinating.”

“I’m afraid,” Mr. Blenkinsop said stiffly, “I mustn’t stay here and discuss etymology.”

===Chinese===

“哦，”布伦金索普先生说，“这么说你安顿下来了。”

“您愿意这么说，也无不可。我倒巴望您进来坐坐，不过今晚雨后的天色确实好，”她仰望天空说。“星星多亮！”

“是，很亮。”

“就是冷，”莫尔小姐说。

“别叫我耽搁您，”他说，人却没有动，汉娜便闲聊似的接着往下说。“是啊，是冷，可我想咱们该说这天气正应时令。这个词儿有趣。只有冷天气才配称应时，热天气从来不应时。这是为什么呢？我觉得字眼儿这东西迷人极了。”

“恐怕，”布伦金索普先生僵僵地说，“我不能站在这儿讨论词源学。”

===Original===

“I thought that was beetles,” Hannah said innocently, “and we can have a practical study of them in the kitchen, if you like. I seem doomed to have trouble in kitchens. And that reminds me﻿—How are the Riddings getting on? You know, I feel I haven’t been properly thanked for that evening.”

“Thanked!” Mr. Blenkinsop exclaimed, staring at her malignantly. “What I want to know is why on earth you wanted to do it. And as for thanks, whose did you expect?” he demanded, and with those words he marched away.

She had roused him at last, but she thought his fervour somewhat disproportionate to the slightness of the inconvenience he suffered.

===Chinese===

“我还当那是甲虫呢，”汉娜天真地说，“您要是乐意，咱们可以到厨房里去做一次实地考察。我好像注定同厨房结冤家。这一说倒提醒了我——里丁一家近来怎么样？您知道，那个晚上过后，我总觉得自己没得到一回正正经经的道谢。”

“道谢！”布伦金索普先生嚷道，恶狠狠地瞪着她。“我想知道的是，你干吗非要揽那件事。至于道谢，你指望谁来谢？”他诘问道，撂下这句话，扬长而去。

她总算把他撩动了；只是她想，他那股激愤，比起他那点小小的不便来，未免有些不成比例。

## XIX / 第十九章

===Original===

It was pleasant to see Ruth looking happier and Hannah subdued a sort of jealousy of Uncle Jim, whose advent had succeeded where her own presence had failed, and she determined to be happy too. It was pure wastefulness to spoil the present because the future might hold trouble, and everything conspired to help her. Ruth was less quarrelsome with Ethel, and Ethel, perhaps exhausted by her outburst, seemed more peaceful and more enthusiastic about the club, though Doris had definitely deserted it in favour of her young man. There were Christmas festivities to be arranged, both there and at the chapel and, when she chose, Ethel could be as thorough in her work as in her grief. Hannah herself had more than enough to do with her own preparations, but she found time for a visit to Mr. Samson, now and then. That disreputable-looking old gentleman, who seemed to have been all over the world and to have tried all trades, who distrusted all men and most women with a good-humoured cynicism, whose chief prejudice was against parsons of every denomination and who had settled down in Beresford Road as though in obedience to some unsuspected craving for respectability, was the perfect antidote to Robert Corder. He was, in fact, in many ways, what Hannah would have been if she had been a man, and in his freedom from any received set of opinions, in his loose, but not offensive, tongue, in the stories he told her and, above all, in his appreciation of herself, she found a relief which, no doubt, had its subtle effect on the Corder household. Her demon of mischief, getting its exercise in talking to Mr. Samson and provoking his thick chuckle of amusement, had a less persistent desire to tease Robert Corder. It was impossible to resist puzzling him when the opportunity came, but she did not go out of her way to do it, and she could feel that he was inclining to think of her as the right woman in the right place. This, in itself, was irritating, but the contrast between his view and Mr. Samson’s, between the careful face Robert Corder knew and the one she could show Mr. Samson, between the self-conscious propriety of the minister and the old man’s lively disregard of it, was a secret delight. If Mr. Samson’s stories were true, he had known many women intimately; he talked of marriage with knowledge, though he did not mention a wife, and whatever his experiences had really been, they had produced what, to Hannah, seemed a sane apprehension of the relationship between men and women, giving due, and often humorous, importance to its physical side, but accepting it as naturally as he accepted food, and refusing to make a definite cleavage between male and female, anywhere. Mr. Samson, clearly, had not deliberately thought this out, nor did he express his opinions: like his red, puffy face and his wicked old eyes, they were the fruit of life as he had lived it, and though the fruit was ripe, it was not rotten. Ruth need have had no fear of him; indeed, as Hannah discovered, she had every cause to trust him, and Hannah herself could enjoy his approval of her remarkably neat legs and feet without feeling obliged to assure him of her inherent modesty. Hannah had no modesty in Robert Corder’s sense which implied a perpetual and restraining consciousness of her sex. She was not anxious to forget it; she was as feminine as anyone else and she had suffered too much from being treated as a machine, but she was a human being more abundantly than she was a woman, and this was what Mr. Samson understood.

===Chinese===

看到露丝神情开朗起来，是桩愉快的事；汉娜压下了一股对吉姆舅舅的近乎嫉妒的情绪——他的驾到，做到了她亲自到场也做不到的事——并且决意自己也快活起来。只因将来或许藏着麻烦就糟蹋了眼前，纯属浪费；何况万事万物又都齐心来成全她。露丝同埃塞尔不那么爱吵嘴了；埃塞尔——兴许是那一场发作把她耗空了——显得心平气和了些，对少女俱乐部也热心了些，尽管多丽丝已断然弃它而去，投奔她那位小伙子去了。圣诞的庆祝要操办，俱乐部和礼拜堂两处都有；而埃塞尔只要愿意，做起事来可以同她悲伤一样彻底。汉娜自己单是备办各色事情就已经忙不过来，却还是时不时挤出工夫，去桑普森先生那儿走一趟。那位看上去就不甚体面的老绅士，似乎走遍了天涯海角、试遍了百样行当；他怀着一种乐呵呵的愤世嫉俗，对一切男人概不信任，对多数女人也不信任；他的头一桩成见是冲着各宗各派的牧师去的；而他偏偏在贝雷斯福德路安顿下来，倒像是遵从了某种连自己都未曾察觉的、对体面生活的渴念——对于罗伯特·科德，他正是一剂完美的解药。说实话，在许多方面，他就是汉娜若是生为男人便会成为的那个人：不受任何现成的一套见解束缚，言谈放诞却不伤人，加上讲给她听的那些故事，尤其是他对她本人的赏识——她从中得到一种宽解，而这宽解，无疑对科德一家也起了微妙的作用。她那促狭魔鬼，在同桑普森先生说话中得到了操练，还逗得老人粗声粗气地呵呵直乐，便不再那么执着地要来捉弄罗伯特·科德了。机会送到眼前，要不作弄他一作弄，是办不到的；可她也不特意寻隙，而且她感觉得到，他正渐渐倾向于把她看作放在合适位置上的合适女人。单是这一层就够叫人恼火了，可他眼中的她同桑普森先生眼中的她之间的对照——罗伯特·科德熟悉的那张小心翼翼的脸，同她可以亮给桑普森先生的那张脸之间的对照——牧师先生那副端方自持的做派，同老先生对这做派活活泼泼的蔑视之间的对照——却是一桩秘而不宣的乐子。倘若桑普森先生讲的那些故事句句是真，他曾同许多女人有过知根知底的交往；他谈起婚姻来是有阅历底子的，虽然从不提起自己有过妻子。而无论他的经历究竟如何，这些经历在他身上酿成的，是——在汉娜看来——一种对男女关系的健全把握：给其中的肉体一面以应有的、且往往带几分诙谐的看重，却又像他受用饭食一样自然地接受它，并且死活不肯在任何一处给男人和女人划出一条截然的分界。桑普森先生显然不曾存心把这套道理想个通透，他也不发表什么主张：如同他那红涨的松泡脸、他那双透着邪气的老眼睛，这些主张是他那般活法结出的果实；果子虽然熟透了，却没有烂。露丝原不必怕他；实际上，汉娜发现，她有十足的理由信任他。而汉娜自己，受用他对她那双利落得出奇的腿脚的赞美，也无须向他申明自己天生端庄。罗伯特·科德意义上的端庄，意味着时时刻刻惦着自己的女人身份、并且受它拘束——这样的端庄，汉娜没有。她并不急于忘记自己是女人；她和随便哪一个人一样有女人气，被人当作机器使唤，她也吃够了苦头；只是她之为“人”，更甚于她之为“女人”——这一层，桑普森先生懂得。

===Original===

She had her superstitious moments when she feared she had too many sources of happiness. She had the friendship of Wilfrid and Ruth and Mr. Samson, she was looking forward to the arrival of Howard and Uncle Jim, and she had Mr. Samson’s stories to remember and her own to concoct. These were a pleasant accompaniment to her household tasks. She could read when she was in bed, and she read late into every night at the expense of Robert Corder’s candles, but while she stoned the raisins for the Christmas puddings, dusted or darned, she was busy with her own romances in which sometimes Robert Corder and his wife, sometimes the bold buccaneer and herself were the chief actors or, more often, Mr. Blenkinsop struggled with the puzzling emotions she had created for him. She took most pleasure in the Blenkinsop story, for she could make it either comic or pathetic, and Mr. Blenkinsop was a subject to her taste. She could see the man who believed in safety being drawn into those dangerous, dim regions of pity where, as he groped his way, he would suddenly find himself on the frontier of a world still more dangerous and more attractive. As Mrs. Gibson said, he had a kind heart and it was touched by that spectacle of a slip of a girl pretending she had no troubles. Hannah, too, felt every admiration for Mrs. Ridding, but Mrs. Ridding had a baby, and a neurotic husband was a price worth paying for it. For the sake of one good baby, Hannah would have paid more than that and there were times when she felt vexed with Mr. Blenkinsop. Because Mrs. Ridding was the first woman of his acquaintance who had faced a difficult situation bravely, he would naturally think she was the only one there was and, flattered by the belief that he had made this discovery for himself, he was ready to credit her with every other good quality, as a man, finding a new continent, would refuse to see a fault in it. She could now understand his resentment at the unnecessary preservation of Mr. Ridding, yet﻿—here was the vicious circle again﻿—if Mr. Ridding had been left to die, Mrs. Ridding would, in all likelihood, have disappeared from Prince’s Road or, if she had remained, Mr. Blenkinsop would have considered her fortunate and taken no further interest in her, and it seemed to Hannah that her own mistake in life, always had been, and still was, her refusal to feel any sorrow for herself or to suggest herself as a subject for sorrow in other people. In her attempts to attract Mr. Blenkinsop’s attention, with sprightliness, intellect and ignorance, she had omitted the one method which would have been successful. Then she caught sight of her image in the glass and remembered that pathos without beauty is merely irritating, that a woman with a long nose could not be touching in her sadness, and at this point she neglected her stories for philosophical speculations on the effect of noses on destiny. In women, the perfect nose, in its appearance and its effects, was rather delicately cut, and very delicately tilted, while the suspicion of a droop had a tendency to produce tragedy unless its owner was a determined person, like herself. She had no theories about men’s noses. In the comparative unimportance of a man’s features, she reluctantly found proof of some sort of superiority in the other sex, and when she tried to remember the details of Mr. Blenkinsop’s face, she could recall no more than a clear skin, an impression of solid worth and a pair of spectacles.

===Chinese===

她也有迷信的时刻，生怕自己幸福的源头是不是太多了些。她有威尔弗里德、露丝和桑普森先生的情谊；她盼着霍华德和吉姆舅舅的到来；她还有桑普森先生的故事可供回味，也自有她自己的故事可以编造。操持家务时，这些是愉快的伴奏。躺在床上，她还能看书，而且每夜都看到深夜，烧的尽是罗伯特·科德的蜡烛；可为圣诞布丁给葡萄干去核的时候，掸尘的时候，织补的时候，她正忙着经营自己那些罗曼司：戏里的主角，有时是罗伯特·科德和他的妻子，有时是那胆大的海盗和她自己；更多的时候，则是布伦金索普先生在她替他设下的种种莫名的情愫里挣扎。布伦金索普的这段故事给她最大的乐趣，因为她爱把它写成喜剧也行、写成哀情也行，而布伦金索普先生本人正是一个合她口味的题目。她看得分明：这个信奉安稳的人正被一步步引进怜悯的那片昏暗而危险的境地，他在那里摸索着走，会蓦然发觉自己已站在一个更危险、也更有魅力的世界的边界上。诚如吉布森太太所说，他有副好心肠，也叫那光景打动了：那么单薄的一个人儿，偏要装作没有半点烦难。汉娜对里丁太太也一样敬佩到十分；可里丁太太有个孩子，为着孩子，一个神经质的丈夫算是值得付的价钱。为着一个好孩子，汉娜肯付比这更高的价；因此，有时候她也免不了对布伦金索普先生恼火。因为里丁太太是他相识的女人中头一个勇敢直面困境的，他自然就当她是天下仅有的一位；又因为相信这发现全出自自己的眼力而暗暗受用，他乐于把其余种种好品性一股脑儿记在她名下——好比一个人发现了一片新大陆，便不肯在新大陆上看出一丝毛病。如今她能懂得他为什么愤愤不平，觉得里丁先生那条命救得多余；然而——恶性循环又转回来了——当初若听凭里丁先生死去，里丁太太多半也就从王子路上消失了；纵然留下，布伦金索普先生也会认定她是幸运儿，不再对她多看一眼。在汉娜看来，自己一生的错处——从前始终是，如今仍然是——就在于她从不肯为自己悲苦，也从不肯把自己当作可供悲苦的题目，呈到别人眼前。她施展活泼、才智与无知，想引布伦金索普先生注意，却偏偏漏掉了那唯一能奏效的法子。跟着她瞥见镜子里的自己，想起哀婉若无美貌相佐，只会惹人生气；长鼻子的女人，纵然悲伤也动人不得；打这儿起，她把那些故事搁在一边，对起鼻子如何支配命运来，做起了哲学玄想。女人的完满鼻子，无论就模样还是就功效而论，都该是雕琢得相当精巧、翘起得极有分寸的；若鼻尖稍露一点下垂的意思，便有酿成悲剧之嫌，除非鼻子主人是个拿得定主意的人，比如她自己。男人的鼻子，她没有立过什么理论。男人的五官比较地无足轻重，她不情愿地从中看出了对方那个性别某种优越性的证据；而她竭力回忆布伦金索普先生面孔的细部时，能想起来的不过是干净的皮肤、一种厚重可靠的印象，外加一副眼镜。

===Original===

She was as glad, then, as she was astonished, when, on another Wednesday night, he gave her an opportunity to look at him. She was alone in the house, for Ruth was at some school festivity and Hannah was working at a party frock which was to be a surprise for her. One of Ruth’s objections to the Spenser-Smiths’ parties was the inadequacy of her clothes and the grandeur of Margery Spenser-Smith’s, and Hannah, who had known worse than inadequacy in her own, was determined that her child should be as prettily dressed as Lilla’s.

She frowned at the interruption when the bell rang, but she smiled when she saw Mr. Blenkinsop. “You have a very bad memory,” she told him. “It’s Wednesday night and Mr. Corder isn’t in.”

“Is anyone else at home?” he enquired.

“Only me,” Hannah said. “Would you like to leave a message?”

===Chinese===

于是，在另一个星期三晚上，当他给了她一个好好端详他的机会时，她又惊又喜。屋里只有她一个人：露丝去参加学校什么庆祝活动了，汉娜正赶做一件赴会的连衣裙，那是要给她个惊喜的。露丝对斯宾塞-史密斯家的晚会老大不情愿，理由之一是自己的衣裳太不像样，而玛杰丽·斯宾塞-史密斯（Margery Spenser-Smith）的衣裳又那样华贵；汉娜自己当年在衣裳上尝过的，比“不像样”还要糟得多，因此打定了主意：她的孩子也要打扮得同莉拉的一样漂亮。

门铃响起来，她对着这不合时宜的搅扰皱了皱眉，可一见来的是布伦金索普先生，便笑了。“你的记性真够呛，”她对他说，“今天是星期三晚上，科德先生不在家。”

“家里还有别人吗？”他问。

“只有我，”汉娜说，“你要留个话吗？”

===Original===

“I’d rather come in, unless I shall be in the way.”

“Not at all, and if you’re handy with your needle, I can give you plenty to do. But I expect,” she said, looking at him gravely, “you can’t manage anything more complicated than buttons.”

“I haven’t even got a needle. Mrs. Gibson makes that unnecessary.”

“Mrs. Gibson spoils people. You’ll miss her when you leave her.”

In the act of following Hannah into the dining-room, Mr. Blenkinsop stood still. “Who said I was going to leave her?”

“There was a murmur about it, wasn’t there? A kind of subterranean growl? Come and sit down. And I thought you might have come to the conclusion that it was the best thing to do. If I had a little capital, I’d start a boarding house, for what they call single gentlemen, myself, and I’d look after you like a mother, Mr. Blenkinsop.”

===Chinese===

“我宁可进来坐坐，除非我会碍你的事。”

“一点也不碍事；再说，你的针线要还使得，我有的是活儿派给你。不过我看哪，”她一脸郑重地看着他说，“比钉纽扣再复杂些的活儿，你是对付不来的。”

“我连针也没有。有吉布森太太在，就用不着了。”

“吉布森太太把人惯坏了。等你离开她，你会想她的。”

他正要随汉娜走进餐室，却站住了。“谁说我要离开她来着？”

“这事儿外头不是有过一点嘀咕么？地底下那种呜呜的低吼？来，坐下。我还当你已经想明白了，觉得离开她才是上策呢。我要是有几个小本钱，就自己开一家寄宿公寓——专做人们所说的单身绅士的生意——像母亲一样照应你，布伦金索普先生。”

===Original===

“But I haven’t the slightest intention of leaving Mrs. Gibson.”

“Oh, well, Mr. Blenkinsop, you’re the best judge, of course,” she said primly, picking up her sewing.

“Certainly I am,” he said stoutly. “And as for starting a boarding house, that would be a very silly thing to do.”

“Why? If I can manage this family,” Hannah said impressively, “I should make child’s play of the single gents.”

“You’re too young,” Mr. Blenkinsop said, frowning a little.

“Young!” Hannah’s laughter, the sound which had silenced Wilfrid’s whistling as he came down the street, was like a mockery of her own derision. “Why, how old do you think I am?”

===Chinese===

“可我压根儿没有离开吉布森太太的意思。”

“哦，好吧，布伦金索普先生，你最会判断，自然，你最会判断，”她端起架子说，拾起了针线。

“我当然最会判断，”他硬邦邦地说，“至于开寄宿公寓，那是再蠢不过的事情。”

“怎么蠢？我若是管得下这一大家子人，”汉娜说得煞有介事，“管起那班单身先生来，还不当它儿戏一般。”

“你太年轻，”布伦金索普先生说，微微皱起了眉。

“年轻！”汉娜放声笑了——那曾在威尔弗里德沿街走来时止住他口哨的笑声，此刻听来倒像是对她自己的讥诮再作一回嘲弄。“哟，你当我多大岁数？”

===Original===

“About my own age, I suppose.”

Hannah shook her head. “Centuries older, Mr. Blenkinsop. Perhaps only a few years, as we count time, but while you’ve been behind your bars in the bank I’ve been pushing my way into other people’s houses and being pushed out again. Great fun!” she added hastily. “I’d rather be allowed to run wild and pick up my living than be kept in your gilded cage. And the only thing I don’t like about Mr. Samson﻿—.”

“Who’s Mr. Samson?”

“Nobody seems to know Mr. Samson and he’s an extraordinary character. I really think I like him as well as Robert Corder,” Hannah said thoughtfully. “But he keeps birds in cages, and you know about the robin redbreast in a cage, don’t you, Mr. Blenkinsop? He puts all Heaven in a rage and that’s how I feel when I think of you in the bank. From the first moment I saw you, looking through the kitchen window﻿—I’m sorry, I didn’t mean to refer to that again﻿—you reminded me of a bird. My favourite bird,” she said, but she would not tell him what that was and, as she looked at him, she decided that he was not so owlish as she had thought. The spectacles gave him most of his solemnity, his firmly shut mouth seemed to mark his determination to support them, and Hannah wished she dared ask him to take them off.

“You talk a lot of nonsense, don’t you?” he asked patiently. “Does Mr. Corder understand it?”

“I shouldn’t think of trying to find out,” she replied. “Mr. Corder’s mind moves in different spheres from mine and I just accept the fact.”

===Chinese===

“我猜，跟我差不多的年纪吧。”

汉娜摇摇头。“老上几百岁呢，布伦金索普先生。照我们计算光阴的法子，兴许只差这么几年；可这些年头，你关在银行你那铁栏杆后头，我却是一回回挤进人家家里，又给挤了出来。有趣极了！”她慌忙找补一句。“我宁可由着我野马似的跑、东拣西凑地糊口，也不愿给圈进你那镀金的笼子。桑普森先生只有一样叫我看不惯——”

“桑普森先生是谁？”

“好像谁都不认识桑普森先生，他是个奇人。说真的，我觉得我喜欢他不亚于喜欢罗伯特·科德，”汉娜若有所思地说，“可他把鸟关在笼子里。你可知道那只关在笼里的红胸知更鸟的典故吧，布伦金索普先生？它一进笼，整个天堂都要震怒；我一想到你坐在银行里，就是这份心情。从我头一眼望见你起——隔着厨房窗子望进去的那一回，对不起，我不是存心又提这桩的——你就叫我想起一只鸟。我最喜欢的那一种，”她说，却死活不肯告诉他是哪一种。她端相着他，断定他并不像她原先想的那样一副猫头鹰神气。他那份庄重，多半是那副眼镜给的；他紧闭的嘴，仿佛表明他铁了心要撑住这副眼镜；汉娜直恨自己不敢开口请他把眼镜摘下来。

“你净说些没影儿的话，是不是？”他耐着性子问，“科德先生听得懂吗？”

“我连想都没想去弄清楚，”她答道，“科德先生的头脑，同我的运行在不同的天域里，这个事实我照单全收就是了。”

===Original===

She smiled trustfully at Mr. Blenkinsop, who said, “I should hope it does.”

“So does Mr. Corder,” Miss Mole said demurely and, to her great surprise, Mr. Blenkinsop let out a modest burst of laughter.

“I didn’t know you could do it,” she said.

“What?” Mr. Blenkinsop asked, preparing to be affronted.

“I didn’t know you could laugh and I don’t know why you did.”

“Because you meant me to,” he said mournfully, “and I haven’t had many chances.”

===Chinese===

她朝布伦金索普先生信赖地一笑。他说：“理当如此。”

“科德先生的头脑也是如此，”莫尔小姐端端正正地说。使她大为惊异的是，布伦金索普先生竟放出一小阵很有分寸的哈哈。

“我不知道你还会这个，”她说。

“什么？”布伦金索普先生问，先就摆好了要动气的架势。

“我不知道你会笑，也不知道你方才为什么要笑。”

“因为你存心叫我笑，”他怏怏地说，“再说，这样的机会我也一向不多。”

===Original===

“You should make them.”

“And I’m worried.”

“Ah!” Hannah said. “Can’t you balance the cash properly, or whatever it is you do in a bank?”

“I can’t balance Mr. Corder’s religious views with my own.”

“Is that all? Who could? I shouldn’t worry about that. I doubt whether he knows what his are.”

“But I’m a member of his church.”

===Chinese===

“机会该自己造。”

“再说我在发愁。”

“啊！”汉娜说，“怎么，现金的账轧不平了，还是你们银行里那点营生干不利索？”

“我轧不平的是科德先生的宗教见解同我自己的见解。”

“就为这个？谁轧得平？这愁不必发。只怕连他自己都不知道他那套见解是些什么。”

“可我是他教会的会众。”

===Original===

“You can retire, I suppose?”

“I’m going to,” said Mr. Blenkinsop. “When my mother was alive, I let things drift. It didn’t seem worth while to upset her, but lately I’ve come to the conclusion that I’m not honest. The last sermon I heard him preach, on the subject of marriage, well, to put it plainly, it made me sick.”

“Too lax?” Hannah suggested.

“Too idiotic,” Mr. Blenkinsop said resignedly, and Hannah, taking a long time over her next stitch, asked carefully, “When was it? I must have missed that one.”

“Oh, one evening, a few weeks ago.”

“I see,” Hannah said, and she paused in her work to connect Mr. Blenkinsop’s views on marriage with the complicated love affair of which she suspected him. “And you’ve come tonight to tell him what you think.”

===Chinese===

“退出来总可以的吧？”

“我正要退，”布伦金索普先生说，“我母亲在世的时候，我听凭事情拖着；为了让她心绪不宁，似乎不值得。可近来我得出了一个结论：我不诚实。上一回听他讲道，题目是婚姻——唉，直说了吧，听得我直恶心。”

“太宽了？”汉娜猜道。

“蠢透了，”布伦金索普先生听天由命地说。汉娜把下一针纳了好半晌才纳下去，小心地问：“那是什么时候？这一回我必定是错过了。”

“哦，几个礼拜前的一个晚上。”

“原来如此，”汉娜说，手里的活儿停了一停，把布伦金索普先生对婚姻的见解，同她疑心他陷进去的那桩纠葛的爱情对上了号。“这么说，你今晚就是来把你的想法当面告诉他的。”

===Original===

“No. I don’t want to lose my temper. I’m going to write to him. Argument would be a waste of time.”

“Well, I’m glad you’ve warned me. It will be a stormy day, I’m afraid.”

“Will it really? I’m sorry. But don’t you think I ought to do it?”

“I don’t feel so keenly as you do about marriage, Mr. Blenkinsop.”

“I didn’t say I felt particularly keenly.”

“No, you didn’t say so,” Hannah said, with an irritating smile.

===Chinese===

“不是。我不想发脾气。我要写信给他。争辩是白费时间。”

“那好，多亏你先给我通了气。那一天只怕少不了一场风暴。”

“真的会？我很抱歉。可你不觉得我应当这么做吗？”

“婚姻这件事，我没有你感受得那么切肤，布伦金索普先生。”

“我没说我感受得特别切肤。”

“是啊，你是没说，”汉娜说，脸上带着叫人来火的笑。

===Original===

“That simply happened to be the subject that brought things to a head. I differ fundamentally from Mr. Corder and the doctrines he has to teach.”

“Exactly,” Hannah said, “so you’d better declare your independence as quickly as possible. You’ll feel more comfortable, won’t you? How’s the chess getting on?”

“I knew you’d ask that, sooner or later,” he said, trying not to smile.

“And the country walks?” Hannah persisted, “You want to hide your light under a bushel, but Mrs. Gibson brings it out. You’re doing good by stealth and blushing to have it known.”

“No,” said Mr. Blenkinsop with an effort, “I’m afraid my motives are not altogether unselfish,” and he looked as if he would have said more if he had not heard someone entering the hall.

Hannah hastily put away her sewing and wished she could do the same with Mr. Blenkinsop. “Every room,” she said, looking at him with twinkling eyes, “ought to have two doors. If this is Mr. Corder, what are you going to do?”

===Chinese===

“那不过是碰巧成了把事情逼到摊牌地步的题目罢了。我同科德先生、同他不得不传的那些教义，有着根本的分歧。”

“一点不错，”汉娜说，“所以你顶好尽快宣布独立。这样你会自在些，对不对？棋下得怎么样了？”

“我就知道这个问题你早晚要问，”他说，竭力忍住不笑。

“还有乡间散步呢？”汉娜追着问，“你想把自己的光藏在斗底下，可吉布森太太偏叫它亮出来。你这是暗中行善，又臊得怕人知道。”

“不，”布伦金索普先生费了番劲才说出口，“只怕我的动机并不全然无私，”他看上去还有下文，却听见有人进了门厅。

汉娜急忙把针线收起来，心想布伦金索普先生要是也能这么一收就好了。“每一间屋子，”她说，眼睛里闪着光，“都该有两扇门。要是来的是科德先生，你打算怎么办？”

===Original===

“I shall say good evening and walk out.”

“And what about me?”

“You?”

“He won’t approve of finding a young spinster like me alone in the house with a single gent.”

“Then he’ll have to put up with it,” Mr. Blenkinsop said, and Hannah was telling him that so bold a spirit was wasted on a bank when Robert Corder opened the door.

===Chinese===

“我说一声晚上好，然后走出去。”

“那我呢？”

“你？”

“让他撞见一个我这样的年轻老姑娘，同一个单身先生单独待在屋里，他会看不惯的。”

“那他也只好忍着，”布伦金索普先生说。汉娜正告诉他，这样一副胆魄给银行使唤真是屈才，说话间罗伯特·科德推开了门。

## XL / 第四十章

===Original===
“I have just come back from Beresford Road,” he said. “They told me you were out.”

“They?” Hannah said anxiously.

Mr. Blenkinsop smiled. “A loose expression! I only saw the servant. How did we miss each other?”

“I’ve been for a walk,” Hannah said, watching Mr. Blenkinsop hanging up his coat and hat.

===Chinese===
“我刚从贝雷斯福德路回来，”他说。“他们说你出门了。”

“他们？”汉娜急急地问。

布伦金索普先生微微一笑。“这话说得笼统了！我只见到了用人。咱们俩怎么会错过的呢？”

“我出去散步来着，”汉娜说，眼睛望着布伦金索普先生挂大衣和帽子。

===Original===
“You oughtn’t to do that, at this time of night. And nothing on your head! It’s quite wet,” he said in annoyance.

“What time is it?” They spoke in low tones, careful for the slumbers of Mrs. Gibson and the little maid and the people in the basement.

“Ten o’clock.”

“Then I oughtn’t to be here, either. I must go back.” The smile of the alert Miss Mole was weak and wavering, like a nervous child’s. “I don’t really know why I came at all,” she said, and she looked at him as though she expected him to explain her action. “I shall be locked out. I forgot to bring my latchkey.”

===Chinese===
“这样的时辰可不该出去走动。头上还什么都不戴！外头湿得很呢，”他气恼地说。

“现在几点钟了？”两人说话都压低了嗓音，唯恐搅扰了吉布森太太、小使女，还有地下室里那些人的酣睡。

“十点了。”

“这么说，我也不该在这儿。我得回去了。”机敏的莫尔小姐，这会儿笑得又弱又晃，像个胆小的孩子。“我实在不知道自己干吗要来，”她说，一面望着他，倒像要他来解释她的举动似的。“我会被锁在外头的。我忘了带门钥匙。”

===Original===
“Then we shall have to trouble Mr. Corder to let us in,” Mr. Blenkinsop said.

That pronoun had an extraordinarily friendly sound in Hannah’s ears and she repeated it. “We’d better go now.”

“No. Come upstairs. I’m going to make some tea. You’re cold and wet.”

“But﻿—” Hannah began, and Mr. Blenkinsop said severely. “Just try to forget there are any such people as the Corders. I’ll go up first and turn on the light.”

===Chinese===
“那咱们就只好烦劳科德先生来给咱们开门了，”布伦金索普先生说。

那个代词听在汉娜耳中，响着一种分外亲热的调子；她把它照原样又念了一遍。“咱们最好这就走吧。”

“不。上楼来。我去沏点茶。你又冷又湿。”

“可是——”汉娜刚起了个头，布伦金索普先生便板起脸说：“你只管把科德家那些人忘个一干二净。我先上去开灯。”

===Original===
Mr. Blenkinsop’s room was warm and glowing with firelight and a shaded lamp, and there was not a sound in the house but the lapping of the fire and not a sound in the street. A dreary peace stole over Hannah, an indifference to duty and disaster, and she went towards one of the deep armchairs in an unreasonable conviction that if she could once get between its arms she need never get out again.

“Take off your coat, first,” Mr. Blenkinsop said. He was busy at a cupboard, getting out cups and saucers and a cannister of tea.

“My poor old coat!” Hannah said with a vague laugh. Months ago she had promised Ruth not to wear it. “But it will have to last for a long time yet,” she said to herself. She leaned back and shut her eyes and listened to Mr. Blenkinsop’s movements, to the change in the notes of the kettle, and then the hissing sound as tea and water met, and she did not open them until he said, “There, drink that.”

===Chinese===
布伦金索普先生的屋子暖融融的，炉火和一盏罩着灯罩的灯烘出满室暖红；满屋只听得见炉火轻轻舐焰的低响，街上也悄无声息。一种凄清的安宁悄悄漫上汉娜心头，对职责也好，对灾祸也好，一概漠然了；她朝一张深陷的扶手椅走过去，心里存着一个不讲道理的确信：只要能在那两只扶手中间坐进去，从此就再不必出来了。

“先把大衣脱了，”布伦金索普先生说。他正在碗柜跟前忙着，往外拿杯碟和一只茶叶罐。

“我这件可怜的旧大衣！”汉娜含含糊糊地笑着说道。几个月前，她答应过露丝不再穿它。“可它还得再撑上好长一阵子呢，”她暗自说。她往椅背上一靠，闭上眼睛，听布伦金索普先生张罗的响动，听水壶的声音渐渐变了调，随后又是茶叶碰上滚水时的嘶嘶声；直到他说了句：“好了，喝吧。”她才睁开眼睛。

===Original===
Suddenly she was awake, remembering when she had last seen Mr. Blenkinsop, and urgent with all the things she ought to say to him before she went away. “Have you taken that cottage?” she said.

“No. I was going to ask you if you’d like to sell it.”

“Not to you,” she said quickly.

“I don’t want it. I’ve found another that will suit us better, I think.”

“But how﻿—” she was realising that she had not told him the house was hers. It was natural for him to know, and right, but she had not told him. “How do you know it belongs to me?” she asked, and though it was right for him to know, her eyes were wide and her mouth dropped piteously.

===Chinese===
她猛然全醒了，记起上回见布伦金索普先生是在什么时候，心里火烧火燎，急着要在离开以前，把该对他说的话统统说出来。“那座小屋，你租下了吗？”她说。

“没有。我正想问你，愿不愿意把它卖掉。”

“卖也不卖给你，”她赶紧说。

“我不想要它。我另外找着一处，我想那处更合咱们用。”

“可是你怎么——”她这才醒悟过来，自己从没告诉过他，那房子是她的。他知道，原是自然，也是应当，可她没有说过。“你怎么知道房子是我的？”她问；尽管他理应知道，她的眼睛还是睁得老大，嘴也可怜巴巴地耷拉下来。

===Original===
“I’ve been there again,” he said with a slight embarrassment, but a steady look. “I don’t like breaking appointments,” and at this description of the chase she had given him, she laughed without much mirth.

Mr. Blenkinsop responded with a smile and then, quietly, looking at his well-shod feet, he said, “I’ve turned the fellow out.”

Like an arrow from a bow, Hannah’s thin body darted forward, a hand on each arm of her chair, and the last embers of her loyalty leapt into a blaze under the indignant breath with which she cried, “How dare you? How dare you? What business was it of yours to interfere?”

Without raising his head, Mr. Blenkinsop turned it towards the fire. “Somebody had to do it,” he said mildly. “You see, when it came to talking business, he couldn’t produce a deed or a lease and he isn’t a very competent liar. In the end, he had to refer me to the owner so I just told him he’d better go.”

===Chinese===
“我又到那儿去过一趟，”他说，神色间略略有些发窘，目光却是定的。“我不爱叫约会落空。”听他这样形容自己挨的那场追逐，她笑了，笑得却没什么快活。

布伦金索普先生报以一笑，跟着垂下眼睛，望着自己脚上那双体面的皮鞋，轻轻说道：“我把那家伙撵走了。”

汉娜瘦削的身子像离弦的箭一般朝前一冲，两手各扣住椅子的一只扶手，忠心的最后一堆余烬，在她义愤的一口气里腾地蹿成火苗，她嚷道：“你怎么敢？你怎么敢？干你什么事，要你来插手？”

布伦金索普先生没有抬头，只把脸转向炉火。“总得有人来做这件事，”他心平气和地说。“你想，一谈到正经交易，他拿不出一张地契，也拿不出一张租约，扯起谎来又不高明。末了他只得叫我找业主去，我便直截了当，叫他趁早走人。”

===Original===
“Then you can just go back and tell him he can stay.”

“Oh, he’s gone by this time, and the man who owns the farm is willing to buy the place. Somebody has to look after you,” he explained patiently.

Hannah stood up and reached out blindly for her coat. “But not you,” she said, and her voice seemed to come from the very source of sorrow. “Isn’t there anybody in the world who won’t trample on the few things I’ve had?” she asked plaintively. “All these people﻿—why must they? And you﻿—I didn’t think you would do it.” Her anger took hold of her again. “What right had you to interfere?” she repeated. Then pure pain overcame her anger and, she said, “It doesn’t matter. It doesn’t matter, but I didn’t think you would do it. I didn’t think you would try to find out why I ran away.” Again she put out her hands for her coat, but they did not meet it and she sat down again, as though she had forgotten what she meant to do.

===Chinese===
“那你这就回去，告诉他，就说他可以留下好了。”

“哦，这会儿他早走了。农场的主人倒愿意把这地方买下来。总得有人照看你，”他耐心地解释道。

汉娜站起身，伸手盲目地去够她的大衣。“可不能是你，”她说，那声音像是打悲伤最深的源头里发出来的。“这世上难道就没有一个人，肯放过我仅有过的那几样东西吗？”她哀哀地问。“这许多人——何苦呢？还有你——我原当你不会这样。”怒气又把她攫住了。“你有什么权利来干涉？”她又说了一遍。跟着，纯粹的痛楚压过了怒气，她说：“没关系。没关系，可我原当你不会这样。我原当你不会去打听，我干吗要逃走。”她又伸出两手去够大衣，两只手却没碰着它，她又坐下了，仿佛忘了自己方才要干什么。

===Original===
“What else could I do?” he said simply. “I told you I couldn’t leave it at that. You were in trouble and you wouldn’t tell me what it was. I see now that I ought not to have gone without asking you, but I’m glad I did. No, I ought not to have gone, but when I went I thought there might be something I could do for you. All sorts of queer ideas came into my head and I never thought, I never thought for a moment﻿—.”

Hannah dropped her hands from her face and he saw the familiar, teasing smile. “And yet it ought to have been the first thing that occurred to you.”

“Ought it? I suppose I’m stupid. You’ll have to forgive me. I saw he was trying to rob you, or not caring whether he did or not, but I didn’t turn him out until﻿—until﻿—”

“No, no,” Hannah mourned. “Don’t tell me. Don’t tell me anything he said. Is there no end to it? I didn’t mind your knowing about it, but I didn’t want you to see him. That’s why I ran away. I didn’t want you to see him and now you’ve seen him and talked to him. You’ve seen the kind of man I loved﻿—and lived with. It was the only thing, the only thing about me I didn’t want you to know﻿—the kind of man﻿—and even that I couldn’t keep. I can’t keep anything! Oh, let me go back. I must go back.” With a tremendous effort at self-command she changed her voice to one of acid amusement. “It seems to me that your affection for one woman has made rather a busybody of you, Mr. Blenkinsop.”

===Chinese===
“不这样，我又能怎样？”他简简单单地说。“我对你说过的，这件事我不能就这么撂开。你有难处，又不肯告诉我是什么难处。如今我明白，我不该不问你一声就去；可我又高兴我去了。不，我不该去，只是去的时候，我想着也许有什么是我能替你做的。脑子里转过各式各样古怪的念头，就是没有想过，一刻也没有想过——”

汉娜把捂着脸的手放了下来，他看见了那熟悉的、带着打趣的笑。“可这原该是你头一个想到的呀。”

“原该吗？想来我是笨。你只好包涵了。我看出他存心讹你的钱，要不就是讹不讹全不在乎，可我没有撵他走，直到——直到——”

“不，不，”汉娜哀声说。“别告诉我。他说的什么，一个字也别告诉我。这件事难道就没有个完么？你知道了这回事，我不介意；可我不要你见到他。我逃走，就为的这个。我不要你见到他，这会儿你见了，还同他谈了话。你见着了我爱过的那种男人——还同他过了日子的那种男人。这是唯一的一桩，唯一一桩我不愿意叫你知道的事——那种男人——连这个我也留不住。我什么都留不住！哦，让我回去吧。我非回去不可。”她拼足力气管住自己，把声音换成一种又酸又带笑谑的腔调。“照我看，你为着一个女人动了情，倒把自己弄成个不折不扣的爱管闲事的人了，布伦金索普先生。”

===Original===
“I’m afraid that’s true,” he said. “Drink your tea. There’s no hurry. I want to tell you about the other cottage we’ve got hold of. It belongs to Ridding’s brother-in-law. He’s a farmer and he’ll be able to keep an eye on Ridding and help him with his fowls.” He was looking at the fire, avoiding her eyes, and he did not see astonishment gradually spreading over her face. “Ridding will be better in the country. An office isn’t the place for a man like that, and Mrs. Ridding thinks it will suit the baby. They’ll be going in about a fortnight and it’s a great load off my mind,” he ended with a deep sigh.

“Take my cup,” Hannah said in a strangled voice. “Take my cup. I shall spill the tea. I’m going to laugh. But I can’t!” she cried after a moment. “Oh, what’s going to happen to me if I can’t laugh any more?”

“You’re tired out,” he said.

===Chinese===
“这话说得怕是真的，”他说。“把茶喝了。不忙着走。我想同你说说咱们另外弄到手的那处小屋。屋主是里丁的内兄。他是个庄稼人，可以照应里丁，还能帮他养鸡。”他望着炉火，避开她的眼睛，没有看见惊愕正一点一点爬满她的脸。“里丁到了乡下会好起来的。坐办公室那样的营生，本不合他那种人，里丁太太也觉得乡下对娃娃好。他们约摸两个星期里动身，我心上的一块大石头可算落了地，”他长长叹了一口气，收了话。

“把我的杯子接过去，”汉娜的声音憋得紧紧的。“把我的杯子接过去。茶要泼出来了。我要笑了。可我笑不出来！”过了一会儿她喊道。“哦，往后我要是再也不会笑了，那我可怎么办呢？”

“你是累坏了，”他说。

===Original===
“Yes, but it isn’t that.” She looked about her, seeking an explanation. “It must be because it isn’t really funny,” she said in a low, puzzled voice. “You see, I thought you were in love with Mrs. Ridding. I thought the cottage was for you and her.”

“Good God!” Mr. Blenkinsop exclaimed in horror, and again Hannah had the sensation that her heart was shrinking to the size of a pea. He was kind to her, as he was kind to Mrs. Ridding, but this was what he thought of such love affairs as hers, and now she stood up with a deceptive briskness.

“And that,” she said, in a hard voice, “just shows you what kind of a mind I’ve got. I suspect everybody else of what I did myself! And now I must go back, for Ruth may want me.”

“She can’t want you as much as I do,” Mr. Blenkinsop said quietly, in unmistakable accents.

===Chinese===
“是啊，可不是为这个。”她朝四下里张望，要找出个缘由来。“一定是因为，这件事其实并不好笑，”她低低地说，声音里满心困惑。“你瞧，我原当你是爱上里丁太太了。我当那小屋是给你跟她置下的。”

“我的天！”布伦金索普先生骇然失声，汉娜又觉出那种感觉，仿佛她的心正缩成一粒豌豆。他待她好，也正像他待里丁太太好；可是对她那一路的爱情，他心里原来是这样的看法。她当下站起身来，带着一副装出来的爽利劲。

“这一层嘛，”她的声音硬起来，“正好叫你瞧瞧，我长的是一颗什么样的心。我自己做下的事，就疑心人人都做！好了，如今我可得回去了，露丝兴许用得着我。”

“她要你，决计要不到我要你的地步，”布伦金索普先生轻轻地说，声口里再没有半点含糊。

===Original===
Hannah stood quite still. She clung to her coat, but it dropped out of her hand, and she said slowly, addressing the wall in front of her, “This isn’t true.”

“Yes, it’s true,” he said. “That’s why I’ve been bothering about the Riddings, to do something I thought would please you. And if you’re going to say you don’t care about me﻿—”

“But I’m not!” Hannah cried, with a wide, tremulous smile. “I’m not! Don’t talk to me for a little while. Don’t say anything,” she begged and Mr. Blenkinsop was obediently silent while she lay back in her chair, telling herself that the miracle she had believed in had really happened, it had really happened, it was here, in this room, but in a moment she started up again. “I’ll tell you what we’ll do. We’ll sell the cottage and give the money to the Riddings.”

===Chinese===
汉娜站着一动不动。她还死死攥着大衣，大衣却从手里滑落了，她慢慢地开口，对着面前的墙壁说：“这不是真的。”

“是真的，”他说。“我这些日子为里丁一家操心奔走，就为的做一件我想着会叫你欢喜的事。你要是打算说你并不在乎我——”

“可我不打算！”汉娜喊道，脸上绽开一个宽宽的、颤巍巍的笑。“我不！这一会儿先别跟我说话。什么都别说，”她央告着，布伦金索普先生便乖乖地没了声；她仰身靠回椅子里，告诉自己，她一直信着的那个奇迹当真应验了，当真应验了，就在这里，就在这间屋子里；可不多时，她又霍地直起身来。“我说，咱们这么办。把小屋卖掉，钱送给里丁一家。”

===Original===
“As a thank-offering!” he said.

“Yes,” she said, rather wistfully, “if it seems like that to you. Well, you know everything about me.”

“No,” he said, “and I don’t think I ever shall,” a speech more satisfying to Hannah than any more lover-like protestation.

===Chinese===
“权当谢恩的祭礼！”他说。

“好，”她说，语气里颇有点怅惘，“既然在你看来是那么回事。好了，我的事你全知道了。”

“不，”他说，“我想我永远也不会知道个全。”这句话落在汉娜耳中，比任何更像情话的表白都叫她受用。

===Original===
It was twelve o’clock when they walked down Beresford Road, and Hannah had no latchkey, and Mr. Blenkinsop was looking forward to his interview with Robert Corder. And, after all, Ruth need never know, Hannah thought, in great content, and Mr. Corder would be relieved of the responsibility of taking action, and Ethel would marry Mr. Pilgrim and, surely, Uncle Jim would rescue Ruth and Robert Corder would marry Patsy Withers and find her somewhat dull after the incalculableness of Miss Mole, and, for this misfortune, Lilla would find compensation in the disappearance of a cousin who would cause her no more anxiety. The miracle had happened and though, through the wonder of it, there were regrets for Ruth, Hannah had never been less inclined to doubt that everything was for the best.

Can this be me? she asked herself. She had run up the road, two hours ago, in a drizzling rain and an unbearable loneliness, and now she had hold of Mr. Blenkinsop’s hand and the stars were shining.

“We’ll go away,” he was saying, and she glanced up at him and wondered if, like herself, he saw something whimsical and unlikely in their love. She hoped he did not. She could trust herself to see it with other people’s eyes and laugh, with them, without doing it any injury, but, for him, she wished this happiness to be too solemn and beautiful for mirth.

===Chinese===
他们顺着贝雷斯福德路一路走来时，已是十二点钟；汉娜没有带门钥匙，布伦金索普先生正满心盼着同罗伯特·科德的那一场会面。而说到底，露丝根本不必知道，汉娜心满意足地想；科德先生从此卸下那副非有所行动不可的重担；埃塞尔会嫁给皮尔格林先生；吉姆舅舅呢，准会来把露丝搭救出去；罗伯特·科德会娶佩茜·威瑟斯，而在领教过莫尔小姐的深不可测之后，会觉得她有几分乏味；至于这个不幸，莉拉自会得到补偿——那个叫她悬心的表妹从此不见了，再不会叫她操一丝的心。奇迹当真发生了；虽然透过奇迹的光晕，也为露丝存着遗憾，汉娜却从来不曾像此刻这样笃信不疑：一切都是最好的安排。

这是我么？她问自己。两个钟头以前，她顶着毛毛细雨，怀着难熬的孤独，一路奔上这条街；如今她手里握着布伦金索普先生的手，天上星光正亮。

“咱们要远走高飞，”他这样说着；她抬头瞥了他一眼，心里疑惑，他是不是也同她自己一样，在这场爱情里看出几分古怪无稽、不大像真的意思。但愿他没有。借旁人的眼睛来看它，跟着旁人一道笑它，又不叫它受半点伤——这一层她信得过自己；可是在他跟前，她但愿这份幸福庄严、美丽得叫人笑不出声。

===Original===
“We’ll go away,” he said. “I’ll leave the bank. You’ve made me rather ashamed of the bank. It’s too safe.”

“But I want safety now! That’s the worst of happiness﻿—it makes you want safety. We mustn’t want it. I’ve always been afraid of wanting too much,” she said.

“Oh﻿—my poor heart!” Mr. Blenkinsop exclaimed in a broken voice, and stopped and stooped to kiss her.

===Chinese===
“咱们要远走高飞，”他说。“银行里的差事我辞掉。你叫我对银行有几分羞愧了。它太四平八稳。”

“可如今我要的正是安稳！这就是幸福顶坏的地方——它叫人想要安稳。咱们不能要。我一向就怕要得太多，”她说。

“哦——我可怜的心哪！”布伦金索普先生叫道，声音都哽住了；他停住脚，俯下身去吻她。

## XV / 第十五章

===Original===

That night, Ethel left the Girls’ Club in the charge of Miss Patsy Withers and another helper and went home earlier than usual. She had a headache and felt miserable. It was cold in the tramcar, after the heat of the Mission Room, and she wanted to be in bed with a hot-water bottle and something warm to drink, and she wished, as she wished more often than anyone suspected, that she could find her mother at home. Ethel’s loss was of a different nature from Ruth’s. For Ethel, her mother had been a quiet voice and a kind hand, a voice never raised in expostulation and a hand that knew how to put a pillow under an aching head. When she was overwrought and her mother applied physical remedies, it did not occur to Ethel that in this refusal to reprove or advise, this assumption that her child was ill, her mother was doing all she could to treat her mentally. Ethel’s life was so fiercely subjective that her mother, for her, had hardly existed objectively. Perhaps no one and nothing existed for her in that sense and inevitably she became the prey of every misadventure. Now she sat huddled in her corner, trying to keep her eyes shut to ease her headache, but each time the tramcar stopped she had to open them to see who was boarding it or alighting: she had to make a quick comparison of each woman’s clothes with her own, to wonder if she could twist her hat into the shape of the one opposite, or train her hair to fall over her ears like that of the girl who had just come in, and under this eagerness to be doing something, this restlessness which had its value, there was a weariness of the Girls’ Club and a sense of futility.

Doris had not been there, Doris, her favourite, who had been elevated to service in the Corder’s household, and was supposed to adore Miss Ethel. There had been laughter of a familiar kind when her absence was mentioned and Ethel had felt sharp tears spring into her eyes, as though she had been struck. They were laughing because they knew more about Doris than she did, and they were glad because the favourite was proved disloyal.

Ethel told herself she was too sensitive, but that was her nature. She was hurt and she was anxious and her head throbbed more violently as she pictured Doris wandering in the darkness with a young man and imagined, with the inaccuracy of theoretical knowledge, the probably disastrous progress of that courtship. She would have to speak to Doris, and she knew that, in such matters, Doris would see her as a child in arms and feed her with appropriate sops. She had felt sure of her influence over the girl and her certainty was taken from her. Life, for Ethel, was something like walking across a bog, leaping from what looked like a solid tuft to another and finding many of them shaking and some sinking under her, and she lost her nerve, and then her judgment, at each mistake. Doris’s absence and the girls’ laughter had made the friendliness of Patsy Withers the more welcome and, in a gust of grateful confidence, Ethel had talked about Miss Mole and detected a gleam of pleasure in Patsy’s eyes when she told the puzzling story about the mattresses. It had been rather silly to tell her, Ethel thought, but then, she was so impulsive; she was sensitive and impulsive, and no one understood her now except Howard﻿—and Wilfrid, when he was not teasing her. She had an unbounded admiration for her father, but she wished he had more time and patience to spare her. He had a way of making her difficulties seem very small in comparison with his own and of suggesting that he had cares enough already. There was Miss Mole﻿—Ethel felt guilty again at the thought of her﻿—who always listened with interest, but how could anyone be sure she was not another of those tufts? And there was God, she remembered hurriedly, and she looked askance at her neighbours, as though they had divined her forgetfulness. No one was looking at her and she shut her eyes again, saying she would pray, she must have more faith, and with that thought, or because the tramcar stopped at the place where she intended to get out, her troubles became less pressing.

===Chinese===

那天夜里，埃塞尔把少女俱乐部托付给佩茜·威瑟斯小姐和另一位帮手，比平日提早回了家。她头疼，心里也难受。布道会活动室里热烘烘的，上了电车反倒冷了；她只想躺到床上去，怀里抱个热水袋，手里再有一样热乎的东西喝；她盼着一进家门就能见到母亲——这个愿望她在心里许的次数，比任何人猜想的都要多。埃塞尔所失，同露丝所失，性质并不相同。母亲之于埃塞尔，是一道安静的声音、一只和善的手：那声音从不提高来劝诫她，那只手懂得往疼痛的脑袋底下垫个枕头。每当她神经过度紧张，母亲便施以身体上的调治；埃塞尔却从未想到，母亲这样不肯责备、不肯规劝，只认定她的孩子是病了，正是在竭尽所能，医治她的心灵。埃塞尔的日子过得太死地以自我为轴心，母亲在她那里，几乎不曾作为一个客观的存在。在那层意义上，或许于她谁都不存在、什么都不存在；于是每一次不幸临头，她都无从躲闪，只能束手就擒。此刻她蜷缩在车角，极力阖着眼，好让头疼轻些；可是电车每停一站，她都得睁开眼来看看谁上了车、谁下了车：她得飞快把每位太太的衣裳同自己的一一比较，琢磨自己能不能把帽子拧成对座那顶的样式，或者把头发练得像刚上车的那个姑娘那样，垂下来盖住耳朵；而在这股想做点什么的热切底下，在这种自有其价值的躁动底下，藏着的却是对少女俱乐部的倦怠，和一种徒劳之感。

多丽丝没在场。多丽丝，她的宠儿——如今已高升到科德家帮佣，人人都当她崇拜埃塞尔小姐——偏偏没在场。一提起她的缺席，照例响起一阵熟悉的哄笑，埃塞尔只觉尖锐的泪水涌上眼眶，像挨了一击似的。她们笑，是因为关于多丽丝，她们知道得比她多；她们乐，是因为这位宠儿原来并不忠心。

埃塞尔对自己说，她是太敏感了，可敏感正是她的天性。她受了伤，心里又悬着；一想到多丽丝正同一个年轻人在黑暗里游荡，她的头疼便擂得更凶，凭着那点纸上谈兵、不走准的理论，她把那场恋爱的进程想象成多半要以灾祸收场。她少不得要同多丽丝谈一谈；而她也知道，这种事上，多丽丝会把她看作襁褓里的娃娃，拿合口的软话像喂泡面包似的哄她。她本来笃信自己对这姑娘有影响力，这份笃信如今被夺走了。生活之于埃塞尔，很像横越一片沼泽：从一块看似结实的草墩跳向另一块，却发现许多块都在晃，有几块正在她脚底下往下沉；每错一步，她先失了胆气，跟着又失了判断。多丽丝的缺席、姑娘们的哄笑，倒把佩茜·威瑟斯的和气衬得格外可亲；埃塞尔怀着满腔感激的信赖，讲起了莫尔小姐，讲到那个叫人纳闷的床垫故事时，她瞥见佩茜眼里闪过一丝快活。把这些讲给她听，未免有点傻，埃塞尔心想；可她就是这么爱冲动；她又敏感又爱冲动，如今除了霍华德——还有威尔弗里德，只要他不来取笑她——没有谁懂她。她对父亲怀着无限的敬慕，可她但愿父亲能多匀出一点时间和耐心给她。他有本事让她那些难处一与他自己的相比就显得无足轻重，还有本事叫人觉出，他的烦忧已经够多了。还有莫尔小姐——一想到她，埃塞尔又觉出心虚——她听人说话总是兴味盎然，可谁能担保她不是又一个草墩呢？还有上帝——她慌忙记起——便斜眼瞟了瞟邻座们，仿佛他们已窥破她的健忘。谁也没有看她，她又阖上眼，对自己说要做祷告，她必须有更多的信心；这么一想，或者因为电车恰好停在她要下的那一站，她的烦忧便不那么咄咄逼人了。

===Original===

She could have gone on to the halt at the end of Beresford Road, but this one was at the end of University Walk and once, as she walked back from the club, Wilfrid had overtaken her there and they had gone home together, by way of Prince’s Road, because Wilfrid had said it was more romantic, and then he had spoilt her happiness by asking why she walked so slowly.

Wilfrid was one of Ethel’s tufts, but so bright, so alluring, that she could not believe it would betray her, and she had found her own way of explaining its instability. It was not altogether satisfactory and she knew it, but it served for consolation when her faith in herself and in the charms she hoped she possessed, was tottering. Wilfrid and she were cousins and when he was unkind to her it was when he remembered that relationship. He had to repress her feelings and hide his own under his banter. It was noble of him, but Ethel would have been happier if there could have been one passionate scene which would have become a sacred memory, casting its pale light over the rest of their barren lives, and thinking of Wilfrid thus, hoping to hear his gay voice behind her and trying to walk as she would like him to see her walking, she followed the route they had taken together, up Prince’s Road, dim and wide and quiet, with the shadows of bare branches outlined on the pavement in the lamplight.

The drizzling rain of the afternoon had stopped, there was a starry sky above her, and though she was not moved by beauty, the influence of the place and hour was soothing and she went slowly, forgetting her troubles, hardly thinking, but letting little plans and hopes﻿—her dress for the Spenser-Smiths’ party, the altering of her hat, a cup of cocoa beside the fire and a few words with Wilfrid before she went to bed﻿—flit in pictures across her mind, and then, at the angle made by the junction of Prince’s and Beresford Roads, she stopped for a dreadful moment before she turned and ran.

===Chinese===

她本可以坐到贝雷斯福德路尽头那一站再下，可这一站恰在大学步道（University Walk）的尽头；有一回，她从俱乐部往回走，威尔弗里德就在那里追上了她，两人便一同回家，打王子路绕过去，因为威尔弗里德说那条路更浪漫；随后他又问她干吗走得那么慢，把她的欢喜给毁了。

威尔弗里德也是埃塞尔的草墩之一，可它太亮、太诱人，她不肯相信它会塌陷，还自有一套说法，来解释它的不稳。那说法并不全然叫她满意，这一点她自己也明白，可每逢她对自己的信心、对她自盼拥有的那些魅力摇摇欲坠时，它总还能拿来当个安慰。威尔弗里德同她是表兄妹；他待她刻薄，恰恰是在他记起这层关系的时候。他不得不压一压她的感情，再把自己的感情藏进打趣里。这固然高洁；可若能有一场炽烈的戏，化作一段神圣的记忆，把它淡淡的余光洒在两人此后贫瘠的岁月上，埃塞尔原会更幸福些。她这样想着威尔弗里德，盼着身后响起他欢快的话音，又竭力走出她愿意让他看见的步态，便沿着他们同走过的那条路上了王子路——路又宽又静，朦朦胧胧，灯光把光秃秃的枝影描在人行道上。

下午那场毛毛雨已经停了，头顶上满天星斗；她虽不为美景所动，此地此时的影响力却抚慰人心。她慢慢地走，忘了烦忧，几乎什么也不想，只由着一些小小的心愿和指望——为斯宾塞-史密斯家聚会备下的衣裳、要改的帽子、炉边一杯可可、睡觉前同威尔弗里德说的几句话——化作一幅幅图画掠过心头。后来，在王子路与贝雷斯福德路交汇拐出的那个转角上，她猛地站住，挨过可怕的一瞬，随即转身飞奔。

===Original===

Like Ruth, earlier in the day, she was strangling her sobs﻿—and she was happier than Ruth, because she was not pursued by her own disloyalty, but she was also more miserable, because, with what she called Wilfrid’s faithlessness, her world was darkened. The stars had gone out when she saw him a few yards ahead of her, beyond the turning, holding a girl’s hand as though, when she offered it in farewell, he could not let it go. There was no mistaking Wilfrid’s bare head and his slim figure leaning backwards as he held the girl’s hand at arm’s length, to see her better, perhaps, or to draw her to him, and as Ethel ran, she was caught by a greater pain than that of seeing Wilfrid’s alliance with another, though the two were mingled; it was the primitive pain of being undesired by any man and the conviction﻿—acknowledged in the moment’s misery﻿—that no one would ever hold her hand in that half-playful, lingering grasp.

Centuries of loneliness seemed to pass over her before she reached the garden gate and saw, through her blurred eyes, one bulky figure, standing there, changed into two. She rushed past them. She had left Wilfrid, holding a girl’s hand, to find Doris in a young man’s arms, and they had both betrayed her. She sped up the path and banged the door in the face of Doris who was following behind her; she flung open the door of the dining-room and saw Ruth and Miss Mole smiling at each other across the hearthrug and for an instant she stood there before she turned and went stumblingly, noisily, up the stairs to her room.

The sight of her, distraught and angry, remained like a material object in the doorway, and when Hannah looked at Ruth she saw that her face was white.

===Chinese===

同白天的露丝一样，她把呜咽扼在喉咙里——她比露丝还幸运些，因为追赶她的不是她自己的不忠；可她又比露丝更苦，因为在她所谓威尔弗里德的负心面前，她的世界暗了下来。等她望见他时，星星已经隐灭了。他就在前面几码开外、转过弯去的地方，拉着一位姑娘的手，仿佛那姑娘道别时把手递过来，他便怎么也舍不得放开。威尔弗里德那颗光着的脑袋、那瘦削的身子决计错认不了：他把姑娘的手直伸到手臂尽头拉着，身子朝后仰着，也许为了把她看个真切，也许要把她拽向自己。埃塞尔奔跑着，一股比眼见威尔弗里德同别人相好更烈的疼痛攫住了她，尽管两者本混作一处；那是一种最原始的疼痛：没有一个男人渴慕她；并且在那一刻的悲苦里，她认下了一个定论：永远不会有人用那种半带戏谑、又恋恋不舍的握法，来握她的手。

仿佛几百年的孤独从她身上碾过，她才走到自家园门口，隔着泪眼望见一个粗壮的身影立在那里，一变而为两个。她从他们身边直冲过去。她丢下了拉着姑娘手的威尔弗里德，回来却撞见多丽丝正偎在一个年轻人怀里——他们两个都背叛了她。她飞奔上小径，砰地把门摔在紧跟在后面的多丽丝脸上；她一把推开餐室的门，看见露丝和莫尔小姐正隔着炉前毯相视而笑；她在原地站了一刹那，随即转身，跌跌撞撞、乒乓作响地奔上楼梯，进了自己的房间。

她那副心烦意乱、怒气冲冲的模样，像一件有形有质的东西，留在了门口；汉娜去看露丝时，只见她的脸白了。

===Original===

“Oh, what can have happened now?” she moaned.

Hannah had no answer ready, and a knock at the front door called her to open it. There stood Doris, her head flung up, her meekly virtuous expression changed to one of defiance.

“He’s a steady, respectable young man,” she said, “and if he wasn’t it would be all the same! I’ve as good a right to walk out as anybody else, and more chance than some I could name, and so I’m willing to tell her at her convenience.”

“Bless my soul!” Hannah said mildly, looking the little maid up and down, and what she left out of her voice, she put into that cool glance. “You trot up to bed and I’ll talk to you in the morning,” she said, and Doris went. Hannah twisted her nose in satisfaction. It was a good thing there was someone capable of command in a house inhabited by one young woman who was distraught, another who was defiant, and a child who looked ready to faint.

“Life in the happy Nonconformist home!” she thought. “This would do Mr. Blenkinsop a power of good,” and she stepped outside the door and a few paces down the path, to taste the freshness of the night before she went back to Ruth. She could still see Ethel’s face clearly against the darkness. It was the face of one who ragingly but helplessly had watched murder done, and almost mechanically, but with a grim smile, Hannah cast her eyes about for the corpse.

===Chinese===

“哦，这又出了什么事啦？”她哀叹道。

汉娜一时答不上来，前门的一声敲响唤她去开门。门外站着多丽丝，头高高扬起，那副温顺贤良的神情换成了满脸不服。

“他是个稳重、体面的年轻人，”她说，“就算不是，也照样一个样！我出门走走，权利同谁都一样，有些人我叫得出名字，我的机会比他们还多些；这话她几时方便，我就几时乐意当面讲给她听。”

“我的老天爷！”汉娜和和气气地说，把小女佣从头到脚打量一遍；她声音里省下的那点东西，全数放进了那道冷冷的目光。“你乖乖跑上楼睡觉去，明儿早上我再同你谈，”她说；多丽丝便去了。汉娜满意地扭了扭鼻子。一幢房子里住着一位心慌意乱的年轻女人、一位桀骜不服的年轻女人，外加一个眼看就要晕倒的孩子，里头能有一个人镇得住场子，真是件好事。

“这才叫非国教的快乐家庭呢！”她心想。“这可够布伦金索普先生受用一场的，”她走出房门，顺着小径下了几步，先尝一口夜的清新，再回去陪露丝。黑暗里，埃塞尔那张脸她仍看得清清楚楚：那是一张暴怒而无助、眼睁睁看着凶案做成的脸。汉娜几乎出于机械，嘴角挂着一丝冷笑，四下张望，寻那具尸首。

===Original===

Something swift and dark curvetted past her feet and, at the same moment, she heard the thick voice of Mr. Samson in its nightly call of “Puss, Puss, Puss!”

“Your cat’s here,” she cried back, and she crossed the grass plot and looked over the dividing hedge of laurel to see Mr. Samson standing where the parrot’s cage had been. “Your cat’s here, in the garden,” she said again.

“That you, Miss Fitt?” he said in a rumble. “Catch her for me, can you?”

“Catch an eel!” Hannah said, making a dart for the kitten who was amused by the clumsiness of these human beings.

“Gently does it,” Mr. Samson advised. “You’ve got a good voice for calling a cat. I’d come and catch her myself, but I might be caught by the Reverend﻿—Ha, ha! Got her? Good! Hand her over. This’ll bring on my bronchitis again, I shouldn’t wonder. Haven’t been outside the house for a week, but I’ve had my eye on you, out of the window. ’Tisn’t much I miss. I’ve seen you running in and out, looking so perky, and off to the chapel on Sunday! Well, when you feel like it, Miss Fitt, just come in and have a talk and a look at my cats.”

===Chinese===

一个又快又黑的什么东西打她脚边腾跃而过；同一瞬间，她听见桑普森先生那副浑厚的嗓音，正发出他每晚的那声呼唤：“咪咪，咪咪，咪咪！”

“你的猫在这儿！”她回身喊道，随即跨过草地，从隔开两家的月桂树篱上望过去，只见桑普森先生站在原先搁鹦鹉笼子的地方。“你的猫在这儿，在花园里！”她又说了一遍。

“是你吗，菲特（Fitt）小姐？”他瓮声瓮气地说。“替我逮住她，成不成？”

“逮你的鳗鱼去吧！”汉娜说着，朝那只小猫猛扑过去；小猫正看这两个人类笨手笨脚，看得直乐。

“轻着点儿来，”桑普森先生指点道。“你这副嗓子，唤猫再合适不过。本来想自己出来逮她，又怕叫牧师大人给逮了去——哈哈！逮着了？好！递过来。这么一折腾，怕不又要勾起我的气管炎来。我一个礼拜没出过家门了，可在窗口一直盯着你呢。没什么能逃过我的眼睛。瞧见你跑出跑进，神气活现的，礼拜天还上礼拜堂去！好了，菲特小姐，哪天你有了兴致，只管进来坐坐，聊一聊，再看看我那些猫。”

===Original===

“Shall I? Perhaps I will, but I must go back now and look after my little girl.”

“What, the scared one? Ought to be in bed,” Mr. Samson growled.

“And you’ve got my name wrong,” Hannah said. “My name is Mole.”

“That’s a silly kind of a name,” he said indignantly. “I’ll stick to the one I’ve given you﻿—Miss Fitt! See the joke? That’s what you are and if you don’t know it you’ll soon find out.”

“Oh﻿—I see!” Hannah said, and laughed so clearly that a young man, coming whistling down the street, stopped his own music to listen.

===Chinese===

“是吗？也许我会来的。不过这会儿我得回去了，去照看我那小姑娘。”

“怎么，那个吓坏的小丫头？早该在床上躺着了，”桑普森先生嘟哝道。

“还有，你把我的姓弄错了，”汉娜说。“我姓莫尔。”

“这姓起得可真傻，”他忿忿地说。“我还就认准我给你起的这个了——菲特小姐！瞧出笑话的门道没有？不合式（misfit）——说的正是你这个人；眼下你不知道，早晚自会知道。”

“哦——原来如此！”汉娜说着，笑得那样清亮，一个吹着口哨打街上过来的年轻人，竟停了自己的调子，侧耳来听。

===Original===

Wilfrid was by her side when she reached the door and he slipped his arm into hers. “What’s the meaning of this, Mona Lisa? I heard sounds of revelry and girlish laughter. Clandestine meetings with our godless neighbour?”

“Catching his cat,” Hannah said.

“Useful things, cats,” said Wilfrid. “And dogs. I suppose the uncle isn’t in yet, but you’ve been running it rather close, you know. And don’t try to look severe, because you can’t, with love’s young dream all over your face.” He shut the door and looked round the hall as though he scented trouble. “Ethel in?” he asked carelessly.

“Yes,” said Hannah, and she gave him a sharp look.

===Chinese===

她走到门口时，威尔弗里德已经到了她身旁，把胳膊一伸，挽住了她的胳膊。“这唱的是哪一出，蒙娜丽莎？我听见欢宴之声，还有少女的笑。同咱们那位不敬上帝的邻人私会么？”

“替他逮猫呢，”汉娜说。

“猫是好使唤的东西，”威尔弗里德说。“狗也一样。舅舅想必还没到家，可你知道，你这一趟走得相当悬。还有，别想摆出一副严正面孔——你摆不出来的，你满脸都漾着爱情的青春美梦呢。”他关上门，环顾门厅，像嗅到了麻烦的气味。“埃塞尔回来了？”他漫不经心地问。

“回来了，”汉娜说着，狠狠剜了他一眼。

===Original===

He shrugged his shoulders and spread his hands. “It’s not my fault, Mona Lisa,” he said languidly, but there was dancing laughter in his eyes. “How did I know she’d come home by Prince’s Road?”

“I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Hannah said, and she went into the dining-room where Ruth was crouching by the fire, her face as sharp as a rat’s.

“You left me alone in the house,” she complained bitterly. “You needn’t have done that, need you? And Ethel might have come down and killed me.”

“Don’t be ridiculous. Come along to bed.”

“But you don’t know, you don’t know! This is the first bad one she’s had since you came. I can’t go into that room and listen to her banging. She’ll do it for hours and I can hear her.”

===Chinese===

他耸耸肩，摊开两手。“又不是我的错，蒙娜丽莎，”他有气无力地说，眼睛里却舞动着笑意。“我怎么知道她会打王子路回家？”

“我不知道你在说什么，”汉娜说着走进餐室；露丝正蜷在炉火旁，小脸尖得像只耗子。

“你把我一个人丢在屋里，”她怨苦地数落。“你原本可以不这么办的，对不对？埃塞尔没准儿正下楼来杀我呢。”

“别胡说八道了。睡觉去。”

“可你不知道，你不知道！你来了以后，这是她头一回犯得这么凶。我没法进那间屋，也没法听她砰砰砰地摔打。她能摔打上几个钟头，我听得见的。”

===Original===

“What am I to do with you all?” Hannah asked sadly.

“And we’d had such a happy evening,” Ruth went on. “It’s no good being happy. It’s better to be miserable all the time.”

“And still better to be brave. Think of me and the burglar!”
