“But there’ll have to be a rescue, of another kind, sooner or later,” he said, and Hannah told herself that he had come to the party to talk to her about Mrs. Ridding.

===Chinese===

“我看这里头没有什么好笑的。”

“兴许是没有，”汉娜和颜悦色地说，嘴唇却直抽动——她在想象他那按部就班的就寝章程：她看见他给表上弦，把衣裳叠放整齐，取出第二天用的干净领子，逐一查验那些叫他提心吊胆的纽扣；而布伦金索普先生的声音——愈发激切了——传到她耳边时，他恰正钻进被窝。

“可迟早得有一场营救，另一种营救，”他说；汉娜心想，他今晚来赴会，原就是要同她谈里丁太太（Mrs. Ridding）的事。

===Original===

“I’m afraid to offer to help,” she said, “in case I do the wrong thing again, but if I can, I will.”

“The ridiculous part of it,” he confided, “is that I believe he’d be just as happy without her.”

“Well, that’s a comfort, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===

“我不敢再主动去帮忙，”她说，“怕又帮岔了；不过只要使得上力，我一定帮。”

“这事可笑的地方，”他推心置腹地说，“是我相信没有她，他照样过得很快活。”

“咦，这不是叫人宽心么？”

===Original===

“I don’t know. I think it makes it worse.”

“You’re the best judge of that, Mr. Blenkinsop, but I should call it an extenuating circumstance.”

“These people simply suck the strength out of their relations﻿—like vampires,” Mr. Blenkinsop said, becoming fanciful under emotion. “I believe he’d be better if he had a thorough change.”

===Chinese===

“我说不好。我倒觉得这更糟。”

“这个你最有资格评判，布伦金索普先生；可要我说，这算是一条从轻发落的情节。”

“这号人简直是把亲戚身上的力气吸干——跟吸血鬼一样，”布伦金索普先生动了情，说起玄想来。“我相信他要能彻底换一换环境，会好起来。”

===Original===

“Of wives?” Hannah asked flippantly.

“I shouldn’t care to offer anybody the position,” he said bitterly.

“I may be wanting a job myself, before long,” Hannah said. “I suppose you don’t know any respectable old gentleman﻿—but not too respectable﻿—with a competency﻿—and not an annuity﻿—who would suit me?”

===Chinese===

“换妻子？”汉娜轻佻地问。

“这位置我可不敢举荐给任何人，”他苦涩地说。

“我自己没准儿也快找事做了，”汉娜说。“你不会碰巧认识哪位体面的老先生吧——也别太体面——有一份足以度日的家产——而不是年金——正合我用？”

===Original===

“Don’t be silly,” said Mr. Blenkinsop and, when he looked at her, he missed the mocking curve of her lips.

“I’m proud to say I’ve never asked anyone for help yet,” she said, “but the day may come. Yes, the day may come,” she repeated, and now he saw that she was smiling again, but not mockingly and not at him. “Mrs. Spenser-Smith has got her eye on you,” she said, “but stay here for a little longer if you can. It doesn’t do to boast. You see, I’m asking you for help already.”

“Just sitting here?”

===Chinese===

“别说傻话，”布伦金索普先生说完，朝她看去，却没找到她唇边那道揶揄的弧线。

“我还能自豪地说，我至今没求过人帮忙，”她说，“可那一天也许会来。是啊，那一天也许会来，”她又重复了一遍；这一回他看见她又在笑了，却不是揶揄，也不是冲他笑。“斯宾塞-史密斯太太盯上你了，”她说，“可要是能行，就在这儿多坐一会儿。夸口没有好下场。你瞧，我已经在求你帮忙了。”

“就这么坐在这儿？”

===Original===

“Just sitting here,” she said, and Mr. Blenkinsop settled himself in his chair with an adhesive pressure.

He was observant, as far as he went, which was no further than the person who held his interest at the moment. He had not the power to watch another group of people at the same time, to see the development of a situation or to feel slight differences in the atmosphere, which was Hannah’s by nature and by training, and he had no clue to her strained look which quickly changed to one of quivering, half merry determination. Mentally lost, Mr. Blenkinsop was firm in his seat. He knew not why he had to stay there, but stay there he would and, indifferent to everything but this duty, he endured the confusion of voices and laughter, the danger to his patent leather toes from all these young people pushing past him, carefully avoided the eye of Mrs. Spenser-Smith, and calculated that Robert Corder﻿—now talking to another minister who had just arrived and who also wore a frock coat and had a sleek black head﻿—would ignore the presence of his deserter.

===Chinese===

“就这么坐在这儿，”她说；布伦金索普先生便带着一种黏住不走的劲儿，往椅子里坐定了。

他也有观察力，只是所记有限——超不过当下握住他兴趣的那一个人。他没有本事同一面再照应另一堆人，看不出一桩局面的走向，也觉不出气氛的细微变化；这些本事是汉娜的，一半生就，一半练成；至于她脸上那阵绷紧的神色转眼换成一种颤巍巍的、半带快活的决绝，这里头的缘故他更是一无所知。心思早已走神，布伦金索普先生的身子却稳稳钉在座位上。他说不上自己为什么非待在这儿不可，可他铁了心要待下去；除这桩职责以外万事不萦怀，他忍受着满屋人声与笑浪的纷乱，提防那群从他身边挤来挤去的年轻人踢着他漆皮鞋尖，小心翼翼避开斯宾塞-史密斯太太的目光，心里还算定：罗伯特·科德——这会儿正同一位刚到的牧师攀谈，那人也穿一件常礼服，也顶着一颗油光乌黑的脑袋——总不会理会他这个开小差之徒的到来。

===Original===

“Can’t you talk about something?” Hannah asked sharply.

“Yes,” he said obediently. “I was going to tell you that I’m looking for a little house in the country.”

“Good gracious! Are you going to start farming too?”

===Chinese===

“你就不能说点儿什么吗？”汉娜尖着声问。

“能，”他乖乖应道。“我正要告诉你，我想在乡下寻一处小房子。”

“我的天！你也要跟着务农了？”

===Original===

“Not exactly, but I want a little house with a bit of land.”

“And do you expect me to tell you where to find it? Are you going to live in it yourself?”

“Not exactly,” he repeated, and though he looked embarrassed, his confidences continued. “You see, the present state of things can’t last.”

===Chinese===

“也不全是，我想要一处带点地的小房子。”

“怎么，你要我告诉你上哪儿去找？你打算自己住进去？”

“也不全是，”他又说了一遍；他虽然面有窘色，知心话却继续往外倒。“你想，眼下的局面总不能长久下去。”

===Original===

“Which?” Hannah asked perversely.

Mr. Blenkinsop frowned again. “With the Riddings. But we don’t want to talk about it till things are settled.”

“They’re never settled,” Hannah warned him. “Take my word for it. If you want to be comfortable, don’t do anything.”

===Chinese===

“哪个局面？”汉娜存心刁难地问。

布伦金索普先生又皱起眉头。“里丁家（the Riddings）那摊子。不过事情没有定当之前，咱们不去谈它。”

“那种事情永远不会定当，”汉娜警告他。“信我的话。你若想过得安生，就什么也别做。”

===Original===

“Not what I think is right?”

“That’s the very worst thing you can do. What other people think right, if you like, Mr. Blenkinsop, but not what you do. That’s the advice I give you out of my own experience.”

“Well, I’ve been doing nothing in particular for forty years and now that I’ve started I’m going my own way, and it was you who laughed at me for living behind the gilded bars, you know.”

===Chinese===

“连我自己认定该做的事也不做？”

“那正是你能干出来的最糟糕的事。旁人认定对的，你倒不妨做做，布伦金索普先生；你自己认定对的，千万别做。这条忠告，是我拿自己的经验换来的。”

“呃，这四十年我一直没特意做过什么；如今既然开了头，我就要走我自己的路了。再说，笑我蹲在镀金栅栏后头过日子的，可是你呀，别忘了。”

===Original===

“Oh, don’t blame me!” she cried.

“But it’s no good being off with the old love before you are on with the new.”

“But aren’t you on with it?”

===Chinese===

“哦，可别怪到我头上！”她嚷道。

“可是还没跟新欢续上，就先跟旧欢断了，终究不妥。”

“可你现在续上了没有？”

===Original===

“Getting towards it,” Mr. Blenkinsop said, smiling slyly. “But first I want to find the little house.”

“And if you do,” she said, speaking slowly because she was thinking of two things at once and watching the owner of the sleek black head making towards her deviously but surely, “it will bother you for the rest of your days﻿—I know all about these little houses﻿—for when it comes to hard tacks, Mr. Blenkinsop, if you know what they are, for I don’t﻿—but then, you’ve got what they call an independent income, haven’t you?”

“I shouldn’t do it if I couldn’t afford it,” he said, a little stiffly.

===Chinese===

“正往那儿续呢，”布伦金索普先生狡黠地笑着说。“不过头一桩，我得先把那处小房子寻着。”

“你若寻着了，”她说——她说得很慢，因为她心里同时想着两件事，眼睛正盯着那颗油光乌黑脑袋的主人绕着弯儿、却稳稳当当地朝她蹭过来——“那房子往后就得缠你一辈子——这种小房子的门道我全懂——因为真到啃硬面饼的时候，布伦金索普先生，硬面饼是什么，你兴许知道，我是不知道的——不过话又说回来，你有人们所说的那种独立进项，对吧？”

“若是负担不起，我也不会做，”他说，语气有点发僵。

===Original===

“And that,” she said, “is just the difference between you and me,” and Mr. Blenkinsop turned from her, who had spoken the last words on an upward and then a descending breath, to the man at whom she was looking with a little questioning lift of the brows.

This was the minister with the black head and Mr. Blenkinsop took a great dislike to him. He was interrupting an important conversation, and he was objectionable in himself and in his unctuous smile as he addressed Miss Mole, saying he thought they had met before.

Miss Mole shook her head. “I don’t think so,” she said. “I have a very good memory for faces, but if I’ve seen yours before I’ve quite forgotten it.”

===Chinese===

“而这一点，”她说，“恰恰就是你我的分别，”——她末了那几个字，是一口气先扬后抑吐出来的——布伦金索普先生便撇下她，转向了她目光所注的那个人；她望着那人，眉梢微微一挑，带着询问的意味。

这位正是那颗乌黑脑袋的牧师，布伦金索普先生一见他就大为反感。他打断了一场要紧的谈话；他这个人本身就惹人嫌，笑起来又一脸谄油；他向莫尔小姐搭话，说他们从前想必会过。

莫尔小姐摇摇头。“我想没有，”她说。“我记人脸的本事一向很好；可您这一张，从前若是见过，我是忘得干干净净了。”

===Original===

“But you’re Miss Mole, aren’t you?”

“Yes, I’m Miss Mole.” Then she smiled with the vividness which always startled Mr. Blenkinsop. “I wonder if you’re thinking of my cousin,” she said. “Another Mole.”

“Is her name Hannah?”

===Chinese===

“可您是莫尔小姐，对吧？”

“是啊，我是莫尔小姐。”说着她粲然一笑，那份鲜活劲儿，一向都要吓布伦金索普先生一跳。“您莫不是想起了我的表妹吧，”她说。“莫尔家的另一位。”

“她叫汉娜吗？”

===Original===

“No, Hilda. There’s supposed to be a strong family likeness. Oh, don’t go, Mr. Blenkinsop!”

“But we’re going to play Clumps,” said the voice of Mrs. Spenser-Smith, “and I want Mr. Blenkinsop to be one of those to go outside. Mr. Corder will be one, of course, and Mr. Pilgrim, perhaps you’ll be another.”

She swept them both away and it was some time before Mr. Blenkinsop could see more of Miss Mole, for they were in different clumps, than a small steady head among many other heads, like those of bathers, bobbing above a sea of coloured dresses.

===Chinese===

“不，叫希尔达（Hilda）。据说一家人相像得很呢。哦，别走呀，布伦金索普先生！”

“可我们要玩‘分堆’（Clumps）了，”传来了斯宾塞-史密斯太太的声音，“我要布伦金索普先生做一个到门外头去的。科德先生自然是一个，皮尔格林先生（Mr. Pilgrim），也许您来凑一个。”

她一阵风似地把他们两个都卷走了；此后好一阵子，因为他们分在不同的堆里，布伦金索普先生能望见的莫尔小姐，只是众颗脑袋中间一颗小小的、稳稳当当的脑袋，像泳客的脑袋一样，在彩色衣裙的海洋上一起一伏。

## XXX / 第三十章

===Original===
Hannah’s cousin Hilda had developed into a definite person before Hannah went to bed, but it was just as well to know everything about her and, when it was all settled, Hannah liked her very much and as good as believed in her existence. She had some of the characteristics of the people who used to live in the kitchen cupboard; she would come out when she was wanted and, in the meantime, it was nice to feel that she was there, for her own sake as well as for her possible uses, and as the creator is not satisfied until someone else has seen his work, so Hannah longed to produce her cousin for inspection. By the time she had dressed, the next morning, Hilda, that wayward, charming girl, impulsive, but sound at heart, was woven into the substance of Hannah’s youth and it seemed a pity not to entertain Ruth with stories of their escapades: there was the time when they were chased by the bull, for instance, and Hilda had nobly diverted his attention from Hannah, but, as it happened, Ruth was in no need of entertainment that day, for she was confronted with an event which demanded all her horrified, trembling attention.

It was on this day that Howard vanished like a shadow, without a sound, or like some wild beast slipping into the forest without the snap of a twig for warning, and in the excitement that followed, the rage, the bewilderment, the demands for explanations, the grief and tears, Uncle Jim stood calm and stolid in his certainty that he had done right to let the animal escape. There was a story Hannah had often heard told in her childhood about some resident in their countryside﻿—dead before her time﻿—who had made himself famous and infamous for giving shelter to a hunted fox and withstanding the clamour of the hounds and the abuse of the huntsmen, and she thought of him when she looked at Uncle Jim. But it was hardly fair to compare Robert Corder with a hunter: he was more like a man who had brought up, and thought he had tamed, some creature he half despised, yet took a pleasure in having about him, and he had lost it through the treachery of one of his own household. This, however, was far too simple a metaphor for a complicated position. He had been outraged as a father and, as a father, he was responsible for Howard’s scurvy treatment of Mrs. Spenser-Smith. How was he to explain matters to her and how would she explain them to his congregation? Was he to repeat his brother-in-law’s statement that he had advised Howard against dealing frankly with his father, had told him it would be a waste of time and would make bad blood between them, and had persuaded the boy to take the opening that had been offered to him on a South African fruit farm and leave his uncle to stand the racket? Those were Jim’s words and Robert Corder was more enraged with him than with his son. He was genuinely and bitterly hurt by what he saw as a perverse cruelty, he was shocked at such an estimate of his sympathetic understanding, but it was inevitable that he should immediately calculate the impression this extraordinary behaviour would make on his world, of which the chapel was only a part. If he owned to a bad son, there would be people ready enough to imply that the father was to blame, yet with what other story was he to approach Mrs. Spenser-Smith? He could not conscientiously take the fault on himself, but when he rehearsed what he should say to her, his pride, and perhaps his love, forbade him to show her the picture of Howard which he saw himself.

He tried to shut his mind against the things Jim had told him quietly, as though they were accepted facts, unjust things about his character and his treatment of Howard and his incapacity to see another person’s point of view. He had said, in so many words, that if the boy was to escape from the net Mrs. Spenser-Smith and his father had spun for him, it must be done on the instant, with a sudden break: he would never have been able to disentangle himself gradually from reproaches of ingratitude and that gentle bullying of which he had had too much. He would have stayed where he was rather than have struggled without dignity, but he had gone and Robert must make the best of it. It was bad now, but it would be better later on, and when the two met again they would find themselves feeling more kindly towards each other than they had ever done before.

===Chinese===
汉娜的表妹希尔达在汉娜上床之前，已经长成了一个有鼻子有眼的人物；不过把她的底细桩桩件件都摸清，总归是好的。等一切敲定之后，汉娜很喜欢她，几乎就当真信了世上真有这个人。她有几分从前住在厨房碗柜里那伙人的脾性：用得着的时候自会出来，在那之前，晓得她守在那儿就叫人安心——既为她本人打算，也为她随时可能的用处。创造者不到旁人见过自己的作品，总不肯罢休；汉娜也一样，一心巴望着把这位表妹捧出来给人验看。到第二天早晨穿戴停当，希尔达——那个任性而迷人的姑娘，性子急，心眼儿却正——已经织进了汉娜青春岁月的肌理；不拿她们俩的淘气旧事给露丝解闷，倒似乎可惜了：比方说，有一回她们给公牛追赶，是希尔达见义勇为，把牛的注意力从汉娜身上引了开去。可偏巧这天，露丝用不着谁来解闷——她正撞上一桩事，吓得浑身发抖，全部心思都叫它占去了。

就在这一天，霍华德像一道影子似的没了踪影，无声无息；又像什么野兽溜进了森林，连一根树枝的脆响都不曾留下作警告。随后是一场沸反盈天：暴怒、惶惑、逼着讨解释的声浪、悲伤和眼泪——唯独吉姆舅舅站在那里，平静而木然，笃信自己放走那头牲口是做得对。汉娜小时候常听人讲一个故事：她们乡下从前住着那么一个人——早在她出世之前就过世了——只因窝藏一只被追猎的狐狸，顶住了猎犬的狂吠和猎手们的咒骂，闹得既有名又有恶名；她望向吉姆舅舅，就想起了此人。可拿罗伯特·科德比作猎人，未免不大公道：他更像这样一个人——养大了一只活物，自以为驯服了它，心里半是看它不起，身边有它却又觉得有趣，如今倒丢了，坏在自家一个人的背信上。然而，拿这么个简单的比喻去套一个复杂的处境，实在差得太远。身为父亲，他受了奇耻大辱；也身为父亲，霍华德卑劣地亏待了斯宾塞-史密斯太太，他难辞其咎。这件事他怎么对她开口？她又怎么向他的会众去说？难道要他把内兄那套说法复述一遍——说是他劝霍华德千万别跟父亲坦诚相见，说那是白费工夫、只会叫爷儿俩结下仇隙，还说动那孩子抓住送上门的一份南非果园的差事，把他舅舅留下独自挨骂？这些是吉姆的原话；罗伯特·科德恨他，比恨自己的儿子还狠。他把这看作一种悖谬的残忍，痛彻心扉地受了伤；人家竟这样估量他那体恤谅解的心肠，他为之骇然。然而不可避免地，他立刻盘算起这场出格的行径，会在他那个世界——礼拜堂不过是其中一角——掀起怎样的波澜。他若认下一个不成器的儿子，有的是人乐于暗示：过在乃父。可除了这一套说辞，他还拿什么去见斯宾塞-史密斯太太？凭良心，他不能把这过错揽到自己头上；可每当他排演该对她讲的话，他的自尊——或许还有他的爱——都不容他把自己亲眼看见的那个霍华德，端到她面前去。

吉姆平平静静讲给他听的那些话，他一心想闭门不纳——那些话说得不公道，却仿佛是公认的事实：说他品性如何，说他待霍华德如何，说他多么没有本事体察别人的立场。吉姆曾一字一句地说过：这孩子若要逃出斯宾塞-史密斯太太和他父亲替他织下的那张网，就得趁当下，猛地一下挣断；忘恩负义一类的责备，还有那种温柔的威逼，他受的太多了，要想一点一点从中脱身，他断然办不到。他本宁可留在原地，也不肯丢了体面去挣扎；可他到底是走了，罗伯特只得将就。眼下固然糟，往后总会好起来；等爷儿俩再见面，会发现彼此心里那份善意，比从前任何时候都多。

===Original===
It is difficult to quarrel with a man who refuses to be ruffled, but Robert Corder managed to do it with the man who was quietly insulting him, as the anxious listeners in the next room could tell, and at the end of this angry eloquence, Jim suggested, still mildly, that Robert might like to pay back what Mrs. Spenser-Smith had spent on Howard and, if so, he would let him have the money. He was willing to pay for his undertaking, he was glad to buy the boy what he wanted, what all the Erleys wanted, he added, and Robert, angry, resentful, in a quandary about this money business, and foreseeing his own compliance, was still curious enough to ask, unwillingly, what it was that the Erleys wanted. Uncle Jim became less articulate when his own feelings were in question. He mumbled that it was what had sent him to sea, and made his sister feel like a hen in a coop.

Robert Corder repeated these last words, slowly, as though he could not understand them, and then, realising their cruel import, he struck his desk a hard blow so that Mrs. Corder’s silver-framed photograph let out a little jingle, like laughter, and: “You can leave my house!” he cried, in a great voice. “You’re trying to take my wife from me now!”

“Don’t be a fool, Bob. I’m not saying she wasn’t fond of you, but she was cramped. I’m sorry if I’ve hurt your feelings, but I still think I’ve done right﻿—and what she would have wanted me to do.”

He left the room but he had no intention of leaving the house immediately. In his experience, the man who shouted his commands was not the one who expected them to be obeyed, and though he had enough imagination to divine the needs of those he loved﻿—and his sister’s children were dear to him for her sake﻿—he could not picture the real distress of Robert Corder whose imaginative powers were concentrated on himself. His son! His wife! Mrs. Spenser-Smith and the chapel! The fellow members of his committees, who all knew he had a son at Oxford! The ingratitude and cowardliness of Howard! And how could his wife have been cramped when she shared her husband’s life? Yet he thought of her strange visits to Mr. Samson and he knew he had consistently sneered at Howard. He admitted that he was not perfect: no doubt he had made mistakes, but, where his wife was concerned, he could see none, and he felt a slight, unconfessed easing of his duty towards the dead.

His thoughts were too tumultuous, too painful and too sad for clearness. He was more unhappy than he had ever been before, and neither of his daughters had offered him a word of comfort, but already he was adapting himself to new conditions, hearing his own remarks and seeing himself as he went about his work with undiminished spirit, disappointed but tolerant, and gradually acknowledging that Howard had been right and quoting extracts from his letters. But it was very lonely in the study that night, and when Miss Mole came in with his tea he was glad to see her, but he thought of Miss Patsy Withers who would have been tenderly indignant for him and more helpful in her tearfulness and her pity than Miss Mole, with her swift, sure movements and her matter-of-fact face.

===Chinese===
一个人打定主意不动声色，你想跟他吵也吵不起来；可罗伯特·科德偏偏办到了——他同那个不声不响羞辱他的人大吵了一场，隔壁屋里那些坐立不安的听客都可以作证。这顿怒气腾腾的雄辩收了场，吉姆依旧和和气气地进言：斯宾塞-史密斯太太垫在霍华德身上的那笔钱，罗伯特若是愿意奉还，他可以把钱给他。他为自己揽下的事出钱，甘心情愿；他乐得给那孩子买他想要的东西——厄利家人人想要的东西，他补了一句。罗伯特一腔怒火，满腹怨气，为这笔钱进退维谷，也预见到自己终归会让步；可好奇心到底占了上风，他勉勉强强问了一句：厄利家的人想要的究竟是什么。一关系到自己的心事，吉姆舅舅的话就含混了。他咕哝说，正是那样东西当年把他送上了海船，也叫他妹妹活得像笼子里的母鸡。

罗伯特·科德把这最后几个字慢慢地重复了一遍，仿佛听不懂似的；随即悟出话里残忍的分量，他一拳重重地砸在书桌上，震得科德太太那张银框照片叮的一声轻响，倒像一串笑。“你给我离开这座房子！”他放声喝道，“你这会儿是要从我手里抢走我的妻子了！”

“别犯傻了，鲍勃。我不是说她不疼你，可她是憋屈的。要是伤了你的心，我抱歉；可我仍旧觉得我做的是对的——也是她愿意让我做的。”

他走出了房间，却没打算立刻离开这座房子。凭他的经验，高声喝令的人，自己并不指望那命令被人遵行；他的想象力，尽够揣摩他所爱的人的需要——看在妹妹的份上，妹妹的孩子于他也是亲的——可他想象不出罗伯特·科德真实的苦楚，那人的想象力全都聚在他自己身上。他的儿子！他的妻子！斯宾塞-史密斯太太，还有礼拜堂！各委员会里的同仁——人人知道他有个儿子在牛津！霍华德的忘恩负义、怯懦可耻！再说，妻子同丈夫相依共命，怎么谈得上憋屈？可他不由得想起她那些古怪的、上桑普森先生家的拜访，也承认自己向来对霍华德一味讥诮。他自认并非完人：错无疑犯过；可一关系到亡妻，他便一桩也看不见了，而且他感到，自己对亡人所负的那份责任，轻轻卸下了一丝——这一层，他没有招认过。

他的心绪太汹涌、太痛切、太哀伤，理不出个头绪。他从前没有这样不幸过，两个女儿也没送来一句安慰；可他已经在适应新的光景：一边照旧做事，一边听着自己的言语、看着自己的行止，劲头不见减退——失望，然而宽容——并且渐渐承认霍华德做得不差，还引用起他来信里的字句。只是那天夜里，书房里冷清得很。莫尔小姐端着他那盏茶进来时，他见了她倒也欢喜，心里却想念着佩茜·威瑟斯小姐——她准会替他温柔地愤愤不平，她那含泪的怜悯，也必比莫尔小姐更能帮他分担；莫尔小姐呢，手脚又快又稳，脸上却是一派就事论事。

===Original===
“I’ve cut you some sandwiches,” she said, “as you didn’t have much supper.”

“That is very kind.”

“And I hope you’ll eat them.” She was pouring out his tea and remembering that he liked one big and one small lump of sugar.

“This is a bad business, Miss Mole,” he said.

===Chinese===
“我给您切了几块三明治，”她说，“您晚饭没吃多少。”

“难为你费心。”

“还望您把它们吃了。”她给他斟茶，心里记得他要一块大的、一块小的方糖。

“这桩事糟透了，莫尔小姐，”他说。

===Original===
“Yes,” she agreed. She was, indeed, sorry for him and thought he had been unfairly treated. She was sorry for Howard too, for Ethel and for Ruth: she was suffering the discomfort of seeing everybody’s point of view and the impatience of considering it all unnecessary. “But when you think of the sun and the moon and the stars﻿—”

“What have they to do with it?”

“Nobody knows,” she replied, “but they do make our affairs seem rather small beer, don’t they? And if you compare infinity﻿—whatever that is﻿—with three weeks, for in about three weeks all this will be forgotten﻿—”

“I shall never forget it,” he said, his head in his hands.

===Chinese===
“是啊，”她应道。她确实替他难过，也认为他受了委屈；她替霍华德难过，替埃塞尔难过，也替露丝难过：她正受着双重的煎熬——一面把每个人的道理都看得分明，浑身不畅快；一面又满心不耐烦，觉得这一切原本大可不必。“不过，您想想太阳、月亮和星星——”

“它们跟这桩事有什么相干？”

“谁也不知道，”她答道，“可它们的确把咱们这些事儿衬得像淡啤酒，对不对？您再拿无穷——管它是什么——同三个礼拜比一比；因为不出三个礼拜，这一切就全给忘掉啦——”

“我是忘不了的，”他说，双手抱着头。

===Original===
“No, but other people will, and that’s what really matters. That’s our weakness﻿—and our strength. There’s no shame,” she said, as though to herself, “no disappointment, no disillusionment, we can’t bear if we can keep it to ourselves. It’s the beastly curiosity and the beastly speculations of other people that get one on the raw. But no,” she was faithful to her creed, “it’s not beastly. It’s natural. I’d do it myself.”

“Then,” he said, forgetting, in the relief of speech, to be superior, “you can understand how I feel.”

“I do!” she cried, and he said sadly, from the depths of an experience denied to her. “But no. You are not a parent, Miss Mole.”

She gave him one of her sidelong glances. “You take a good deal for granted,” she said, trying to control the upward tilt of her lips. “But you happen to be right,” she went on with a calmness which left him displeased, but dumb, “I’m not a parent. I’m not a minister, either, or a son, and yet, somehow﻿ ﻿… Please eat these sandwiches. Minced ham and turkey. They’re very good. And it isn’t so difficult for you as it seems.” She spoke to him quietly, as though she persuaded a child to its good. “Your son had a sudden offer in South Africa, an out-of-door life is really what he is fitted for, he had to take it or leave it, so he cabled his reply and went off at once and there was no time for explanations.”

===Chinese===
“您是忘不了，可旁人忘得了，这才是顶要紧的。这是我们的软弱——也是我们的力量。羞耻也罢，”她说，像在自言自语，“失望也罢，幻灭也罢，只要闷在自己心里，就没有一样是人熬不住的。真正叫人疼到嫩肉上去的，是旁人那可恶的好奇心、可恶的揣测。不，不对，”她忠于自己的信条，“那不可恶，那是人之常情。换了我，我也一样。”

“那么，”他说，说话的酣畅让他忘了摆居高临下的架子，“你能明白我的感受了。”

“我明白！”她扬声说。他却悲哀地接了一句，那悲哀来自一处她无门可入的深处：“不。你不是为人父母的，莫尔小姐。”

她斜睨了他一眼——她惯有的那一眼。“您这可是把好些事都当成定论了，”她说，一面竭力按住嘴角，不让它往上翘。“可您偏偏说对了，”她接着说，那口平静叫他气闷，又叫他说不出话来，“我不是父母。我也不是牧师，也不是儿子；可是，不知怎么……请把这三明治吃了吧，火腿末的，火鸡的，都很好。再说，事情也远不像看上去那么难。”她轻轻地对他说，像哄一个孩子往好处走。“您儿子在南非骤然得了一份差事，户外的生活才是他真正合宜的活法；这样的机会，接下或者放过，只在一念之间，他拍了电报作答，随即动身，哪里还来得及细说原委。”

===Original===
“So that was what cost fifteen shillings,” Robert Corder muttered. “And Mrs. Spenser-Smith, who has been so generous?”

“I should tell her as much of the truth as is good for her﻿—and you. That’s only fair to yourself.”

“I shall pay back the money!” he said loudly and with determination.

“Then she can’t complain, and I don’t believe she will. Good night, Mr. Corder.”

===Chinese===
“原来那十五个先令就是这么花的，”罗伯特·科德咕哝道。“还有斯宾塞-史密斯太太呢，她一直那么慷慨。”

“依我说，您就把实情告诉她，拣对她有益的说——也对您有益。这对您自己才算公道。”

“这笔钱我要还！”他大声说，斩钉截铁。

“那她就没什么可抱怨的了，我也不信她会抱怨。晚安，科德先生。”

===Original===
“Good night, Miss Mole.”

This time, he did not call her back, but she returned. She stood with her hands clasped in front of her, smiling timidly and looking pleasingly diffident, and more like his idea of what a woman should be than he had ever seen her.

“I wonder﻿ ﻿…” she began, and he said briskly, with a defensive caution at the back of his mind. “Well, Miss Mole, what is it?”

“Could you pretend,” she said, “not to be as angry as you are?”

===Chinese===
“晚安，莫尔小姐。”

这一回，他没有唤她回来，她却自己转了回来。她双手交握，垂在身前，怯怯地笑着，一脸悦人的迟疑，比他见过的任何时候都更像他心目中女人应有的样子。

“我倒想……”她刚起了个头，他便爽爽快快地接了腔，心底暗自提防。“好了，莫尔小姐，什么事？”

“您能不能装一装，”她说，“装得不这么生气？”

===Original===
“I’m not angry, but deeply hurt.” Her silence accepted the paraphrase and he added, with his usual authority, “And pretence of any kind is against my rule.”

Her eyes widened, she looked remarkably childlike and that gaze made him uneasy. He was beginning to know her well enough to expect some retort in violent contrast to the expression of her odd, mobile face, but it did not come.

“I was thinking of Ruth,” she said. “And Ethel. Yes, she has asked me to call her Ethel,” she said quickly, in answer to the slight contraction of his brows. “They are very unhappy.”

“That is my son’s responsibility. We must all suffer together.”

===Chinese===
“我不是生气，是深深地受了伤。”她的沉默认可了这一换法，他又添上一句，带着他惯有的权威口吻：“而且，无论什么样的作假，都为我的规矩所不容。”

她眼睛睁得大大的，显得出奇地孩子气；这一望，望得他心里发虚。他渐渐把她摸得够熟了，正等着一句回敬——跟她那张古怪而善变的脸上此刻的神情恰成强烈的对照——可这句回敬迟迟不来。

“我想的是露丝，”她说，“还有埃塞尔。是啊，她要我叫她埃塞尔，”见他的眉头微微一拢，她赶忙补上这一句。“她们难过得很。”

“那是我儿子的责任。我们只好大家一齐受苦。”

===Original===
“But don’t suffer at all,” she suggested. “If you can’t pretend, the only thing to do is not to feel. You can’t have people thinking your son has done something wrong. In your position,” she added softly. “And Ethel and Ruth are very much worried about you.”

“They’ve shown no signs of it.”

“Ah,” she said, “they’re a little afraid of you. You can be rather terrible, Mr. Corder, if you’ll forgive my saying so. They are sitting in there, shivering and trying not to cry. Could you make them realise that you have the situation in hand, that there’ll be nothing for them to do but follow your lead? Ethel is troubling herself about Mrs. Spenser-Smith and wondering what she is to say to her﻿—and to everybody else.”

===Chinese===
“可您干脆一点儿都不必受，”她献策道。“既然不能装，就只有一个法子：不动心。总不能让旁人当您儿子做了错事呀。您是何等地位，”她轻声补了一句。“再说，埃塞尔和露丝正为您担心得很。”

“她们可没露出一点儿担忧。”

“唉，”她说，“她们有点儿怕您。恕我直言，科德先生，您严厉起来，是满可怕的。她们就坐在那屋里，瑟瑟发抖，忍着不敢哭。您能不能叫她们明白：局面在您掌心里攥着，她们什么都不必做，只消跟着您走？埃塞尔正为斯宾塞-史密斯太太煎熬，猜想着见了她该说什么——见了旁人又该说什么。”

===Original===
Robert Corder did not fail in his response. “There is no need for Ethel to distress herself,” he said. “She can leave it to me. I shall go and see Mrs. Spenser-Smith tomorrow. I suppose there are troubles in every family and some day she may have some of her own.”

“I should think it’s more than likely,” Hannah said. She glanced at the clock. “I must write a letter before I go to bed. I shall just have time to catch the post. Good night, Mr. Corder.” She hesitated and said, with the timidity he found very becoming in her, “I shall tell your daughters they must try to be as brave as you are,” but as she left the study, she was telling herself that one of his children was free and she must do what she could to secure liberty for the others.

===Chinese===
罗伯特·科德果然不负所望地接了茬。“埃塞尔不必自寻苦恼，”他说，“这事交给我。明天我就去看斯宾塞-史密斯太太。我想，家家都有一本难念的经，往后她说不定也会推上几桩自己的。”

“依我看，多半免不了，”汉娜说。她瞥了一眼挂钟。“睡觉前我还得写一封信，赶邮班还来得及。晚安，科德先生。”她顿了顿，又带着那种他看她极顺眼的怯意说：“我会告诉您的两位小姐，叫她们也学一学您这样的勇敢。”可走出书房的时候，她心里正对自己念叨：他的孩子里，已经有一个得了自由，其余几个的自由，她必须尽力去成全。

## XXXI / 第三十一章

===Original===

To see Robert Corder reassuring his children, gravely taking this new burden on himself and shouldering it courageously, was for Hannah, rather like watching a man palming off the work of an artist as his own, and she wondered he did not see the pathos, the tacit reproach, of Ethel’s surprised admiration for him and of the relief which flushed Ruth’s face, but, after all, it did not matter to Hannah why he acted well so long as he did it, and she remembered that her suggestions had been successful, not because she was skilful, but because they were practical. If he was to keep his picture of his broad-minded, optimistic self, he must take Howard’s departure and give it his approval in a wise, foresighted spirit; only so could he mount his pulpit on Sunday and feel that he still dominated his people. There are certain kinds of sorrow which add to a man’s value, but that of possessing a bad son is not one of them, and Robert Corder decided not to have it. That, at least, was Hannah’s criticism of his behaviour.

===Chinese===

看着罗伯特·科德安抚自己的儿女，郑重其事地把这副新的重担揽到自己身上，又勇毅地扛了起来，汉娜心里的感觉，就像看一个人把画家的作品冒充为自己的手笔；埃塞尔对他那分惊讶的钦佩，露丝脸上那片如释重负的红晕，里头含着的哀婉、含着的不言之责，他竟全然看不出，汉娜颇觉纳闷。不过说到底，只要他戏演得好，至于他为什么演得好，汉娜并不在意；她还记得，她的种种建议之所以奏效，并非因为她手段高明，而是因为它们切实可行。他若要保住自己那幅胸襟开阔、乐观进取的自画像，就必须把霍华德的出走接过来，以一种贤明而有远见的精神予以首肯；唯有如此，礼拜天他登上讲坛时，才还能觉得自己统御着信众。世间有几种悲哀能给一个男人增添身价，可是养了个坏儿子这类悲哀不在其列；罗伯特·科德便决意不受这份悲哀。至少，这是汉娜对他行止的评语。

===Original===

Ruth had her own comment to make, though she put it as a question which was not meant to be enlightening. She had the appearance, Hannah thought, as she lit the night-light, of a person who has had a severe bout of pain, and lies limp and contented in its cessation. Her face, which softened and hardened so quickly, seemed to have taken on childish contours, but her eyes, drowsy with comfort, kept their look of guarded intelligence.

“Isn’t it wonderful,” she sighed, “not to be as miserable as you thought you were going to be?”

“Is it? I don’t know. I’m never miserable.”

===Chinese===

露丝自有她的评语，只是把它当作问题提了出来，那问题并不指望给人什么启发。汉娜点上小夜灯时心想，她这模样，像害过一场剧痛的人，痛一停，便软软瘫着，心满意足。她那张脸，说软就软、说硬就硬，此刻似乎显出了孩童的轮廓；可那双眼睛，舒适得昏昏欲睡，仍旧带着戒备的机灵。

“你说妙不妙，”她叹道，“原以为要惨透了的，竟没有惨成那样？”

“是吗？我不知道。我从来不惨。”

===Original===

“Well, you must have been, often, when you were young.”

“There was always something else,” Hannah said. “There’s always something else, when you look for it. But no, I wasn’t unhappy when I was young. There were my boots, of course, but there were the feet inside them and, you know, if I’d had the kind of boots I wanted, my feet wouldn’t have been so pretty now, and I suppose I realised, even then, that it was all for the best. I’ve always been good at that.”

===Chinese===

“哦，那你年轻的时候一定惨过，还惨过好多回呢。”

“总有别的东西在，”汉娜说，“只要你肯找，总有别的东西在。不过没有，我年轻的时候并不愁苦。我的靴子当然够呛，可靴子里头还有脚呢；再说你知道，我当年要真穿上了自己想要的那种靴子，我这双脚如今就不会这么标致了；我想，即便在那个年纪，我也明白了这一切都是因祸得福。这一套，我一向拿手。”

===Original===

“You’re very conceited about your feet. And people don’t notice feet much.”

“What does that matter to me? I notice them myself, and every night I have a little reception for them. They sit at one end of the bed and I sit at the other and I look at my nice straight toes and all the little bones of my feet and think of the manifold works of God. It’s not conceit. I didn’t make them.”

“But you like them more because they’re yours than you would if they were mine.”

===Chinese===

“你这双脚，你也太自负了。再说，人们也不大留意脚。”

“那与我何干？我自己留意呀。每天夜里，我都要为它们举行一场小小的接见：它们坐在床那头，我坐在这头，我瞧着我那些齐齐整整的脚趾，瞧着脚上那些小小的骨头，心想上帝造物的万千精工。这不是自负。它们又不是我造的。”

“可就因为它们是你的，你才这样喜欢；若是我的，你就未必了。”

===Original===

“Perhaps; they’re such a surprise. My father and mother had honest yeomen’s feet and so has Lilla.”

“Lilla? That’s Mrs. Spenser-Smith’s name.”

“Did I say Lilla? I meant Hilda.”

===Chinese===

“也许吧；它们实在叫人惊喜。我父母长的是本分庄户人的脚，莉拉也是。”

“莉拉？那是斯宾塞-史密斯太太的名字呀。”

“我说过莉拉吗？我是说希尔达。”

===Original===

“Who’s she?”

“Oh, she’s a cousin, rather a mysterious cousin. I’ll tell you about her some day. She was better looking than me, but not much, and her feet were ugly. Nice hands, though, and she used to say that if she and I could pick out our best features, we might make one quite decent-looking woman of us.”

“Is she alive now?”

===Chinese===

“希尔达是谁？”

“哦，她是我的一个表妹，一位挺神秘的表妹。哪天我讲给你听。她长得比我好看，不过也好看得有限，脚却生得丑。手倒是好看，她从前常说，她和我若能把各自最标致的地方挑出来拼一拼，没准儿能拼出一个相当体面的美人来。”

“她如今还活着吗？”

===Original===

“Good gracious, yes, I hope so. Somewhere. She’s no older than I am, but I haven’t seen her for years. Elusive creature,” Hannah said, staring at the little flame of the night-light.

“Go on about her.”

“Not now. Good night.”

===Chinese===

“老天爷，活着呢，但愿如此。总在什么地方吧。她年纪跟我一般大，只是多年没见了。一个行踪飘忽的人儿，”汉娜说，眼睛盯着小夜灯那朵小小的火苗。

“接着讲她呀。”

“现在不讲了。晚安。”

===Original===

“Moley﻿ ﻿…” this was Ruth’s question, “was Father angry when you took in his tea? I know he was furious with Uncle Jim, because we heard him shouting, and I never thought he’d come in and be so nice to us.”

“But as it wasn’t your fault, why should he be angry with you?”

===Chinese===

“莫莉……”这是露丝的问题，“你给爸爸端茶进去的时候，他生气了吗？我知道他对吉姆舅舅火冒三丈，因为我们听见他吼来着，我万万没想到他会进来，待我们这么和气。”

“可这又不是你们的过错，他为什么要对你们生气呢？”

===Original===

Ruth made no reply to that; there was none to make and she did not refer to past experience. It was plain to Hannah that Ruth was giving credit where credit was due, and it was sad that she should have to look beyond her father’s character for the cause of his unexpected gentleness. “Well, I hope he’ll stop being angry with Uncle Jim,” she said. “I’ll tell you what I’ve decided. I shall go out to South Africa, to be with Howard, as soon as I can.”

“And what about me?”

“Couldn’t you stay and look after Father?”

===Chinese===

露丝听了这话没有作答；也无从作答，她也没有提从前的经验。汉娜看得分明，露丝是把功劳记在了该记的名下；可叹的是，她竟不得不越过她父亲的人品，到别处去寻找他这份意外温和的缘由。“唉，但愿他别再生吉姆舅舅的气了，”她说。“我告诉你我的打算。我要尽快动身到南非去，跟霍华德作伴。”

“那我呢？”

“你就不能留下来照看爸爸吗？”

===Original===

“I’m here to look after you because you’re such a baby and can’t mend your own stockings. As soon as you can, I shall go.”

“Is that why you haven’t tried to make me do it?” Ruth asked slyly. “And why shouldn’t you come too?”

“I don’t know why I didn’t go years ago,” Hannah said. “What a fool I’ve been!”

===Chinese===

“我在这儿是照看你的，因为你还是个奶娃娃，连自己的袜子都不会补。等你一学会，我就走。”

“所以你才从来不逼着我学吗？”露丝狡黠地问。“再说，你怎么不一起来呢？”

“几年前我怎么没去，连我自己也不明白，”汉娜说。“我真是个大傻瓜！”

===Original===

“There was your little house.”

“Yes,” she said, “perhaps that had something to do with keeping me, but there, I never had enough money to pay my fare and I never shall have.”

“That’s because you’re so extravagant about your shoes,” Ruth said pertly. “And that reminds me. There’s the story about breaking the window, and now there’s your cousin Hilda. You’re always promising and never telling.”

===Chinese===

“还有你的小房子呢。”

“是啊，”她说，“或许这也是把我拴在这儿的一个缘故。不过说实在的，我的钱从来没够付过一张船票，往后也永远不会够。”

“那是因为你在鞋上头太摆阔啦，”露丝伶俐地说。“这倒提醒了我。有打碎窗户那桩故事，如今又添上你的表妹希尔达。你老是许诺，从来不讲。”

===Original===

“I can’t tell you about the window because the story isn’t finished. Neither is the one about Cousin Hilda, for that matter. Stories don’t finish. The window one is coming to what you might call a period, but not an end. No indeed. I fancy that Part Two will be less exciting but more enthralling to the student of human nature than Part One. Really good biographies can’t be written until the what-you-may-call-thems are dead. But I might tell you when we’re in South Africa﻿—far away.”

===Chinese===

“窗户的事我不能讲，因为故事还没有完。说实在的，希尔达表妹的事也没有完。故事是没有完的。窗户那桩，眼下正走近一个你不妨称之为句点的地方，可不是终点。绝非终点。我琢磨着，第二部虽不及第一部惊险，在人性研究者眼里，却会更加引人入胜。真正的好传记，非得等那些个什么什么人物死了才写得成。不过，等咱们到了南非——隔着千山万水的地方——我也许会讲给你听。”

===Original===

“Couldn’t you just give me a hint about why you broke the window? Was it in Radstowe?”

“Not a hint,” Hannah said. “Why can’t you make up some stories for yourself? That’s what I have to do.”

“But these are true, aren’t they?” Ruth cried anxiously. “Not like the burglar one?”

===Chinese===

“你总可以给我一点暗示吧，你们干吗打碎那扇窗户？事情出在拉德斯托吗？”

“半个暗示也不给，”汉娜说。“你怎么不给自己编些故事？我就非编不可。”

“可这些都是真的，对不对？”露丝着急地嚷道。“不像贼那桩故事吧？”

===Original===

“Not in the least like the burglar one. And now, sleep. If you’re going to South Africa, you must take a profession with you and you’ll never have one if you stay awake till this time of night. Be anything you like, but be something. Simply being a useful woman like me isn’t good enough.”

“You’re good enough for me,” Ruth said, shutting her eyes on the awkwardness of this confession.

===Chinese===

“跟贼那桩半点也不像。好了，睡觉。你要去南非，就得随身带一门职业去；夜里闹到这更次还不睡，你永远也别想有职业。随你喜欢做什么，可总得做个什么人物。单单做一个我这样有用的女人，是不够好的。”

“对我来说，你够好了，”露丝说，这句知心话说得发了窘，说完便把眼睛闭上了。

===Original===

Hannah went upstairs and stood in the darkness of her bedroom. For the first time she heard her own true story with Ruth’s ears and she did not like the sound of it, and Ruth did not repeat her assertion that Miss Mole was good enough for her. She was mute, stricken, all her faiths shaken, what she had seen as beauty changed to ugliness, her sight, perhaps incurably, dimmed or distorted, and this was Hannah’s work. Apart from the consequences of her acts on the innocent, the undreamed of, her adult conscience was not troubled. Her regrets were for her lack of judgment, not for a chastity which did not seem to her to be sensibly diminished. She had loved her lover and expected to marry him, but she did not make that expectation an excuse. She did not need an excuse. Her values were not those of Robert Corder and Beresford Road Chapel and she was heartily thankful that there was no legal bond, but she was unhappy when she tried to exchange her own conscience for Ruth’s juvenile one, and what Ruth made of the story would chiefly depend on who presented it to her. If it were her father or her sister, all these happy hours they had had together would be smeared by their disgust. And yet Hannah had a heartening belief in the independence of Ruth’s mind and her natural tendency to go contrary to her relatives: moreover, they were not likely to tell her much. Miss Mole would disappear mysteriously, her name would not be spoken, and Ruth’s silent loyalty might be strengthened by her secret defiance, yet she might be disillusioned, though she was still loyal. No woman, not even Hannah, who had made a practice of indifference to most people’s opinions, could relish the prospect of being thought evil, but it was the effect on Ruth herself about which she was really troubled, and it seemed to her hard that what she had done ten years ago should have its influence on a child of whose existence she had not known, so hard and unreasonable that her sanity refused to take the weight of this responsibility and, as she stood there, meditating immediate flight, her commonsense resisted a temptation which, indeed, was not very alluring, for where was she to go and what good would she be doing Ruth if she left the field to Mr. Pilgrim? And perhaps her cousin Hilda would save her from him as she had saved her from the bull. No, she told herself, undressing in the darkness which clarified her thoughts, the worst thing she could do was to waste the powers which had been signally proved this evening. Through her means, Ruth and Ethel had gone comparatively happily to bed and all her calculations were grossly wrong if Robert Corder did not return cheerfully from his interview with Lilla, for by the first post, she would learn that the occasion for discreet behaviour had arrived. And, during the interview in which they tried to make things easy for each other, Hannah hoped Robert Corder would speak enthusiastically of his housekeeper. That would give Lilla something to worry about, she chuckled, as she got into bed, and she thought she would be almost willing to sacrifice herself maritally to Robert Corder, if she got the chance, for the sake of supplanting Lilla as leading lady of the chapel, a short-lived joy for a long martyrdom, but she knew that he would only praise her in flattery of Lilla and that he was in happy ignorance of his indebtedness to Miss Mole.

===Chinese===

汉娜上了楼，站在卧房的黑暗里。有生以来头一回，她用露丝的耳朵听见了自己那段真实的故事，她不喜欢它发出的声音；露丝呢，也没有再重复她那句断言——莫尔小姐对她说来已经够好了。她哑然，怔忡，一应信念尽皆动摇，昔日眼中的美化作了丑，她的眼力或许已无可救药地蒙翳、变形——而这些都是汉娜的手笔。且不说她的行为加在无辜者身上、加在她做梦也想不到的人身上的种种后果，她这成年人的良心并不为此受熬煎。她悔的是自己失了判断，并不是悔那份贞洁——在她看来，那贞洁也未见得真折损了多少。她爱过她的爱人，也指望过嫁给他，但她并不拿这指望作开脱。她用不着开脱。她的价值尺度，不是罗伯特·科德和贝雷斯福德路礼拜堂的那一套；一纸法律婚约不曾捆住她，她为此衷心庆幸；可是，当她试着拿自己的良心去换露丝那份稚嫩的良心时，她不好受了；露丝把这段故事听成什么样子，主要看讲故事的人是谁。倘若讲的人是她父亲或她姐姐，她们一同度过的所有这些快活时光，都会被他们的嫌恶涂抹上污迹。然而汉娜心里存着一个令人振奋的信念：露丝的心思自有主见，天生爱跟自家人拧着来；何况，他们也未必会对她多讲。莫尔小姐会神秘地消失，她的名字不会再有人提起，而露丝无言的忠诚，或许会因她暗中的不服而愈发坚定；可她也或许会幻灭，尽管依旧忠心。没有哪个女人——连汉娜也不例外，虽然她素来把大多数人的议论不放在心上——能对被人当作坏女人的前景甘之如饴；可她真正忧心的，还是这事落在露丝身上的影响；在她看来，十年前干下的那桩事，竟要在一个她此前全然不知其存在的孩子身上投下影子，这实在残酷，残酷而不近情理，以至于她的神智不肯把这副重担扛上肩头；她站在那里，盘算着即刻出逃，她的常识却抵御住了这个诱惑——说实话，这诱惑本也算不得怎么诱人：她能到哪里去呢？她若把地盘拱手让给皮尔格林先生，对露丝又能有什么好处？再说，她的表妹希尔达或许会救她脱离此人，正如希尔达从前救她脱离那头公牛。不，她对自己说，一面在黑暗中宽衣——这黑暗倒叫她的心思澄澈起来——她顶糟的一步棋，莫过于把今晚已经大见效验的本领白白糟蹋掉。多亏她从中调度，露丝和埃塞尔才总算快快活活地上了床；倘若罗伯特·科德不能从他同莉拉的会谈中兴致勃勃地回来，她全部的算盘便都打错了，因为到头一班邮件时，她自会知道，谨言慎行的时刻已然来临。还有，在那场彼此都设法让对方好受些的会谈里，汉娜盼望罗伯特·科德会起劲地夸一夸他的女管家。那可就够莉拉操心一阵的了，她爬上床时暗自窃笑；她想，若有机会，她几乎情愿把自己牺牲在婚姻里嫁给罗伯特·科德，好取莉拉而代之、坐上礼拜堂头号女角儿的位子——那是拿长久的殉难换短短的欢愉；可是她知道，他夸她，也不过是拐着弯儿向莉拉献殷勤；他还懵懵然身在福中，全然不知自己欠了莫尔小姐多深的情分。

===Original===

It was difficult to endure his look of self-satisfaction the next evening. A stranger would have thought he had planned his son’s escape and made Mrs. Spenser-Smith a party in the enterprise, and Hannah had another glimpse into the life of Mrs. Corder, who, surely, must have wilted and perhaps actually died, under the blandly arrogant delusions of her husband. The face which looked out of its silver frame was that of a woman with delicate perceptions who would find more truth in Mr. Samson’s extravagant inventions than in Robert Corder’s interpretation of his acts and thoughts, but, no doubt, her chief desire, like Hannah’s, was for the happiness of her children and she had borne with her husband as wives find it necessary to do.

===Chinese===

第二天晚上，他那副扬扬自得的神气真叫人难以忍受。换了陌生人，准以为他儿子的远走高飞全出自他的运筹，还把斯宾塞-史密斯太太也拉来做了这桩事业的同伙。汉娜借此又窥见了科德太太身世的一斑：在她丈夫那和婉而倨傲的妄想底下，她必定早已枯萎，说不定竟是真的凋谢了。银相框里向外望着的那张脸，是个感知细腻的女人的脸；在她眼里，桑普森先生那些想入非非的编造，恐怕都比罗伯特·科德对自己言行心曲的注解更接近真实。不过毫无疑问，她的头一心愿同汉娜的一样，是指望儿女幸福；她也就这样容让了她的丈夫，一如做妻子的都觉得非如此不可。

===Original===

This was Hannah’s view of their relationship and Uncle Jim had given her no cause to change or amplify it. He, at least, did not yield to Hannah’s wiles. Under his matter-of-fact directness, he was not simple; he seemed able to guess the destination of Hannah’s conversation, when it was Mrs. Corder, though she might start in the opposite direction, and she gave him up. When she saw him go, the next day, she would be almost as poor in information as she was when he arrived and this was hardly fair, for he had constantly questioned her about farming and expressed his regret that he could not see her little place. He would be a good tenant when she wanted another one. The place would suit him very well, he thought; it was near enough to Radstowe and he wanted to keep an eye on Ruth.

===Chinese===

这是汉娜对他们夫妻关系的看法，吉姆舅舅没有给过她任何改变或引申这一看法的由头。至少他，是从不上汉娜的钩的。在他那有话直说的爽利底下，人并不简单；每当汉娜的话头绕着科德太太打转，哪怕她从相反的方向起头，他似乎也猜得出这场谈话的归宿，于是她只好死了这条心。明天看着他走，她带走的消息几乎会同他来时一样贫乏，这实在有失公允，因为他一路上频频向她打听农事，还连连叹惋无缘看一看她那处小产业。等她想另觅佃户的时候，他会是个好佃户。他想，那地方对他再合适不过；离拉德斯托够近，他又存心照看着点露丝。

===Original===

“Well, one never knows,” Hannah said. “You’d better leave me your address. I suppose,” she went on thoughtfully, “I’ve got another twenty years of work left in me. My tenant may not last as long as that and, while I’m working, you could be paying me rent. And I’ll charge you a high one too, for depreciation of my property. Then, at sixty, I’ll turn you out and retire. But the difficulty is that if I lose this job, I may not get another.”

“Why should you lose it? But look here, if you do, you’ve got to let me know. I’m going to wander about for a bit, but my bank will always find me.”

“Are you thinking of kidnapping Ruth, too?” Hannah asked coolly.

===Chinese===

“唉，世事难料，”汉娜说。“你最好把地址留给我。我想，”她若有所思地接着说，“我身上还剩二十年的力气可卖。我的佃户未必撑得了这么久；我做工的年月里，你正好给我付房租。房租我还要收得高高的，贴补我房产的折旧。到了六十，我就撵你走，自己告老。麻烦就麻烦在，这差事要是丢了，我未必找得着下一份。”

“你怎么会丢差事？不过听着，真丢了，你得让我知道。我要出去游荡一阵子，可我的银行永远找得着我。”

“你是不是还打算把露丝也绑了去？”汉娜冷冷地问。

===Original===

“I’ll have a shot,” he said.

“And Ethel?”

“No, I don’t think I need kidnap Ethel. I’m hoping she’ll get married. Who was that black-haired chap who made such a fool of himself at the party? I thought they seemed pretty friendly.”

===Chinese===

“我打算试试，”他说。

“埃塞尔呢？”

“不用，埃塞尔我看不必绑，我指望她嫁人呢。宴会上那个黑头发的小子是谁？出尽了洋相的那位？我看他们俩倒挺亲热。”

===Original===

“Would you like her to marry him?”

“I’d like her to marry anybody, so long as he didn’t drink or knock her about,” he said, and increased Hannah’s respect for his astuteness.

“But perhaps it will be Mr. Corder who gets married. There are plenty of ladies in the congregation who would meet him more than halfway.”

===Chinese===

“你愿意她嫁给他吗？”

“嫁谁都行，只要他不喝酒、不动手打她，”他说。这话令汉娜对他的精明更添了一层敬重。

“可说不定要结婚的是科德先生呢。会众里的太太小姐多的是，不等他迎上去，早赶着迎过半程去了。”

===Original===

“Yes, I spotted one of those, too,” he said. “The fair, floppy one. No use to Ruth at all. I wish to goodness,” he said, knocking his pipe out against the grate, “you’d marry him yourself.”

“Anything to oblige you, of course,” Hannah said dryly, and then, flaring up, she cried: “Upon my word, I think you’re the most unscrupulous person I ever met.”

===Chinese===

“可不，那路人我也瞧见过一个，”他说。“那个白皙金发、松松垮垮的。对露丝半点用处没有。我真是求天告地，”他把烟斗在炉栅上磕了磕，说道，“你干脆自己嫁了他算了。”

“自然喽，只要能讨你的好，叫我怎样都行，”汉娜干巴巴地说，随即又发作起来，嚷道：“说句良心话，我看你是我这辈子见过的最没顾忌的人。”

## XXXII / 第三十二章

===Original===

In the few days that passed between Uncle Jim’s departure and Wilfrid’s return, there was an atmosphere of unnatural sweetness in the Corders’ house. Ethel’s gratitude to her father, for refraining from making her suffer for Howard’s fault, transformed her into the sunny daughter of an indulgent parent, but Ruth, with the cynicism which both pleased and saddened Hannah, treasured these good moments because she did not believe they would last. Robert Corder, however, had adopted an attitude and he kept it, and Hannah, who found it impossible to attribute a good motive to him if she could find a bad one, saw this consistency as a result of his instinct of self-protection which warned him that if he was to play a part well he must play it all the time. Before many days were over, he had merged himself into his role and she wondered whether there had been any real suffering at Howard’s treatment of him and any response to its implications. His was a curious character and, for all his human weaknesses, she could not believe he was quite real. She would tell herself that he was a marvellous puppet, so much like a man that he could deceive most people, and then, when he came into the house, she had to admit her consciousness of his personality. Ethel was in a flutter to please him, Ruth was vigilantly critical, Hannah herself paid him the tribute of an irritated delight in watching him, in divining the meaning of his looks and foretelling his remarks, and these were the reactions to no puppet. He could absorb the suggestions that suited him as easily as water takes a colour, yet he must have had some suggestive power over other people or they would not have sought him out and gone away comforted. This was a puzzle which it would take a lifetime to solve and Hannah was afraid her sands were running out﻿—for Ethel’s good temper was not all due to her father’s leniency﻿—and as she saw the silent slipping of the moments, she was alternately enraged and amused that, with a few words, Mr. Pilgrim could change the family friend into a person to be shunned, though her management, her economy, her cooking, her counsel, all that made her useful to these people, would remain unchanged. But there was her cousin Hilda and her own word against Mr. Pilgrim’s; it became a matter of pride with her to frustrate him, and there was a strained alertness about her which no one but Mr. Samson noticed. It would have been comforting to tell him everything; he would have listened with a salutary lack of surprise, but, sane as he was, she could not unwrap her little secret to his gaze. He was, in fact, too sane to understand that what he saw in terms of natural, harmless appetite had had a spiritual value for her which she still struggled to keep.

She admitted to being tired and Mr. Samson growled his anxiety about her; he had told that Bible-smiter she was worth taking care of and would have told him a good deal more, but for Miss Fitt and the peaky little girl. He did not want to make trouble that would fall on the family. And what had Corder wanted, poking his nose in and waking Mr. Samson out of his afternoon nap? If he had not thought it was the man with the cat’s meat he would not have gone to the door, and there he was, smirking on the step and trying to look like a herald angel.

“But I let him know it’s you that’s the angel, just as his wife was before you. I’m a lucky old devil, finding two of you at the end of my days, and you wouldn’t think I’d fancy your kind, to look at me, now would you? Well, I’ve fancied all sorts, to tell the truth, but it’s the lively ones I like. A quick tongue’s more use to me than a pretty face. Now, you take care of yourself. And what about a bottle of port to drink on the quiet?”

===Chinese===

吉姆舅舅（Uncle Jim）动身、威尔弗里德（Wilfrid）归来之间的那几天里，科德家浮着一层不寻常的甜意。霍华德（Howard）闯了祸，父亲却隐忍着不叫埃塞尔（Ethel）受苦，埃塞尔感激父亲，摇身变作了慈纵的父母跟前一个满面晴光的女儿；露丝（Ruth）却怀着那股既令汉娜（Hannah）欢喜又叫她心酸的冷峭，把眼下的好时光一一收进珍藏，因为她不信好日子能长久。罗伯特·科德（Robert Corder）却是选定了一种姿态，并且始终保持着。汉娜这个人，只要寻得出一个坏动机，就断不肯归他一个好动机；她把这份一如既往看作他自我保护本能的产物——那本能在警告他：这出戏既要演，就得一刻不停地演到底。不出几天，他已同角色融为一体，她不由纳闷：霍华德那样待他，他可曾真有过切肤之痛？那件事的言外之意，可曾真正进到他心里？他的性子古怪；纵然他人性的弱点一样不缺，她却不能相信他是个十足的真。她常对自己说，他是一只绝妙的木偶，像人像得骗得过大多数人；可等他一跨进家门，她又不得不承认，她分明感觉到他的人格。埃塞尔讨好他，讨得心花怒放；露丝警戒着，时时挑剔；汉娜自己也向他纳了一份贡——看他行事，猜他眼色的用意，预支他将要出口的话，看得又恼又乐——这决计不是对着一只木偶才有的反应。凡投合他口味的暗示，他吸纳起来如水着色一般容易；可他对旁人必定也有几分感召之力，不然人们不会寻上门去，去时还得了安慰。这是个要花一辈子才解得开的谜，而汉娜怕自己命里的沙已所剩无几——因为埃塞尔的好脾气，并不全是父亲宽纵之功——她眼看时光无声地溜走，一时恼，一时又觉得好笑：皮尔格林先生（Mr. Pilgrim）只消三言两语，就能把这家的朋友变作一个人人躲让的人物，而她的操持、她的节俭、她的厨艺、她的见识，凡此种种使她于这家人有用的东西，却不会因此挪动分毫。可她还有个表妹希尔达（Hilda），有她的一面之词，足与皮尔格林先生的一面之词相抗；挫败此人渐渐成了她赌一口气的事，她周身绷着一股紧张的警醒，除桑普森先生（Mr. Samson）外无人察觉。把一切原原本本说给他听，原该是桩宽心的事；他会听着，带着那种有益于人的不惊不诧；可他到底是太清明了，她没法把自己这点小小的秘密摊开在他眼下。说实在的，他是清醒得过了头，才领会不到：在他眼里不过是自然无害的口腹之欲的那点东西，对她原有过一种心灵的价值，她至今还在挣扎着保全它。

她自认乏了，桑普森先生便把这份牵挂咆哮了出来；他早对那个捶着《圣经》讲道的家伙说过，她这样的人值得好好照看；若不是有菲特小姐（Miss Fitt）和那个病恹恹的小丫头在场，他还有的是话要讲。他不想惹是非，惹出来是要落到那家人头上的。再说，科德先生凑的是什么热闹，鼻子伸进门来，把桑普森先生的午觉都搅了？他若不是当成了送猫食的，才不会去开门；倒好，那人站在台阶上，一脸的得意，竭力装出一副报喜天使的架势。

“可我让他弄明白了：天使是你，你前头那位，是他过世的太太。我这老魔鬼造化不浅，熬到风烛残年，倒寻着你们两位。再瞧瞧我这张脸，你总不会当我配看上你们这样的人吧，是不是？嗐，实不相瞒，什么样儿的我都看得上过，可我最中意有活气的。一条利嘴，比一张俏脸对我有用得多。好了，你自家多保重。弄一瓶波特酒来，咱们偷偷喝一口，怎么样？”

===Original===

“No, no, it’s a teetotal household.” Her laughter rang out. “But Mr. Corder had to eat some brandy with his Christmas pudding! I don’t want any port. I’m going to have a day’s holiday and spend it in the country.”

“And that’s a funny idea of a holiday,” Mr. Samson said.

It was certainly a euphemistic description of the expedition she had planned and for which, knowing that Ruth would beg to go with her, she had not settled on the day. It would have been better to wait until the beginning of the school term, but that was a long way off and she would have to refuse Ruth a pleasure which, for her, would be something like a day in Radstowe had been for Hannah, and Hannah herself, full of shrinking though she was, had a great longing to see her own country, to sit in the train and watch the city and its suburbs giving way to fields and woods, flat meadows cut by dykes and villages dominated by their stately perpendicular churches, just as she had watched all these giving way to the promise of Radstowe, that fairy place of streets and towers, bridges, water and ships. She would get her business done as soon as possible, and then she would cross the fields to the old farm. She did not know who owned it now, but if they had any likeness to her own wary but kindly people, they would let her have a peep into the kitchen, where she ought to have been living with the red-cheeked children; they would let her stroll round the farm buildings and have a look at the cows and, as she sat in the Beresford Road dining-room, darning the everlasting socks and stockings, she fancied she could smell the sweet breath of the cows, and the sweetness of wallflowers, old man and pinks, in the little garden where the bear had lurked. There would be no flowers blooming now, unless there was a chance primrose on a sheltered bank, but the cows would be there, and she looked at Ruth, sitting on her feet in the old saddlebag armchair, and reading with absorption, and thought it would be cruel to go without the child. Was this an excuse for procrastination? she asked herself, as there came a loud knock on the front door which made Ruth look up and say: “Postman, Moley. It may be the fortune.”

===Chinese===

“不，不，这家里可是滴酒不沾的。”她的笑声清亮地荡开。“不过科德先生吃圣诞布丁的时候，白兰地也只好就着布丁一并咽了下去！波特酒我不要。我要放一天假，到乡下去过。”

“拿这个当假期，这主意可真够新鲜的，”桑普森先生说。

这说法拿来形容她盘算的那趟出行，确乎太文饰了些。那趟出行她一直没定下日子，因为她知道露丝准会闹着同去。本该等到开学再走，可那还远得很；而她势必得回绝露丝一桩乐事——那乐事之于露丝，恰如当年的一日拉德斯托（Radstowe）之于小汉娜。汉娜自己呢，尽管素来畏缩不前，心里却烧着一股大愿，要再看看自己的乡土：坐在火车里，看城市连同近郊渐渐退去，让位给田野和树林，让位给沟渠纵横的平坦草场，让位给被庄严的垂直式教堂俯临的座座村落——正如当年她看着这一切渐渐退去，让位给拉德斯托的许诺：那座仙城，街衢与塔楼，桥梁、流水与船只。她要把正事尽快办完，然后穿过田野，往老农场去。农场如今归了谁，她不知道；那家人只要同她本乡本土那类心存提防却心地厚道的人有几分相像，就会容她往厨房里偷觑一眼——照本分说，她原该在那厨房里，同红脸蛋的孩子们一起过活；就会容她在农舍场院之间信步走走，看看那些奶牛。坐在贝雷斯福德路（Beresford Road）的饭厅里缝补那永远缝不完的短袜长袜时，她恍然觉得自己闻得见奶牛甜甜的鼻息，闻得见那个有熊潜踪的小园子里桂竹香、老人蒿和石竹的甜香。眼下自然没有花开，除非哪一处向阳背风的土坎上侥幸还留着一朵樱草花；可奶牛总会在那儿。她望望露丝——正蜷在那把老马鞍包扶手椅里，脚缩在身子底下，读书读得入了神——心想撇下这孩子自己去，未免太狠心。这莫不是给拖延寻的口实？她正自问，前门上一记响亮的敲门声传来，震得露丝抬起头来说：“邮差，莫莉（Moley）。说不定是那笔财产来了。”

===Original===

“What fortune?” Ethel asked. She was trying to alter one of her many unsatisfactory dresses, but she was inept, and presently she would ask Miss Mole to gather her scattered pieces into some semblance of a whole.

“If it’s the fortune,” Hannah said, going towards the door, “I’ll give you each﻿—well, it depends, but I’ll give you each something.”

“And she would, you know,” Ruth said, looking gravely at Ethel.

===Chinese===

“什么财产？”埃塞尔问。她正试着改一条裙子——她那许多不合身的裙子中的一条——可她手笨，过不了一会儿，就得请莫尔小姐来，把她那一堆散落的裁片收拢成个大致像样的整衣裳。

“要是那笔财产真来了，”汉娜一边朝门口走，一边说，“我给你们一人——嗯，得看情形，不过我总归给你们一人一点东西。”

“她真会给的，你知道，”露丝说，一本正经地望着埃塞尔。

===Original===

The knock had drawn Robert Corder from his study and he found Miss Mole, in the hall, holding a letter. “The postman?” he asked.

“No. This came by hand, as they say, as though a postman hadn’t got one.”

“For me?”

“No, for me,” she said and she opened and read it while he stood there.

===Chinese===

敲门声把罗伯特·科德从书房里引了出来；他在门厅里遇见莫尔小姐，手里正拿着一封信。“是邮差？”他问。

“不是。这是专人送来的——照他们的说法——倒好像邮差不长手似的。”

“给我的？”

“不是，是给我的，”她说着拆开信来，当着他的面读了。

===Original===

“Not bad news, I hope,” he said.

“Not at all,” she replied, smiling at him as she tucked the letter into the bosom of her dress, and returned to the dining-room.

The smile was still on her lips, though she did not know it until Ruth cried: “And I do believe it really is the fortune!”

===Chinese===

“但愿不是坏消息，”他说。

“一点也不是，”她答道，朝他一笑，把信塞进胸前的衣襟里，回饭厅去了。

那笑意还留在唇边，她自己却没觉察，直到露丝喊了起来：“呀，我敢说，真真是那笔财产来了！”

===Original===

“Good gracious! Do I look as pleased as all that?”

“Not now. Now you’re frowning a little. Isn’t it something nice?”

“That depends on one’s point of view,” Hannah said, and Ruth resumed her reading. There were times when she knew it was of no use to ask Miss Mole any questions.

===Chinese===

“我的老天！我有那么一副欢喜相吗？”

“方才像。这会儿你微微皱着眉呢。莫不是什么好事？”

“那得看是谁的眼光了，”汉娜说。露丝重新埋头看书。她知道，有些时候，问莫尔小姐什么都是白问。

===Original===

Hannah wondered, and was half annoyed at her satisfaction, but a day in the country with Mr. Blenkinsop would be a day of inward and outward laughter: she could not look at him without a bubbling feeling of pleasure, even his handwriting made her smile and, if she gave him the day he asked for, she must postpone her own expedition. This, in itself, was a relief, and to be looked after as she knew Mr. Blenkinsop would look after her, to have her ticket taken for her, to be asked if she were tired, was an alluring prospect to Hannah, who had spent so much of her time in looking after other people. But Mr. Blenkinsop took a good deal for granted: he assumed, and no doubt she had given him cause, that her interest in Mrs. Ridding was considerable, but to take her into the country to inspect the little house he had found﻿—that house, which, no doubt, was to be Mrs. Ridding’s refuge from her husband﻿—was a dependence on her judgment which that lady might resent, and it was a deliberate involving of Mr. Corder’s housekeeper in an affair which would do her reputation no good. This care for her reputation satisfactorily explained her slight feeling of irritation with Mr. Blenkinsop, but it did not influence her desire to oblige him. Such invitations did not often come her way and, irritated or not, she liked Mr. Blenkinsop, and the thought of his companionship, preoccupied though he might be, for a whole day in the country, with bare branches against grey skies and pale fields slipping into brown ones, and the chance of a primrose, was more than enough to make her smile.

Mr. Blenkinsop hoped the following Sunday would not be an impossible day for her. He was afraid it would be awkward but﻿—he was quite playful in the excitement which had made him begin his note without any formality of address﻿—he also hoped she would be able to produce the usual grandmother or aunt whose illness or funeral called her away. Hannah, however, did not need these ladies, for she had a tenant, and Mr. Corder’s prejudice against Sunday pleasures would not be applied to business if she explained that it could be transacted on no other day. Her trouble was not Mr. Corder; it was lack of suitable clothing for this outing. She had the shoes, but she had no well cut tweeds, no gay scarf and jaunty hat. Life was simpler for men. Their festive occasions were not brightened or dimmed by the clothes question; it was dull for them, but easy, and Hannah looked at her battered headgear and sighed. She counted her little savings and saw the coins as so many meals and so many nights of shelter: it would be madness to spend a penny, but why should she not be mad? There was not much difference between having food for a month and having it for a day, and she put her purse in her pocket and decided to go and look at the shops. The January sales had begun, and she might pick up a bargain: she might save a rich old gentleman from being run over and her future would be secured because she had dared to risk it, and she set off, believing the miracle was really going to happen, ready to squander, but possessed of the pleasant certainty that she could restrain herself if she chose. This was the right mood in which to go shopping. The smell of spring in the mild air, the thought of Sunday, and the purse held firmly in the pocket of her coat, were like wine to Miss Mole, who walked with her quick, light tread until she reached the shops, and then she walked slowly, gazing into the windows, but her exhilaration left her before she had gone far. She was born fastidious, and the heaps of clothing, boldly ticketed, did not attract her. She knew it was better to be decently shabby than cheaply gay and she retraced her steps up The Slope, looking back now and then at the beauty she could get for nothing.

===Chinese===

汉娜心里犯着琢磨，对这番称心又有几分恼意；可同布伦金索普先生（Mr. Blenkinsop）下乡过一天，将是一天里里外外的欢笑：她没法看他而不心头直冒欢喜的泡泡，连他的笔迹都能逗她微笑。她若把他讨的那一天给了他，自己的出行就得往后挪——这本身就叫她松了口气。再说，被人照看着——她知道布伦金索普先生必会照看她——车票有人替她拿了去打，乏了有人问她一声，对汉娜这样一位大半辈子都在照看别人的人来说，这前景实在诱人。可布伦金索普先生把许多事认作了当然：他认定——这由头无疑是她自己给的——她对里丁太太（Mrs. Ridding）的事关切极深；可带她下乡去看他寻到的那所小房子——那房子，无疑就是里丁太太躲避丈夫的避难所——这是拿她的判断作倚仗，那位太太对此兴许要恼的；再说，这是存心把科德先生的女管家卷进一桩于她名声无益的事里。这份对名声的顾惜，倒也顺顺当当地解释了她对布伦金索普先生那点轻微的恼意，却丝毫没有动摇她想帮他这个忙的心愿。这样的邀请不常落到她头上；恼也好，不恼也好，她喜欢布伦金索普先生；一想到整整一天有他做伴——尽管他兴许满心是事——在乡下，光秃的枝桠衬着灰蒙蒙的天，浅淡的田亩一寸寸滑向褐色，兴许还能碰上一朵樱草花——这念头足够她笑逐颜开了。

布伦金索普先生希望下星期日对她不至于是个全然不便的日子。他怕那天叫她为难，可是——他兴致好得近乎顽皮：兴奋之下，那张字条连称呼的客套都没有，提笔就写起了正文——他还希望她能照例搬出一位老祖母或姨姑来，不是病重，就是出殡，好叫她脱得了身。汉娜却用不着这些太太们，因为她有一位房客；科德先生对星期日寻乐子的成见，只要她说明这桩事非在这天办不可，就不会施到正事头上。她的难处不在科德先生，在于拿不出一身配得上这趟出门的衣裳。鞋她有；可她没有剪裁入时的花呢衣裳，没有鲜亮的围巾，没有俏皮的小帽。做男人家省心多了。他们的喜庆场合，不会因衣裳问题增一分色，也不会减一分色；这于他们是乏味了些，却省事。汉娜瞧着自己那顶破旧的帽子，叹了口气。她数了数自己那点积蓄，一枚枚铜子在她眼里都化作一顿顿饭、一个个遮风避雨的夜：花掉一个便士都是发疯；可她为什么不许自己疯一回呢？粮食够吃一个月与够吃一天，差得了多少？她把钱包揣进衣袋，决定上街看铺子去。一月大减价已经开张，说不定能捡着个便宜：说不定还能从车轮底下救下一位阔老先生，她的下半辈子就此有了着落——正因为她敢拿它冒险。她就这样出了门，相信奇迹这回真要应验，摆出一副要挥霍的架势，心里却稳稳地快活：只消她愿意，随时收得住手。上街购物，正该是这份心气。温软的空气里透着春天的气息，心头想着星期日，再加上那只紧紧攥在大衣袋里的钱包，于莫尔小姐都如美酒一般；她迈着轻快的碎步一路走去，到了店铺跟前才放慢脚步，一家家张望橱窗；可没逛出多远，那股兴致就散了。她天生挑剔，那成堆的衣裳、扎眼地钉着标价签，引不动她。她明白，体面的寒酸强过廉价的鲜亮，便转身往回走，重上陡坡（The Slope），不时回头，望一眼那些分文不取的美景。

===Original===

As she neared the top of the hill, she spied the figure of Lilla bustling down, and a new light came into her eyes. If she could not have a new hat, she could have some fun with Lilla, and she greeted her cousin so loudly and lovingly that Lilla looked round for a retreat.

“Come in here and have some tea,” she said, indicating the shop where they had met on that October evening when Hannah first saw Mr. Blenkinsop, and Hannah followed her into the most secluded corner.

“I’ve been wanting to see you,” Lilla said.

“You don’t look a bit pleased, dear,” Hannah said sadly.

===Chinese===

快到坡顶时，她瞥见莉拉（Lilla）正急匆匆往下走来，眼里顿时亮起一道新的光。新帽子既指望不上，跟莉拉寻点乐子总是可以的；她招呼表姐的那一声又响又亲热，亲热得莉拉直四下张望，想寻个退身之处。

“进来这儿喝杯茶，”她说，指的是那家铺子——十月里那个傍晚她们在此相遇，汉娜头一回望见布伦金索普先生便是在那儿；汉娜跟着她走进最僻静的角落。

“我一直想见你来着，”莉拉说。

“瞧你这模样，可一点儿也不欢喜，亲爱的，”汉娜忧伤地说。

===Original===

“I’m not pleased, but I wanted to tell you that I wish you’d mind your own business and leave me to mind mine. I don’t need instructions about how to behave, Hannah, and your letter was quite unnecessary.”

“Not instructions, dear﻿—only hints. You told me I was to bark when necessary, so I barked. You put me in that house to take care of Mr. Corder, and I’m trying to do it. I thought you’d be glad. Are you paying for this tea, or am I?”

“Never mind about that. I want to get to the bottom of this affair of Howard’s.”

“But I do mind, Lilla. If it’s me, I’m having a bun. If it’s you, I’ll have buttered toast to start with.”

===Chinese===

“我是不欢喜，可我想告诉你：但愿你管好你自己的事，把我的事留给我自己管。怎样做人处事，汉娜，用不着你来指点，你那封信实属多余。”

“不是指点，亲爱的——不过提个醒。是你说过，必要时我要吠叫的，我就吠了。是你把我安进那所房子去照看科德先生的，我正尽力照看。我还当你该高兴呢。这茶是你付账，还是我付？”

“这你不用管。我要把霍华德这件事查个水落石出。”

“不，我偏要管，莉拉。归我付账，我就来个小圆面包；归你付账，我先来一份黄油吐司。”

===Original===

“Have anything you like,” Lilla said grandly. “I suppose that boy is running away from trouble, but of course his father wouldn’t admit it. He talked a lot of rubbish about temperament and the open air, and I did my best to make things easy for him, but he ought to have trusted me. I’ve a great respect for Robert Corder﻿—”

“Nothing to what he has for you,” Hannah said feelingly. “I really believe the only thing that worried him about Howard was the fear that you would be hurt. He has a noble character, Lilla.”

“H’m,” Lilla said. “I’m not satisfied, but I must say he seemed anxious to return the money I’d spent. Where he’s going to get it from, I don’t know and, if he can pay it back, it seems to me that he can’t have needed it. And that’s not a pleasant thought.”

“Then don’t think it. Just remember that you’re a lucky woman. If Mr. Corder admired me as he admires you﻿—”

===Chinese===

“随你爱点什么，”莉拉摆出慨然施与的气派说。“我看那孩子是在躲祸事，可他父亲当然死不肯认。他扯了一通什么性情啦、野外空气啦的废话，我尽力替他把事情圆了过去，可他本该信得过我。我对罗伯特·科德一向敬重——”

“比起他对您的敬重，那算不得什么，”汉娜动情地说。“说真的，我相信霍华德这件事上，唯一叫他发愁的，就是怕您受了伤害。他品格高尚，莉拉。”

“哼，”莉拉说。“我并不满意，可我得承认，他倒像是急着要把我花掉的钱还我。他打哪儿筹这笔钱，我不知道；再说，他若还得起，照我看来，那就是他当初并不缺钱。这念头可不叫人痛快。”

“那就别想它。只消记着：您是个有福气的女人。倘若科德先生敬慕我如同敬慕您——”

===Original===

“Now don’t begin getting sentimental, Hannah. It’s no good. He thinks you’re a capable woman, but there he stops. I took care to find that out.”

“Oh, did you?”

“Yes, I did. There’s too much nonsense about him in the chapel already, and really, Patsy Withers made me feel quite ashamed at my party. What with her and Mr. Pilgrim﻿—”

“What was she doing?” Hannah asked quickly. “I didn’t notice her.”

“No,” Lilla said drily, “you were too busy monopolising Mr. Blenkinsop, and I may tell you that Mr. Corder saw it. It wasn’t fair, Hannah, with so many girls in the room.”

===Chinese===

“得了，汉娜，可别又发起多愁善感来。没有用的。他当你是个能干女人，到此为止，再没有下文。这是我特意打听明白的。”

“哦，是么？”

“是的，打听明白了。礼拜堂里关于他的昏话已经够多了；说真的，我那次宴会上，佩茜·威瑟斯（Patsy Withers）臊得我够呛。她跟皮尔格林先生两个搅在一起——”

“她干吗了？”汉娜急忙问。“我没留意她呀。”

“是啊，你没留意，”莉拉干巴巴地说，“你忙着独占布伦金索普先生呢。不妨告诉你，科德先生看见了。屋里那么些姑娘，汉娜，这样可不公道。”

===Original===

“I couldn’t help it, dear. I’ve a fatal sort of attraction for him. Why is it do you think? I was the belle of the ball for Mr. Blenkinsop there isn’t a doubt.”

“I invite young men for the sake of the girls, not for you to sharpen your wits on, but if I’d known, as Mr. Corder tells me, that he’d left the chapel, I shouldn’t have invited him at all. And he wouldn’t have been missed, as it happened, and that was your fault. Do you know where he goes?”

“Goes?” said Hannah.

“What place of worship?”

“Oh, here and there; here and there. He wants to take me with him on Sunday.”

“Stuff!” Lilla said, but she said it doubtfully. “Well, Mr. Pilgrim isn’t likely to get him, after the exhibition he made of himself﻿—and that was Ernest’s fault. What did you think of him?” she asked, and her bright eyes sharpened a little. “He told me he’d seen you before, Hannah, and he seemed curious about you.”

===Chinese===

“我有什么法子呢，亲爱的。我对他有股致命的吸引力。你想这是为什么？那晚上，在布伦金索普先生眼里，全场之花是我，这没有半点可疑。”

“我请年轻人来，是为姑娘们着想，可不是给你磨机锋用的。再说，我当初若是知道——科德先生告诉我了——他已经离开礼拜堂，我压根儿就不会请他。况且，事到临头，少他一个也不觉得，这可要怪你。你可知道他如今上哪儿去？”

“上哪儿去？”汉娜说。

“在哪个地方做礼拜？”

“哦，东一处，西一处；东一处，西一处。他还想星期日带了我和他同去呢。”

“胡说！”莉拉说，说得却不大有底气。“哼，经过他自己那一场出乖露丑，皮尔格林先生多半捞不着他了——那场丑，还是欧内斯特（Ernest）闹出来的。你觉得他这个人怎么样？”她问道，一双明亮的眼睛锐了锐。“他告诉我他从前见过你，汉娜，他好像很打听你。”

===Original===

“Naturally,” Hannah said easily. “I’ll never get you to understand that I’m a noticeable character.”

“And he used to live in your part of the country,” Lilla went on, “but as you haven’t lived there for years﻿—” she sighed. “I wish I could feel more comfortable about you.”

“Don’t try,” Hannah said. “I haven’t split on you yet.”

“I’m thinking of your own good, Hannah. I didn’t like the man’s manner at all. If there’s anything I ought to know, you’d better tell me.”

===Chinese===

“那自然，”汉娜神色自若地说。“我是个惹眼的人物——这一点，我永远没法叫你领会。”

“再说，他从前就住在你家乡那一带，”莉拉接着说，“可你毕竟多年不在那边住了——”她叹了口气。“我但愿对你能更放心一点。”

“不必费心，”汉娜说。“你的事，我到今天可还没抖搂出去呢。”

“我是为你自己好，汉娜。那人的做派我一点儿也不喜欢。若有什么我该知道的事，你顶好说了吧。”

===Original===

Hannah shook her head. “I won’t betray him.”

“Him?” Lilla cried.

Hannah smiled in the way Wilfrid loved and Lilla distrusted.

“It hasn’t occurred to you that he might be nervous about his own little secrets, I suppose?”

===Chinese===

汉娜摇了摇头。“我不会出卖他的。”

“他？”莉拉叫了起来。

汉娜笑了——那笑法，威尔弗里德见了欢喜，莉拉见了犯疑。

“你想必从没想过，他自己那点小秘密，说不定也正叫他惴惴不安吧？”

## XXXIII / 第三十三章

===Original===
Wilfrid was to return that evening and, as Hannah walked home slowly, she was glad to think she would see him soon. He, if anyone could, would restore her liking for herself. Her little outing had been a failure. She had not bought a hat; she had told Lilla what amounted to a lie about Mr. Pilgrim, and, until today, Hannah’s excursions into fiction had always stopped at the injury of anyone else. Her offence was the greater because she did not in the least mind injuring Mr. Pilgrim: it was at her own peculiar kind of integrity she had aimed a blow, and it would not be the last one. It was a choice between her integrity and her treasure, and she had not been tender with her treasure for ten years to have it breathed upon by Mr. Pilgrim’s outraged sanctimony and revengeful spite. She was tired and disheartened as she walked through the streets and she was careless, for once, of the life about her. She forgot to remind herself that hers was only a small part in a big drama and that all these men and women, going home from work or coming from the opposite direction in search of pleasure, felt the same overwhelming importance of their lives as she felt of hers: she forgot her little sermon to Robert Corder about infinity, the sun, the moon and the stars: she allowed her own affairs to cast a dun cloud over the whole world, and the tramcars, like noisy magic lantern slides, the shadows of trees on the pavements, the sound of her own footsteps which she had often heard as a sound of advance and adventure, had lost their significance and beauty. In her heart there was the unacknowledged belief that with her lies and evasions she was paying too big a price for what she was concealing; she would have had to pay no more for the memory of something perfect, something she would not have wanted to conceal, and, without actually making that confession, her mind went on to imagine what a real love might have been. But such loves do not come in the way of the Miss Moles of this world, and now she was nearly forty. And thinking thus, she allowed the threatening wave of her loneliness, avoided for so long, to sweep over her, and she stood still in the street, helpless while it engulfed her. It fell back, leaving her battered, but on her feet, and longing for a hand to help her upward before she could be swamped again, but she longed in vain and it was a weary woman who walked up Beresford Road and found no comfort in the ruby glow of Mr. Samson’s window curtains.

She assumed her usual look of competence as soon as she entered the house. Employers do not expect their servants to have visible emotions, and professional pride straightened her back when she went into the dining room. Yet at the sight of Wilfrid, sitting by the fire and listening to what his cousins had to tell him, and leaping to his feet at her appearance, she felt as she had felt when she opened his Christmas parcel, tearfully grateful for a liking which was for herself and not for what she could do for him, and she put her hand on his shoulder and kissed his cheek, without a thought, as naturally as though he were her son.

“Miss Mole!” Ethel exclaimed. And in her voice, the rolling of her eyes, the gleam of her teeth and the checked spring of her body, Hannah recognised the colt she had been trying to tame, now scared, shocked and jealous.

===Chinese===
威尔弗里德当晚就要回来；汉娜慢慢往家走，一想到很快就能见到他，心里便觉欢喜。倘若真有谁能叫她重新喜欢自己，那便是他了。她这趟小小的出门，落了个败兴而归。帽子没有买成；关于皮尔格林先生，她对莉拉讲了一番等于谎话的话；而在今天以前，汉娜历次编造故事的冒险，从来都止步于不伤及任何人的地步。她的罪过所以更重，正因为她对伤害皮尔格林先生毫不在乎：她那一击瞄准的，是她自己那路独特的正直，而且这样的出击不会是最后一次。这是她的正直与她的珍宝之间的一场抉择；十年辛苦呵护下来的珍宝，岂容皮尔格林先生那副受了委屈的道学相和记仇的恶毒来呵上一口气。她在街上走着，又累又沮丧，破天荒头一遭，对身旁的人生漠不关心。她忘了提醒自己：她不过是一出大戏里的小角色，而眼前这些男男女女，下工的往家赶，寻乐的迎面来，人人觉得自己的生活自有压倒一切的分量，正如她觉得她自己的生活一样；她忘了自己对罗伯特·科德讲过的那番小道理——关于无穷，关于太阳、月亮和星辰；她任凭自家的事给整个世界罩上一层灰蒙蒙的阴云，于是电车——那些聒噪的走马灯画片——人行道上的树影，还有她自己的脚步声——她从前常把它听成前进与冒险之声——统统失了意义，也失了美。她心里藏着一种不曾承认的信念：凭她这些谎话与遮掩，她为自己瞒下的东西付出的代价未免太大了；倘若瞒着的是一段圆满的往事、一段她根本不愿瞒的往事，所费也不过如此。这个招认虽然不曾出口，她的心思却顺势往下想开去，揣摩起一段真正的爱情本可能是什么样子。可这样的爱情，从来不会落到这个世界上莫尔小姐们这样的女人身上，何况她如今已年近四十。这样想着，她便听任那股躲闪了太久的孤独的巨浪当头卷来；浪头把她吞没的时候，她直挺挺立在街心，全然无助。浪头退了下去，留给她满身狼藉，好在她还站着；她渴望有一只手扶她一把，好叫她在下一波浪头打来之前重新站直——可她白盼了一场。走上贝雷斯福德路的，是一个疲惫的女人；连桑普森先生家窗帷上那片宝石红的光，也没能给她半点安慰。

一进家门，她便摆出平日那副干练的神情。雇主不指望自家的用人把情绪挂在脸上；走进餐室时，职业的自尊把她的腰板撑得笔直。然而一见威尔弗里德——他正坐在炉火边，听表姐表妹们讲给他听的种种新闻，一见她进来便跳起了身——她心里涌起的，正是拆开他那件圣诞包裹时的感觉：这一份喜爱是给她本人的，不是为了她能替他做什么事，她感激得几乎掉下泪来。她一只手搭上他的肩头，在他脸颊上亲了一下，什么也没想，自然得就像亲自己的儿子。

“莫尔小姐！”埃塞尔失声叫道。从她的声音、她翻起的眼珠、她闪亮的牙齿和她那骤然收住的、正要弹起来的身子里，汉娜认出了那匹她一直想驯服的马驹儿——如今它受了惊、吃了吓，还妒火中烧。

===Original===
“Yes?” Hannah said pleasantly, but she looked at Ruth who was smiling stiffly, and Wilfrid, laughing, seized Hannah’s hand and said dramatically, “we have betrayed ourselves, Mona Lisa, but no gentleman will compromise a lady and refuse to make honourable amends. You must marry me!”

“Wilfrid! She can’t!” Ethel cried. “She’s old enough to be your mother!”

“Oh, not quite,” Hannah begged. She took off her hat and threw it down. “Don’t be so silly, all of you. Are kisses so scarce among you that you take fright when you see one? I’m sorry, Wilfrid. Absence of mind!”

“Don’t spoil it. I’m grateful. Ruth didn’t kiss me, Ethel didn’t﻿—”

===Chinese===
“怎么？”汉娜和颜悦色地说，眼睛却瞟着笑得僵硬的露丝。威尔弗里德大笑着一把抓住汉娜的手，拿腔作调地说：“咱们把心思泄露出来了，蒙娜丽莎；可没有一位绅士会连累了一位小姐，又不肯给她体面的赔补。你必须嫁给我！”

“威尔弗里德！这怎么行！”埃塞尔嚷道，“她的岁数都够当你的妈了！”

“哦，还差那么一点儿，”汉娜告饶道。她摘下帽子，往下一扔。“你们全都别犯傻了。难道你们的吻就这么稀罕，见着一个也要大惊失色？对不住，威尔弗里德。心不在焉罢了！”

“别煞风景。我很感激。露丝没亲我，埃塞尔也没亲我——”

===Original===
“I shouldn’t think of doing such a thing! I didn’t even kiss my own brother.”

“Perhaps that’s why he’s gone to South Africa,” Wilfrid said.

“Oh, you know it isn’t!” Ethel said helplessly, and Ruth gave a hard little laugh.

“Dear me, dear me, dear me!” Hannah said. “What a fuss! The only thing I can suggest is that we should kiss all round and cry quits.”

===Chinese===
“这种事我想都不会想！我连亲哥哥都没亲过。”

“怪不得他跑南非去了，”威尔弗里德说。

“哎呀，明知道不是这么回事！”埃塞尔无可奈何地说，露丝干干地短笑了一声。

“哎呀呀，哎呀呀，哎呀呀！”汉娜说，“瞧把你们闹的！依我看只有一个法子：大家挨个儿亲一遍，从此扯平。”

===Original===
“It isn’t that. You know it isn’t, but I think kisses ought to be sacred, and I don’t see why you should take such a liberty with Wilfrid.”

“Then I’ll tell you why,” Hannah said, her body as tense, her eyes as green and keen, as a watchful cat’s, and a stillness fell on the little company in the presence of this new and formidable Miss Mole. She held them like that for a few seconds and then, satisfied with this small triumph, she dispersed the thoughts that had been crowding into speech and smiled benevolently at all three, remembering that they were children. “Because he’s a dear boy,” she said, “and I like him.”

“Because he’s a man!” Ethel said with stubborn courage, and Hannah looked him up and down teasingly and said: “Yes, he’ll be a man, some day.”

No one answered her smile, and she felt that there was an influence in the room of which she knew nothing, and she believed it was stealthy and malign. She glanced at Wilfrid and saw that he, too, was puzzled, seeking, behind that thoughtless kiss, some explanation of the atmosphere which Ruth and Ethel created between them, Ethel struggling between caution and the blundering candour natural to her; Ruth sitting on her feet, her back pressed against the back of her chair, perched there like a hard young judge, weighing unspoken evidence against some person unnamed.

===Chinese===
“不是这么回事。你明知道不是。可我觉得吻是应当神圣的东西，我不明白你怎么可以对威尔弗里德这样放肆。”

“那我告诉你为什么，”汉娜说。她浑身的劲儿绷得紧紧的，一双绿眼睛又尖又利，活像一只戒备的猫；面对这位陌生的、不好惹的莫尔小姐，这一小群人静了下来。她就这样把他们镇了几秒钟，然后，对这小小的胜利心满意足，把那些蜂拥着要出口的话头遣散了，想起他们到底还是孩子，便慈和地朝三个人一笑。“因为他是个可爱的孩子，”她说，“我喜欢他。”

“因为他是男子汉！”埃塞尔鼓着一股倔勇气说。汉娜上下打量他，眼里带着揶揄：“是啊，他总有一天会成为男子汉的。”

没有人回应她的笑。她觉察到屋里有一股她不知内情的势力在起作用，而且相信它鬼祟而恶毒。她瞟了威尔弗里德一眼，见他也是一头雾水，正想在那个无心的一吻背后，为露丝和埃塞尔两人造出来的这股空气找个解释。埃塞尔在谨慎与她天生的冒失坦率之间挣扎；露丝双脚蜷在身下坐着，背脊紧抵椅背，高踞在那儿，像个铁面的小法官，就一个尚未指名道姓的人，掂量着没有宣之于口的证据。

===Original===
“I think,” Ethel said at last, “I ought to tell Father,” and even now she looked at Hannah for advice, and though she did not ask for it in words, her expression had a familiar appeal and a pathos in its offended bewilderment.

“Don’t be a fool,” Wilfrid said. “The poor man’s got enough worry as it is. A son who runs away﻿—! He doesn’t want to hear about a nephew who has kissed Miss Mole.”

“It was Miss Mole who kissed you.”

“Yes, but I kissed back, and jolly quick! Didn’t you notice?” His speech fell into its provoking drawl. “I call this exceedingly vulgar. Don’t you, Mona Lisa?”

===Chinese===
“我看，”埃塞尔终于开口，“我应当告诉爸爸。”即便到了这个地步，她还是望着汉娜讨主意；话虽没有出口，她那神情里却带着熟悉的恳求，还有一种又受了冒犯又摸不着头脑的哀婉。

“别犯傻了，”威尔弗里德说，“可怜的人眼下的烦心事还不够多么？一个亲生儿子离家跑了——！他哪还有耳朵去听什么外甥亲了莫尔小姐。”

“是莫尔小姐亲的你。”

“是啊，可我还了手，而且快得很！你没瞧见？”他的谈吐又落回那副招人恼的拖腔，“要我说，这实在粗俗透顶。你说是不是，蒙娜丽莎？”

===Original===
“No,” she said, “nothing seems vulgar to me, not really. There’s something wrong with me, I suppose. It’s funny,” she went on, and she leaned forward eagerly, though she looked at nobody, “it’s funny that it’s so easy to be positive about good things and so difficult about what are called bad ones. And d’you know why I think it is? It’s because the good things exist and the bad ones don’t.”

“Oh, but Miss Mole﻿—” Ethel could not resist a discussion in which she had a sort of professional interest, though her antagonist was Miss Mole, “we know there are bad things like﻿—like deceit.”

“Yes, yes, it has a bad name, but get to know the person, the cause and the circumstances and it may deserve a good one.”

“Then you think I oughtn’t to tell Father?”

===Chinese===
“不，”她说，“在我眼里没有什么粗俗，真的没有。我想是我这人有点儿毛病。说来有趣，”她接着说，热切地把身子探向前去，眼睛却谁也不看，“有趣的是，对好东西，我们下断语多么容易；对那些所谓坏东西，却又多么为难。你们可知道依我看这是为什么？因为好东西是存在的，坏东西并不存在。”

“哦，可是莫尔小姐——”埃塞尔抵不住一场她自觉颇有行家兴味的讨论，纵然论敌是莫尔小姐，“我们知道有些东西是坏的，比如——比如欺骗。”

“是，是，欺骗名声不好。可你把那个人、那缘由、那处境都弄清楚了，它说不定配得上一个好名声。”

“那么你觉得我不该告诉爸爸？”

===Original===
“I’m hardly the person to advise you, and this is rather like a nightmare, but I’ll try. Do you mean about the kiss?”

“Not only the kiss,” Ethel muttered, biting her tortured lips.

Ruth’s voice came clearly. “If you do, you’ll have to tell him that Mr. Pilgrim came to tea.”

“Oh, has Mr. Pilgrim been? Did he recite?” Hannah asked, and there was no one in the world who knew her well enough to detect the anxiety under her careless tones.

===Chinese===
“给你出主意，我实在不是合适的人，再说这一切也真像一场噩梦——不过我试试看。你说的是那个吻的事么？”

“不只是那个吻，”埃塞尔嘟哝说，咬着她那备受煎熬的嘴唇。

露丝的声音清清楚楚地传来。“你若要告诉他，就得连皮尔格林先生来喝过茶的事一并说了。”

“哦，皮尔格林先生来过了？他朗诵了没有？”汉娜问。世上没有一个人熟悉她到能听出这满不在乎的腔调底下藏着怎样的惶惶不安。

===Original===
Ethel turned to Ruth. “Why shouldn’t I tell him?”

“Because Father doesn’t like him.”

“Father doesn’t know him.”

“That won’t make any difference,” Ruth said. “Moley, you told me about your cousin Hilda, didn’t you?”

===Chinese===
埃塞尔转向露丝。“我为什么不该告诉他？”

“因为爸爸不喜欢他。”

“爸爸不认识他。”

“那也改变不了什么，”露丝说，“莫莉，你同我讲过你那位表妹希尔达，是不是？”

===Original===
“I didn’t tell you much.”

“Ah, but you’re going to. So there, Ethel! But of course you wouldn’t believe me.”

“I don’t know who to believe,” Ethel said, blinking away her tears.

“What on earth are you all talking about?” Wilfrid asked.

===Chinese===
“我没讲多少。”

“嗬，往后你还要讲给我听呢。怎么样，埃塞尔！这下你没得说了吧。可你横竖是不会信我的。”

“我不知道该信谁，”埃塞尔说，眨着眼睛，把眼泪忍了回去。

“你们说的到底是什么呀？”威尔弗里德问。

===Original===
“And if there’s nothing bad, what’s the use of trying to be good?” Ethel asked.

“It isn’t being good to be a sneak.”

“But how can you help being a sneak if you try to tell the truth?”

“You can hold your tongue.”

“But I’m worried!” Ethel cried. “And what does a little girl like you know about it?”

===Chinese===
“再说，要是根本没有坏东西，那努力学好又有什么用处？”埃塞尔问。

“当打小报告的，可算不得学好。”

“可你想说实话，又怎么能不当打小报告的？”

“你可以闭上嘴不说。”

“可我烦心哪！”埃塞尔嚷道，“像你这么个小丫头，懂得什么？”

===Original===
“More than you do, anyhow!”

“Don’t quarrel, don’t quarrel,” Hannah begged. “This is the queerest conversation I’ve ever heard. Why doesn’t anyone else want to laugh?”

“Because we like listening to you,” Wilfrid said, “and whatever your sins may be, you’ll go to Heaven. They’ll give you all the indulgences you need because they’ll want you in the choir.”

“But I can’t sing a note.”

===Chinese===
“横竖比你懂得多！”

“别吵了，别吵了，”汉娜央告道，“这是我这辈子听过的最古怪的一场谈话。怎么旁人竟没有一个人想笑？”

“因为我们就爱听你说话，”威尔弗里德说，“不管你有什么罪过，你准能上天堂。你要多少赎罪券，他们就会给你多少，因为唱诗班里正缺不得你。”

“可我连一个音都唱不出来。”

===Original===
“Then the choir will go on strike and say they’d rather hear you talking in your lovely voice.”

“Have I got a lovely voice?” Hannah asked.

“Has she?” said Ethel, and Ruth exclaimed: “Oh, Wilfrid, how sickening of you! I thought nobody knew it except me,” and that remark, flattering though it was, seemed to Hannah indicative of the fiercely individual attitude of the Corders towards anything they considered good. They could see trouble in its effect on the family, as they had shown in the case of Howard’s departure, which neither Ethel nor Ruth had mentioned as a personal loss, but they would not share their pleasures.

She had another proof of this in the information Ruth gave her, later, about Mr. Pilgrim’s visit. She had been allowed to have tea with him and Ethel, but, afterwards, Ethel had got rid of her, and Hannah easily imagined her clumsy efforts at tact. “And she needn’t have bothered,” Ruth said. “I didn’t want to stay. I think he’s a horrid man. He smiles too much and his teeth don’t fit. They click, too. And Ethel was so grinny and giggly, till Mr. Pilgrim began talking about you and saying he was sorry to miss you,” and here Ruth paused and looked at Hannah who could not find it in her conscience to ask questions of this child and waited for her next words. “And, of course,” she said reflectively, “you did look specially nice at the party, almost pretty, Moley, when you were talking to Mr. Blenkinsop.”

===Chinese===
“那唱诗班就索性罢唱，说他们宁可听你用好听的嗓音说话。”

“我有个好听的嗓音么？”汉娜问。

“她好听么？”埃塞尔说。露丝叫了起来：“哎呀，威尔弗里德，你真讨人嫌！我一直以为除了我没谁知道呢。”这句话虽是恭维，汉娜却觉得它泄露了科德家的一种脾性：对凡是他们认定的好东西，都抱着寸步不让的独占态度。祸事么，他们是从它对这个家的影响里看出麻烦的——霍华德离家一节便是明证：埃塞尔和露丝谁也不曾把它当作自家的损失提起；可他们的乐子，也断然不肯与人共享。

露丝后来把皮尔格林先生来访的情形讲给她听，又给了她一桩同样的明证。皮尔格林先生和埃塞尔喝茶的时候，倒也容她在座；可过后，埃塞尔便把她打发走了——那份笨手笨脚的圆滑手腕，汉娜一想便知。“其实她犯不着费这份心，”露丝说，“我也不想多待。我看他是个讨厌的人。他笑得太多，一口牙又装得不合适，而且还会咔哒作响。埃塞尔呢，一个劲儿咧着嘴、吃吃地笑，直到皮尔格林先生谈起你来，还说没见着你很是遗憾，”说到这里，露丝停了停，望着汉娜。汉娜良心上容不得自己向这孩子盘问，只得等着她的下文。“还有啊，”她沉吟着说，“你在那次宴会上气色当真特别好，差不多算得上漂亮了，莫莉，你同布伦金索普先生说话的那会儿。”

===Original===
“What next?” Hannah asked disdainfully. “And I don’t see much use in a face that’s only almost pretty.”

“But it’s so exciting. You don’t know what’s going to happen to it.”

“Well,” said Hannah, “I never thought I should die conceited about my looks and, if they’ve pleased Mr. Pilgrim, I shall also die contented.”

“I don’t know about pleasing him. At first I thought it was that and so did Ethel, and she stopped giggling, but afterwards, when he’d gone, I found out that he’d been more interested in your cousin. I was rather in a muddle about it all, and I told Ethel you had a cousin Hilda when she asked me, and then I rather wished I hadn’t, in case there might be something about her you wouldn’t want people to know. I suppose I ought to have stayed with them all the time.”

===Chinese===
“还有什么下文？”汉娜不屑地问。“再说，一张只差一点儿才算得上漂亮的脸，我看也没什么大用。”

“可是这才叫人兴奋呢。你不知道它下一刻会变成什么样。”

“呃，”汉娜说，“我原没想到自己会到死还为容貌自负；这副容貌既然讨得了皮尔格林先生的欢心，我也就可以到死心满意足了。”
